### Attention Consistency for LLMs Explanation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17178v1)
- **Authors**: Tian Lan, Jinyuan Xu, Xue He, Jenq-Neng Hwang, Lei Li
- **Abstract**: Understanding the decision-making processes of large language models (LLMs) is essential for their trustworthy development and deployment. However, current interpretability methods often face challenges such as low resolution and high computational cost. To address these limitations, we propose the \textbf{Multi-Layer Attention Consistency Score (MACS)}, a novel, lightweight, and easily deployable heuristic for estimating the importance of input tokens in decoder-based models. MACS measures contributions of input tokens based on the consistency of maximal attention. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that MACS achieves a favorable trade-off between interpretability quality and computational efficiency, showing faithfulness comparable to complex techniques with a 22\% decrease in VRAM usage and 30\% reduction in latency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Multi-Layer Attention Consistency Score (MACS), a novel, lightweight heuristic for explaining the decisions of large language models (LLMs), specifically decoder-only Transformers. MACS aims to address limitations in existing interpretability methods, such as low resolution and high computational cost. It estimates the importance of input tokens by measuring the consistency of the strongest attention connection from an output query to each input token across all layers, using layer-wise max pooling followed by element-wise multiplication. The authors demonstrate that MACS offers a favorable trade-off between interpretability quality and computational efficiency, achieving comparable faithfulness to more complex techniques with reduced VRAM usage and latency. They perform experiments on question answering tasks and demonstrate the practical utility of MACS.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a novel approach to LLM interpretability by focusing on attention consistency rather than total attention flow, as in Attention Rollout.  While attention-based interpretability isn't entirely new, the MACS heuristic offers a distinct perspective on the problem. The specific combination of layer-wise max pooling and Hadamard product for measuring consistency is arguably novel, providing a different lens through which to analyze attention patterns.  However, it's also important to note that the underlying building blocks (attention mechanisms, max pooling) are well-established; the novelty lies primarily in their specific configuration and application for this particular interpretability goal.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the work hinges on its ability to provide valuable insights into LLM decision-making processes efficiently. The demonstrated reduction in computational cost (VRAM usage and latency) is a crucial advantage, making MACS more practical for real-time diagnostics and analysis of large models. The empirical results on question answering, showing comparable faithfulness to AttentionLRP while outperforming Attention Rollout and AtMan, further support its practical relevance. The demonstration of MACS's applicability to multi-modal models (Visual Question Answering) further broadens its potential impact. However, the significance is somewhat tempered by the fact that interpretability is a subjective and evolving field. It's not always clear whether these interpretability methods provide useful, or even accurate, insights into a model's decision-making.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Efficiency:** The lightweight nature and computational efficiency of MACS are significant advantages over more complex methods.
    *   **Demonstrated Performance:** Empirical results show promising performance on question answering and visual question answering.
    *   **Ease of Implementation:** MACS can be easily deployed without requiring model modifications.
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:** The authors provide a reasonable theoretical justification for MACS, addressing issues such as over-squashing and softmax dispersion.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study provides valuable insights into the design choices of MACS and validates the contribution of its key components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Interpretation:**  The paper admits that the precise interpretation of the "attention consistency" score is an area for further consideration. This is a crucial point, as the value of any interpretability method is directly related to its ability to provide meaningful and actionable insights. The paper briefly discusses this, saying that MACS focuses on information retrieval and comprehension crucial for answer formation, rather than merely reflecting a surface-level similarity. Further details are needed in understanding how this attention consistency may affect MACS outputs and behavior.
    *   **Scope:** The focus is primarily on decoder-only Transformers. The applicability and effectiveness of MACS in other architectures may vary.
    *   **Limited Baselines:** The set of baseline comparisons could be more comprehensive.
    *   **Lack of Quantitative Comparison on VQA:**  The VQA results are primarily qualitative. A quantitative comparison with other VQA interpretability techniques would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Limited Context Length in QA:**  It should also be noted that, although there were 350 question-context-answer triples, this amount is not enough to have a full picture of its effectiveness on long-range context.

*   **Potential Impact:**  MACS has the potential to become a useful tool for LLM developers and practitioners, providing a more efficient way to understand model behavior and potentially identify areas for improvement. Its lightweight nature makes it particularly well-suited for real-time diagnostics and analysis of large models, which could be valuable in production environments. It also sets the stage for future research exploring attention consistency as a general principle for interpretability.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, the paper presents a solid contribution to the field of LLM interpretability. The MACS heuristic is novel, efficient, and demonstrates promising empirical performance.  While the interpretability is up to scrutiny and a more comprehensive analysis of the attention consistency may be helpful, its practical advantages, especially in terms of computational cost, make it a valuable tool. The paper has a solid grounding in both theoretical motivation and empirical validation. The limitations identified need to be addressed, and its efficacy needs to be more clearly demonstrated.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LifeAlign: Lifelong Alignment for Large Language Models with Memory-Augmented Focalized Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17183v1)
- **Authors**: Junsong Li, Jie Zhou, Bihao Zhan, Yutao Yang, Qianjun Pan, Shilian Chen, Tianyu Huai, Xin Li, Qin Chen, Liang He
- **Abstract**: Alignment plays a crucial role in Large Language Models (LLMs) in aligning with human preferences on a specific task/domain. Traditional alignment methods suffer from catastrophic forgetting, where models lose previously acquired knowledge when adapting to new preferences or domains. We introduce LifeAlign, a novel framework for lifelong alignment that enables LLMs to maintain consistent human preference alignment across sequential learning tasks without forgetting previously learned knowledge. Our approach consists of two key innovations. First, we propose a focalized preference optimization strategy that aligns LLMs with new preferences while preventing the erosion of knowledge acquired from previous tasks. Second, we develop a short-to-long memory consolidation mechanism that merges denoised short-term preference representations into stable long-term memory using intrinsic dimensionality reduction, enabling efficient storage and retrieval of alignment patterns across diverse domains. We evaluate LifeAlign across multiple sequential alignment tasks spanning different domains and preference types. Experimental results demonstrate that our method achieves superior performance in maintaining both preference alignment quality and knowledge retention compared to existing lifelong learning approaches. The codes and datasets will be released on GitHub.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LifeAlign: Lifelong Alignment for Large Language Models with Memory-Augmented Focalized Preference Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LifeAlign, a novel framework for lifelong alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs) that aims to address the problem of catastrophic forgetting when models are continuously adapted to new human preferences or domains.  LifeAlign incorporates two key innovations:

1.  **Focalized Preference Optimization (FPO):** This strategy selectively fine-tunes the LLM on new preference data while reducing the impact on previously learned behaviors. It uses an adaptive loss function that attenuates the learning signal for already well-aligned pairs.
2.  **Short-to-Long Memory Consolidation (SLMC):**  This memory mechanism captures and compresses short-term preference representations into a stable, low-dimensional long-term memory. It uses SVD to denoise the short-term memory and resolves conflicts with past alignment signals by projecting the new knowledge onto a historical knowledge subspace.

The authors evaluate LifeAlign on a new, custom-built dataset consisting of six sequential alignment tasks with diverse domains and preference types.  The results show that LifeAlign outperforms existing lifelong learning methods in maintaining both preference alignment quality and knowledge retention.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of focalized optimization and a memory consolidation mechanism tailored for the unique challenges of *preference alignment*. Here's the breakdown:

*   **Focalized Preference Optimization:**  The idea of selectively updating parameters based on confidence or uncertainty is not entirely new in the context of continual learning (e.g., some regularization methods approximate this). However, adapting this principle specifically to preference alignment and using the confidence derived from the DPO loss function to gate the learning signal is a novel and well-motivated adaptation.

*   **Short-to-Long Memory Consolidation:**  The integration of a memory consolidation mechanism, drawing inspiration from cognitive science, is a valuable addition.  While techniques like SVD for dimensionality reduction are well-established, their use in denoising and resolving conflicts in the context of lifelong *preference alignment* is innovative.  The projection onto a historical knowledge subspace is also a well-reasoned approach to minimize destructive interference.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a very important and relevant problem: how to keep LLMs aligned with evolving human values and preferences in a real-world, dynamic setting. The limitations of traditional alignment methods in the face of catastrophic forgetting are a significant obstacle to the long-term deployment of reliable and trustworthy AI systems.

*   **Empirical Validation:**  The comprehensive evaluation on the new six-task dataset is a significant strength. The use of a variety of metrics (BLEU, ROUGE-L, LLM-Judge) and comparison against multiple baselines provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of LifeAlign. It is also important that the authors investigated the influence of hyperparameter selection on performance in addition to task ordering and a study on different foundational models.

*   **Clear Problem Framing and Solution Design:**  The paper clearly defines the problem of lifelong alignment and provides a well-structured and theoretically grounded solution. The explanations of FPO and SLMC are clear and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** The SLMC module, involving SVD computations and memory management, is likely to add to the computational cost of training. While the paper demonstrates performance gains, a thorough analysis of the computational overhead would be beneficial. The authors should discuss how well their system can scale to more sequential tasks.

*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:**  While the paper explores the impact of two key hyperparameters, lifelong learning methods are often sensitive to hyperparameter tuning. A broader discussion about the robustness of LifeAlign to different hyperparameter settings would further strengthen the paper.

* **Limited Evaluation**: While six task were used in the experiments, the diversity within these tasks was relatively low. For example, the performance on each task could have benefitted from the inclusion of human evaluation to augment the results provided by LLM-Judge or an ablation analysis of the self-designed prompt templates.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in several directions:

*   **Lifelong Alignment as a Core Problem:** By clearly articulating the problem of lifelong alignment and providing a practical solution, the paper can help establish this as a core research area within the LLM community.

*   **Memory-Augmented Alignment:**  The use of a memory consolidation mechanism can inspire other researchers to explore similar approaches for preserving knowledge and mitigating catastrophic forgetting in alignment tasks.

*   **Dataset and Benchmark:** The six-task dataset can serve as a valuable benchmark for evaluating and comparing different lifelong alignment methods.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a **Score: 7.5**. Here's the rationale:

*   **+2.5:** The combination of FPO and SLMC constitutes a novel and well-designed solution to the problem of catastrophic forgetting in the context of aligning LLMs to human preferences. The inspiration from cognitive science for the SLMC module is particularly noteworthy.

*   **+2.0:** The extensive experimental evaluation on the custom-built dataset provides strong empirical evidence for the effectiveness of LifeAlign.

*   **+1.5:** The clear problem framing and well-structured solution design make the paper easy to understand and build upon. The potential impact of these solutions in real world situations is undeniable.

*   **-1.5:** The lack of a comprehensive analysis of the computational cost associated with the SLMC module and thorough evaluation/mitigation of the hyperparameter sensitivy slightly diminishes the practical value of the proposed method.

*   **-1.0:** The scope of the evaluation, particularly regarding task diversity, is relatively limited, leaving room for further validation and refinement.

Overall, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM alignment by addressing a critical problem and providing a novel and effective solution. However, a few remaining limitations prevent it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SignalLLM: A General-Purpose LLM Agent Framework for Automated Signal Processing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17197v1)
- **Authors**: Junlong Ke, Qiying Hu, Shenghai Yuan, Yuecong Xu, Jianfei Yang
- **Abstract**: Modern signal processing (SP) pipelines, whether model-based or data-driven, often constrained by complex and fragmented workflow, rely heavily on expert knowledge and manual engineering, and struggle with adaptability and generalization under limited data. In contrast, Large Language Models (LLMs) offer strong reasoning capabilities, broad general-purpose knowledge, in-context learning, and cross-modal transfer abilities, positioning them as powerful tools for automating and generalizing SP workflows. Motivated by these potentials, we introduce SignalLLM, the first general-purpose LLM-based agent framework for general SP tasks. Unlike prior LLM-based SP approaches that are limited to narrow applications or tricky prompting, SignalLLM introduces a principled, modular architecture. It decomposes high-level SP goals into structured subtasks via in-context learning and domain-specific retrieval, followed by hierarchical planning through adaptive retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and refinement; these subtasks are then executed through prompt-based reasoning, cross-modal reasoning, code synthesis, model invocation, or data-driven LLM-assisted modeling. Its generalizable design enables the flexible selection of problem solving strategies across different signal modalities, task types, and data conditions. We demonstrate the versatility and effectiveness of SignalLLM through five representative tasks in communication and sensing, such as radar target detection, human activity recognition, and text compression. Experimental results show superior performance over traditional and existing LLM-based methods, particularly in few-shot and zero-shot settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SignalLLM, a novel agent framework designed to automate signal processing (SP) tasks using Large Language Models (LLMs). SignalLLM aims to overcome limitations of existing LLM-based SP approaches, which are often narrow in application, reliant on specific prompts, and inflexible in adapting to diverse task constraints. The framework features a modular architecture with task decomposition, adaptive retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) for planning, and specialized modules for reasoning, code generation, and LLM-assisted modeling.  It decomposes high-level SP goals into subtasks, utilizes domain-specific retrieval and in-context learning, and allows for flexible selection of problem-solving strategies. The paper evaluates SignalLLM across five SP tasks (radar target detection, human activity recognition, text compression, handcrafted feature optimization, and modulated signal recognition), demonstrating improved performance compared to traditional and other LLM-based methods, especially in few-shot and zero-shot scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in its agentic framework approach to SP tasks. While LLMs have been applied to SP before, SignalLLM introduces a more structured, modular, and adaptable system. The integration of task decomposition, adaptive RAG, and specialized execution modules is a significant improvement over existing ad-hoc LLM-based SP solutions. The explicit consideration of different solution paradigms (code generation, LLM reasoning, LLM-assisted modeling) and the ability to dynamically choose among them is also innovative. The functional taxonomy of existing LLM-for-SP solutions is a valuable contribution for guiding future research.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of SignalLLM is considerable. SP tasks are often complex and require significant expert knowledge. Automating these tasks with a general-purpose framework could significantly reduce development time, improve efficiency, and enable broader access to SP techniques for non-experts. The reported performance gains, particularly in data-constrained scenarios (few-shot, zero-shot), are promising and could unlock new applications of SP in areas where data is scarce. The framework's adaptability to different signal modalities and task types also enhances its broader applicability. However, some significance rests on the wider adoption of the framework in the SP community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined architecture with clear modularity.
    *   Adaptive execution approach catering to various problem-solving strategies.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation across diverse SP tasks.
    *   Demonstrated performance gains compared to baseline methods.
    *   Addresses a clear need for more generalizable and automated SP solutions.
    *   Comprehensive taxonomy of existing LLM-for-SP methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper doesn't delve deeply into the specifics of the prompts used for task decomposition and planning. A more detailed analysis of prompt engineering would strengthen the results.
    *   The selection of baseline methods could be improved. The compared algorithms on each dataset seem to be chosen independently. The authors should ideally select state-of-the-art methods consistently across the whole experiment to highlight the effectiveness of SignalLLM.
    *   The framework's reliance on external LLM APIs (GPT-4) raises concerns about cost, latency, and reproducibility. Exploring alternatives with open-source LLMs would be beneficial.
    *   The paper does not thoroughly discuss the limitations of each module in the SignalLLM architecture. For example, a discussion of when the LLM-assisted optimization module fails and how these failure modes are addressed would add value to the paper.
    *   While the framework shows promise, the experiments could be considered as proof-of-concept studies rather than definitive demonstrations of its superiority in all SP scenarios.

*   **Potential Influence:** SignalLLM has the potential to influence the direction of SP research by promoting the use of agentic frameworks, modular architectures, and adaptive execution strategies. The framework could also serve as a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners seeking to automate and generalize SP tasks. The task taxonomy and analysis of different LLM-based approaches could also guide future research in this area. However, the extent of the framework's impact depends on its wider adoption, further development, and successful application to other SP domains.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper presents a worthwhile contribution that attempts to resolve the problems facing current LLM-based methods for signal processing. Its architecture offers a flexible approach, and the thorough evaluation bolsters the usefulness of the proposed SignalLLM framework. However, the limitations regarding reliance on closed-source LLMs, the prompt design approach, and baseline comparisons require further refinement. Due to these limitations, a higher rating cannot be justified.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Conditional Policy Generator for Dynamic Constraint Satisfaction and Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17205v1)
- **Authors**: Wook Lee, Frans A. Oliehoek
- **Abstract**: Leveraging machine learning methods to solve constraint satisfaction problems has shown promising, but they are mostly limited to a static situation where the problem description is completely known and fixed from the beginning. In this work we present a new approach to constraint satisfaction and optimization in dynamically changing environments, particularly when variables in the problem are statistically independent. We frame it as a reinforcement learning problem and introduce a conditional policy generator by borrowing the idea of class conditional generative adversarial networks (GANs). Assuming that the problem includes both static and dynamic constraints, the former are used in a reward formulation to guide the policy training such that it learns to map to a probabilistic distribution of solutions satisfying static constraints from a noise prior, which is similar to a generator in GANs. On the other hand, dynamic constraints in the problem are encoded to different class labels and fed with the input noise. The policy is then simultaneously updated for maximum likelihood of correctly classifying given the dynamic conditions in a supervised manner. We empirically demonstrate a proof-of-principle experiment with a multi-modal constraint satisfaction problem and compare between unconditional and conditional cases.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper and will provide a summary followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to solving dynamic constraint satisfaction and optimization problems, termed a "Conditional Policy Generator." The method leverages reinforcement learning (RL) and draws inspiration from conditional Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs).  The core idea is to train a policy network that can generate solutions satisfying both static and dynamic constraints. Static constraints are incorporated into the reward function of the RL agent, guiding the policy toward feasible solutions. Dynamic constraints, representing changes in the problem environment, are encoded as class labels and used in a supervised learning component of the training process. This allows the policy to adapt its solution generation based on the specific dynamic conditions. The authors demonstrate the approach on a simple multi-modal benchmark problem, comparing the performance of unconditional and conditional policy generators.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty of the paper lies in combining RL with the conditional GAN framework to address dynamic CSPs. While RL has been applied to CSPs before, and GANs are used in various contexts, the specific integration presented here appears to be a relatively new approach. The idea of encoding dynamic constraints as class labels and using this information to condition a solution-generating policy is a worthwhile contribution.

**Significance:** The significance is moderate, but dependent on future development. Dynamic CSPs are a prevalent class of problem in various real-world applications.  The approach presented here offers a potentially scalable and adaptable alternative to traditional CSP solvers. However, the experimental evaluation is limited to a simple, synthetic benchmark. The true potential of the method can only be assessed through more extensive experimentation on more complex and realistic problems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and well-defined approach:** The paper clearly outlines the proposed method, including the network architecture, training procedure, and loss function.
*   **Sound conceptual basis:** The integration of RL and conditional GAN concepts is well-motivated and conceptually sound.
*   **Addressing a relevant problem:** Dynamic CSPs are a significant challenge in many application domains.
*   **Promising initial results:** The experimental results on the multi-modal benchmark demonstrate the feasibility of the approach.
*   **Scalable architecture:** The feedforward network architecture for each variable could potentially lead to scalability.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited experimental evaluation:** The evaluation is restricted to a simple, synthetic problem (Synt-3D and Synt-2D, 5D, 10D). This makes it difficult to assess the generalizability and scalability of the method.  There's a lack of comparison against state-of-the-art *dynamic* CSP solvers, or even a discussion of appropriate baselines to consider.
*   **Strong independence assumption:**  The method relies on the assumption that input variables are statistically independent. This assumption might not hold in many real-world CSPs, potentially limiting its applicability. Although acknowledged, the paper doesn't offer mitigation strategies for this.
*   **Lack of ablation study:** There's no ablation study to analyze the impact of the different loss components (LPG, LENT, LNLL). It's not clear how much each contributes to the overall performance.
*   **Limited discussion of computational complexity:** The paper does not provide a detailed analysis of the computational complexity of the proposed method.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in the area of machine learning for CSPs, particularly in the context of dynamic environments. It could inspire the development of more sophisticated methods that combine RL and generative models to solve dynamic optimization problems. However, its actual impact will depend on the method's performance on more challenging problems and comparisons against existing techniques. The concept of conditioning a CSP solver using a class label to satisfy dynamically changing constrains is very intriguing.

**Justification of the Score:**

Despite the strengths mentioned above, the limitations in the experimental evaluation and strong independence assumptions temper the enthusiasm for this paper. While the core idea is novel and the initial results are promising, the lack of rigorous testing on more realistic problems and the absence of comparisons with other dynamic CSP solvers make it difficult to assess its true potential. Therefore, it's rated with a moderate score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Virtual Consistency for Audio Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17219v1)
- **Authors**: Matthieu Cervera, Francesco Paissan, Mirco Ravanelli, Cem Subakan
- **Abstract**: Free-form, text-based audio editing remains a persistent challenge, despite progress in inversion-based neural methods. Current approaches rely on slow inversion procedures, limiting their practicality. We present a virtual-consistency based audio editing system that bypasses inversion by adapting the sampling process of diffusion models. Our pipeline is model-agnostic, requiring no fine-tuning or architectural changes, and achieves substantial speed-ups over recent neural editing baselines. Crucially, it achieves this efficiency without compromising quality, as demonstrated by quantitative benchmarks and a user study involving 16 participants.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel audio editing pipeline based on virtual consistency (VCI) for text-guided modifications.  It addresses the computational cost and fidelity limitations of existing inversion-based methods. By adapting the diffusion model's sampling process, the authors formulate a consistency sampling approach that avoids explicit inversion. A hyperparameter is introduced to control the edit strength, balancing fidelity to the original audio and adherence to the text prompt. The method, dubbed ControlVCI, is model-agnostic and doesn't require fine-tuning or architectural changes. The paper presents quantitative and qualitative evaluations, including a user study, demonstrating that ControlVCI achieves state-of-the-art performance with significantly reduced computational cost compared to other methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of applying virtual consistency to audio editing is interesting and leverages the advantages of virtual consistency models for faster sampling.  The key novelty lies in the specific adaptation of VCI for audio and, more importantly, the introduction of a hyperparameter to control the trade-off between the edit strength and the fidelity of the input audio. While virtual consistency isn't new *per se*, its application *without inversion* to the audio editing problem, coupled with the controlled edit strength, constitutes a significant improvement over existing approaches. This distinction is crucial because previous methods often relied on complex inversion processes or lacked granular control over the editing process. The approach's model-agnostic nature is also a valuable contribution.
* **Significance:** The primary significance of the work is its computational efficiency.  Existing audio editing methods, especially those based on inversion, are computationally expensive and time-consuming. By eliminating the inversion step, ControlVCI substantially reduces the latency, making it more practical for real-world applications. The reported results demonstrate competitive or superior performance compared to existing methods, particularly in balancing audio fidelity and text alignment, further increasing its significance.  The user study adds weight to the claim that ControlVCI produces perceptually pleasing and accurate edits. However, the dependence on text prompts could be a limiting factor for users who prefer more intuitive or visual interfaces for editing.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Computational Efficiency:**  A significant strength is the drastic reduction in computational cost compared to inversion-based methods.
    * **Model-Agnostic Design:**  The approach is model-agnostic and doesn't require retraining or fine-tuning, making it easily adaptable to different diffusion models.
    * **Controlled Edit Strength:** The introduction of a hyperparameter to control the edit strength is a notable advantage, enabling users to fine-tune the balance between fidelity and text alignment.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:**  The quantitative and qualitative results, including the user study, support the claim that ControlVCI achieves state-of-the-art performance.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependence on Text Prompts:** The reliance on text prompts for editing might not be ideal for all users.
    * **Limited Scope of Datasets:** While the datasets used are standard, a more diverse set of audio editing tasks and datasets would further strengthen the paper.
    * **No Ablation Studies:** While the method introduces a hyperparameter for control of edits, the authors have not shown through abalation studies whether this choice is better than a more simplistic approach that scales the 'source' or 'target' noise estimate.
* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of neural audio editing by providing a computationally efficient and effective method for text-guided modifications. Its model-agnostic nature could encourage its adoption by researchers and practitioners working with different diffusion models. The controlled edit strength hyperparameter offers a valuable tool for fine-tuning the editing process.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of neural audio editing. The virtual consistency-based approach offers a compelling solution to the computational challenges of existing methods while maintaining or improving editing quality. The introduction of a hyperparameter for controlling edit strength provides a valuable feature for users. While the reliance on text prompts and the somewhat limited datasets constitute minor weaknesses, the strengths of the paper, particularly its efficiency and experimental results, outweigh these limitations.  The method is likely to be adopted and further developed by other researchers, leading to a lasting impact on the field.  It is not a groundbreaking theoretical advance, which prevents a higher score, but a solid, practically valuable contribution to an active research area.

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 8/10

### Causal Representation Learning from Multimodal Clinical Records under Non-Random Modality Missingness
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17228v1)
- **Authors**: Zihan Liang, Ziwen Pan, Ruoxuan Xiong
- **Abstract**: Clinical notes contain rich patient information, such as diagnoses or medications, making them valuable for patient representation learning. Recent advances in large language models have further improved the ability to extract meaningful representations from clinical texts. However, clinical notes are often missing. For example, in our analysis of the MIMIC-IV dataset, 24.5% of patients have no available discharge summaries. In such cases, representations can be learned from other modalities such as structured data, chest X-rays, or radiology reports. Yet the availability of these modalities is influenced by clinical decision-making and varies across patients, resulting in modality missing-not-at-random (MMNAR) patterns. We propose a causal representation learning framework that leverages observed data and informative missingness in multimodal clinical records. It consists of: (1) an MMNAR-aware modality fusion component that integrates structured data, imaging, and text while conditioning on missingness patterns to capture patient health and clinician-driven assignment; (2) a modality reconstruction component with contrastive learning to ensure semantic sufficiency in representation learning; and (3) a multitask outcome prediction model with a rectifier that corrects for residual bias from specific modality observation patterns. Comprehensive evaluations across MIMIC-IV and eICU show consistent gains over the strongest baselines, achieving up to 13.8% AUC improvement for hospital readmission and 13.1% for ICU admission.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CRL-MMNAR, a novel causal representation learning framework designed to handle modality missingness-not-at-random (MMNAR) in multimodal clinical records. CRL-MMNAR leverages both observed data and informative missingness patterns to improve patient representation. The framework consists of: 1) an MMNAR-aware modality fusion component that integrates structured data, imaging, and text while conditioning on missingness patterns; 2) a modality reconstruction component with contrastive learning to ensure semantic sufficiency; and 3) a multitask outcome prediction model with a rectifier to correct for residual bias. The framework achieves consistent improvements in predictive accuracy across MIMIC-IV and eICU datasets for tasks like hospital readmission, ICU admission, and in-hospital mortality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's key novelty lies in explicitly modeling the missingness patterns in multimodal clinical data as informative rather than simply treating them as a nuisance.  The MMNAR-aware fusion component and the rectifier mechanism are designed to leverage these patterns. While other work has addressed missing modalities, few have incorporated a causal perspective or focused on *non-random* missingness to this extent. The combination of modality fusion, reconstruction, and rectifier is a relatively unique architecture.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in applying machine learning to real-world clinical data: missing modalities and the biases introduced by non-random missingness. By demonstrably improving prediction accuracy in crucial clinical tasks, the paper contributes to the development of more reliable and trustworthy clinical prediction models. The improvements over strong baselines on widely used datasets are compelling.  The focus on causal interpretability can potentially translate to better clinical decision support.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Sound methodology:** The framework is well-motivated, with a clear causal justification for modeling missingness.  The individual components (fusion, reconstruction, rectifier) are reasonably well-designed and contribute to the overall performance.
    * **Strong experimental results:**  The comprehensive experiments on two large datasets show consistent improvements over a wide range of baselines. The ablation studies help to isolate the contributions of each component.
    * **Practical relevance:** The paper directly addresses a challenge encountered in clinical practice. The emphasis on real-world data and the reported computational efficiency increases the potential for practical adoption.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Causal Inference:** While the method is termed "causal representation learning", the approach is primarily focused on mitigating bias *due* to MMNAR, not necessarily learning a deep causal model of disease progression.  The rectifier is essentially a post-hoc correction for observed biases, not a truly causal intervention. The causal diagram itself is rather high-level, without diving into the detailed relationships.
    * **Black Box Nature:**  While the authors provide ablation studies, it is difficult to discern the exact clinical insights encoded within the patient representations that lead to improvements.   The interpretability of the learnt representations remains a challenge.
    * **Generalizability limitations:** Although tested on two datasets, questions remain regarding broader generalizability to different clinical settings and data collection processes. The reliance on proxy assumptions for missingness and lack of international validation may hinder its use beyond MIMIC-IV and eICU.
    * **Modest Gains in Some Tasks**: While improvements are substantial for ICU and readmission tasks, the gains are somewhat less significant for in-hospital mortality. The efficacy may be task dependent.
    * **Limited novelty in some modules**: Some of the modules (e.g.  Transformer encoder) are standard, and innovation is primarily in integration and the overall architecture.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable clinical prediction models. Its focus on causal reasoning around missingness can lead to better interpretability of learnt representations and the rectifier serves as an important tool for identifying potential biases.  Future work may further extend the framework towards a deeper causal understanding.

**Overall Score:**

Given the paper's novelty in explicitly handling MMNAR, its substantial experimental results, and its potential practical impact in healthcare, but keeping in mind limitations on causal interpretation, limited gains for some tasks, and lack of international validation, I assign the following score:

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### DT-NeRF: A Diffusion and Transformer-Based Optimization Approach for Neural Radiance Fields in 3D Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17232v1)
- **Authors**: Bo Liu, Runlong Li, Li Zhou, Yan Zhou
- **Abstract**: This paper proposes a Diffusion Model-Optimized Neural Radiance Field (DT-NeRF) method, aimed at enhancing detail recovery and multi-view consistency in 3D scene reconstruction. By combining diffusion models with Transformers, DT-NeRF effectively restores details under sparse viewpoints and maintains high accuracy in complex geometric scenes. Experimental results demonstrate that DT-NeRF significantly outperforms traditional NeRF and other state-of-the-art methods on the Matterport3D and ShapeNet datasets, particularly in metrics such as PSNR, SSIM, Chamfer Distance, and Fidelity. Ablation experiments further confirm the critical role of the diffusion and Transformer modules in the model's performance, with the removal of either module leading to a decline in performance. The design of DT-NeRF showcases the synergistic effect between modules, providing an efficient and accurate solution for 3D scene reconstruction. Future research may focus on further optimizing the model, exploring more advanced generative models and network architectures to enhance its performance in large-scale dynamic scenes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DT-NeRF: A Diffusion and Transformer-Based Optimization Approach for Neural Radiance Fields in 3D Reconstruction" introduces a novel method, DT-NeRF, that combines diffusion models and Transformers to improve the performance of Neural Radiance Fields (NeRF) in 3D scene reconstruction.  The method aims to enhance detail recovery and multi-view consistency, especially in scenarios with sparse viewpoints and complex geometries. The diffusion model generates latent features from noisy images, which are then used as conditional inputs for the NeRF decoder. The Transformer module captures long-range dependencies and optimizes geometric modeling using a self-attention mechanism.  Experimental results on Matterport3D and ShapeNet datasets demonstrate that DT-NeRF outperforms traditional NeRF and other state-of-the-art methods in terms of PSNR, SSIM, Chamfer Distance, and Fidelity. Ablation studies validate the contribution of each module to the overall performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining diffusion models and Transformers with NeRF is relatively novel. While individual components are well-established, their synergistic integration in the context of NeRF for 3D reconstruction represents a meaningful innovation. Diffusion models have seen success in image generation and restoration, and transformers have proven effective in capturing long-range dependencies. Combining them offers a potentially powerful way to address NeRF's limitations. The use of latent features generated by the diffusion model as a condition for the NeRF decoder is an interesting design choice.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed—improving NeRF's performance under sparse viewpoints and in complex scenes—is important. NeRF has limitations when viewpoints are limited or geometry is complex, leading to loss of detail and poor consistency. Improving this helps extend NeRFs applicability. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating consistent and significant performance gains over existing methods on established datasets.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem statement and motivation.
    *   Clear and detailed explanation of the DT-NeRF architecture and its components.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation using standard datasets and metrics.
    *   Ablation studies that validate the contribution of each module.
    *   Significant performance improvements over existing methods.
    *   The writing is clear and well-organized.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper acknowledges the increased computational cost due to the integration of the diffusion model and the Transformer. However, this aspect could have been explored more thoroughly. A more in-depth analysis of the training and inference time trade-offs, compared to other methods, would be beneficial. Specific numbers of GPU time or memory usage compared to other techniques, are not thoroughly presented.
    *   **Generalization:** While the results on Matterport3D and ShapeNet are impressive, it would be useful to see how well DT-NeRF generalizes to other types of scenes and datasets. More testing would be appropriate.
    *   **Scalability:** The discussion of scalability to very large-scale and dynamic environments is brief. Further investigation into the model's ability to handle larger datasets is needed.

*   **Impact and Influence:** DT-NeRF has the potential to influence research in 3D reconstruction by demonstrating the benefits of combining generative models and attention mechanisms within the NeRF framework. Other researchers may build upon this work by exploring alternative generative models, Transformer architectures, or training strategies. The specific combination of diffusion models with transformers and their application on 3D reconstruction are unique, and thus, has the potential to create a significant impact on the field.

*   **Score Justification:**

I am assigning a score of 7. While the paper has many strengths, it has some limitations that should be considered. The novelty of the component integration is solid, and the reported performance gains are significant. The biggest weakness is the computational cost, which, while acknowledged, requires further analysis and optimization. A thorough analysis of model complexity and compute requirements would be beneficial to more clearly assess its potential for practical application and adoption by the broader research community. There are improvements to be made, especially pertaining to compute and further testing.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### MoEs Are Stronger than You Think: Hyper-Parallel Inference Scaling with RoE
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17238v1)
- **Authors**: Soheil Zibakhsh, Mohammad Samragh, Kumari Nishu, Lauren Hannah, Arnav Kundu, Minsik Cho
- **Abstract**: The generation quality of large language models (LLMs) is often improved by utilizing inference-time sequence-level scaling methods (e.g., Chain-of-Thought). We introduce hyper-parallel scaling, a complementary framework that improves prediction quality at the token level. Hyper-parallel scaling computes and aggregates multiple output proposals for a single token from the model. We implement this concept in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, which we refer to as Roster of Experts (RoE). RoE is a training-free inference algorithm that turns a single MoE into a dynamic ensemble of MoEs. RoE injects controlled stochasticity into the expert routing mechanism, enabling it to sample multiple diverse experts for each token and aggregate their outputs for a more accurate final prediction.To overcome the computational cost, we introduce an efficient batching strategy and a specialized KV-caching mechanism that minimizes compute and memory overhead. For example, RoE enables a 7B MoE model to match the performance of a 10.5B MoE model while using 30% less compute for inference. These gains are achieved without any fine-tuning of model parameters.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the "MoEs Are Stronger Than You Think: Hyper-Parallel Inference Scaling With RoE" paper based on the provided text.

**Summary**

The paper introduces "hyper-parallel scaling," a novel inference-time technique designed to improve the quality of large language model (LLM) generation, particularly with Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models. Unlike existing methods focusing on sequential or parallel scaling at the sequence level, hyper-parallel scaling works at the token level by generating and aggregating multiple output proposals for each token.  The authors implement this through Roster of Experts (RoE), a training-free algorithm that turns a single MoE into a dynamic ensemble. RoE injects controlled stochasticity into the expert routing mechanism, allowing the model to sample diverse experts for each token and aggregate their outputs. To address the computational costs, they introduce batching and a specialized KV-caching mechanism. The results show that RoE can enable smaller MoE models to match the performance of larger models with less compute.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of "hyper-parallel scaling" is presented as novel. While ensemble methods and diversifying computations are not entirely new ideas *per se*, applying them at the *token level* during inference in a controlled and efficient manner within MoEs appears to be a specific and potentially valuable contribution. The combination of stochastic expert routing and optimized caching to enable this is also noteworthy.  The claim that RoE is "training-free" is important as it simplifies adoption. However, similar inference-time methods exist; the claim of novelty, while not totally unfounded, needs to be appropriately caveated relative to the existing literature.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to improve the performance of MoE models without requiring retraining or increasing model size dramatically. This has practical implications for deploying LLMs, especially in resource-constrained environments. The reduced computational overhead achieved through batching and specialized caching is a crucial factor for real-world applicability. Further, the argument that RoE offers an alternative, more efficient path than simply scaling the MoE model size is compelling. The applicability to open-ended generation, where methods like Self-Consistency become less useful, is also a key advantage.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined approach.
    *   Practical focus on reducing computational overhead through optimized caching and batching.
    *   Empirical results demonstrating improved performance and efficiency compared to baseline MoE models.
    *   The approach is training-free, making it easy to adopt.
    *   Appealing to open-ended generative tasks, which is a strong point.
    *   The description of RoE as a dynamic ensemble is a good intuition for the reader.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The novelty could be stronger. The idea of perturbing the transformer block or layer at different depths has been investigated before and might diminish the novelty, as this can be viewed as a special case of those ideas. A stronger discussion on these relations in the work should be made.
    *   The hyperparameter tuning process seems potentially complex, requiring a search strategy for layer-specific temperatures. This might limit its usability in some scenarios.  The tuning results are presented (which is good), but a discussion of the sensitivity to the hyperparameter settings is not really expanded upon. Further, while they optimize by validation perplexity in some cases, they acknowledge the problems of doing this, and this is still not expanded upon.
    *   The evaluation focuses on greedy decoding. While this isolates the benefits of RoE, it might not fully reflect its performance with more sophisticated decoding strategies.
    *   A more thorough comparison with other inference-time optimization techniques beyond simple baselines would be beneficial.
    *   The exact implementation details and open-source availability of the code are not specified in the excerpt, which limits reproducibility and broader impact.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design and deployment of MoE models, particularly for applications where computational resources are limited. It could also inspire further research into hyper-parallel scaling and other inference-time optimization techniques. However, its adoption will depend on its ease of implementation and the robustness of the hyperparameter tuning process. It can be added as a complimentary idea to the sequential and parallel scaling methods.
    *   The idea can be adapted and modified for use in other fields of work.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:** The paper presents a reasonably novel and practically relevant inference-time optimization technique for MoE models. The approach is well-defined, and the empirical results demonstrate its effectiveness. The training-free nature and focus on computational efficiency are significant strengths. However, the novelty is somewhat incremental, and the hyperparameter tuning complexity and limited evaluation scope (greedy decoding only) detract from its overall impact. Stronger discussion on the relations to existing literature is required.

The potential to influence the field is moderate, but RoE offers a viable alternative to simply scaling model size and is particularly appealing for open-ended generation tasks. As such, I assign a score of 7, signifying a solid contribution with practical value but with room for further refinement and broader applicability. The innovation has a high degree of utility and is not too complex.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scalable Multi Agent Diffusion Policies for Coverage Control
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17244v1)
- **Authors**: Frederic Vatnsdal, Romina Garcia Camargo, Saurav Agarwal, Alejandro Ribeiro
- **Abstract**: We propose MADP, a novel diffusion-model-based approach for collaboration in decentralized robot swarms. MADP leverages diffusion models to generate samples from complex and high-dimensional action distributions that capture the interdependencies between agents' actions. Each robot conditions policy sampling on a fused representation of its own observations and perceptual embeddings received from peers. To evaluate this approach, we task a team of holonomic robots piloted by MADP to address coverage control-a canonical multi agent navigation problem. The policy is trained via imitation learning from a clairvoyant expert on the coverage control problem, with the diffusion process parameterized by a spatial transformer architecture to enable decentralized inference. We evaluate the system under varying numbers, locations, and variances of importance density functions, capturing the robustness demands of real-world coverage tasks. Experiments demonstrate that our model inherits valuable properties from diffusion models, generalizing across agent densities and environments, and consistently outperforming state-of-the-art baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MADP, a novel diffusion-model-based approach for collaborative control in decentralized robot swarms. MADP utilizes diffusion models to generate action distributions, capturing interdependencies between agent actions.  Each robot leverages a spatial transformer architecture to parameterize the diffusion process, enabling decentralized inference by conditioning policy sampling on fused representations of local observations and perceptual embeddings received from peers. The method is trained via imitation learning from a clairvoyant expert on a coverage control problem. The paper presents experimental results demonstrating that MADP generalizes well across various agent densities and environments, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines and adapting effectively to changes in importance density function (IDF) parameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of diffusion models and spatial transformers for decentralized multi-agent control, specifically applied to coverage control.  The use of a diffusion model for action generation, coupled with a spatial transformer to handle communication and spatial reasoning among robots, is a unique contribution. While diffusion models have been used in robotics before, their application in a truly decentralized, scalable multi-agent setting with the described architecture is novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in multi-robot systems: achieving scalable and adaptable decentralized control.  The coverage control problem serves as a suitable benchmark for evaluating the proposed approach. The results demonstrate promising performance, indicating that MADP can generalize well beyond its training conditions, particularly in adapting to variations in the environment's importance density function (IDF).  The ability to scale to larger swarms while maintaining performance is also significant.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Decentralized and Scalable:**  The decentralized nature of MADP is a key strength, as it avoids the limitations of centralized control methods. The spatial transformer architecture is crucial for enabling this decentralized inference. The empirical results point to the scalability of the method.
    *   **Adaptability and Generalization:**  The experiments clearly demonstrate the adaptability of MADP to different IDF parameters and its ability to generalize to scenarios beyond the training distribution.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper includes a comprehensive evaluation with comparisons to multiple baselines (LPAC, DCVT), including tests on real-world scenarios using traffic light data from various cities.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and provides a clear description of the proposed method and the experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Imitation Learning:** The reliance on imitation learning from a clairvoyant expert might limit the policy's exploration capabilities and potentially its ability to discover optimal solutions that differ significantly from the expert's behavior.
    *   **Ideal Communication Model:** The assumption of ideal wireless communication conditions is a limitation. Real-world deployments often suffer from communication delays, packet loss, and bandwidth constraints.
    *   **Relatively Simple Robot Dynamics:** The use of single integrator dynamics for the robots is a simplification. More complex robot dynamics and sensor models might pose additional challenges.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** A more in-depth theoretical analysis of the convergence and stability of the diffusion-based control approach would be beneficial. The analysis of the relationship between the architecture and the diffusion model is weak.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Computational Cost:** The computational cost of the proposed method is not discussed in detail. This is an important consideration for real-time deployment on resource-constrained robots.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of decentralized control algorithms for multi-robot systems. The combination of diffusion models and spatial transformers could inspire new approaches for handling communication, spatial reasoning, and action generation in complex robotic tasks.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of multi-robot systems by demonstrating the effectiveness of diffusion models and spatial transformers for decentralized coverage control. The comprehensive experimental evaluation supports the claims of scalability, adaptability, and generalization. However, the limitations related to imitation learning, ideal communication, and the relatively simple robot dynamics prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Extending Automatic Machine Translation Evaluation to Book-Length Documents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17249v1)
- **Authors**: Kuang-Da Wang, Shuoyang Ding, Chao-Han Huck Yang, Ping-Chun Hsieh, Wen-Chih Peng, Vitaly Lavrukhin, Boris Ginsburg
- **Abstract**: Despite Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrating superior translation performance and long-context capabilities, evaluation methodologies remain constrained to sentence-level assessment due to dataset limitations, token number restrictions in metrics, and rigid sentence boundary requirements. We introduce SEGALE, an evaluation scheme that extends existing automatic metrics to long-document translation by treating documents as continuous text and applying sentence segmentation and alignment methods. Our approach enables previously unattainable document-level evaluation, handling translations of arbitrary length generated with document-level prompts while accounting for under-/over-translations and varied sentence boundaries. Experiments show our scheme significantly outperforms existing long-form document evaluation schemes, while being comparable to evaluations performed with groundtruth sentence alignments. Additionally, we apply our scheme to book-length texts and newly demonstrate that many open-weight LLMs fail to effectively translate documents at their reported maximum context lengths.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SEGALE, a novel scheme for evaluating machine translation (MT) quality in long documents generated by Large Language Models (LLMs).  It addresses the limitations of existing evaluation methods that are primarily sentence-level, face token length restrictions, and require strict adherence to sentence boundaries. SEGALE segments the document, aligns source and translated sentences, and evaluates the aligned pairs using existing metrics.  It specifically handles under- and over-translation errors by identifying null alignments and assigning penalties. The authors demonstrate that SEGALE provides comparable performance to sentence-level metrics when those issues don't arise, but importantly, offers a robust method for dealing with over/under-translation. They also test the ability of several open-weight LLMs to effectively translate book-length texts using SEGALE, finding that performance degrades as the input length increases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by addressing a crucial gap in evaluating LLM-based MT: the ability to assess document-level translation quality. While sentence segmentation and alignment techniques exist, the specific combination of these with a mechanism to penalize over/under-translation, and the application to evaluating the effective context windows of LLMs, is novel. The idea of using NA ratio as a measure of translation quality is a valuable component of their scheme. The novelty comes from the system-level integration and targeted application.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in providing a practical and adaptable method for evaluating long-form MT. As LLMs increasingly handle larger contexts, evaluation methods must evolve accordingly. SEGALE enables the use of existing evaluation metrics in a document-level setting, which is crucial for assessing the overall coherence, consistency, and completeness of translations. The finding regarding the limited effective context length of many LLMs is significant as it highlights a discrepancy between claimed capabilities and actual performance. The paper encourages adoption of this approach for a more accurate assessment of MT in LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a critical gap:** Tackles the problem of evaluating long-form MT by LLMs.
    *   **Modular design:** Allows for the use of existing evaluation metrics, making it easily adaptable.
    *   **Handles anomalies:** Addresses over/under-translation errors through null alignment penalties.
    *   **Practical application:** Demonstrates the utility of SEGALE by evaluating the performance of LLMs on book-length texts.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The paper provides sufficient experimental data including ablation studies.
    *   **Adaptive Penalty Search:** This novel search method is a crucial enhancement specifically for Vecalign.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Sentence Segmentation:** While the paper compares different sentence segmenters, the overall performance of SEGALE is still dependent on the quality of the segmentation. Errors in segmentation can propagate through the alignment and evaluation stages.
    *   **Reliance on Vecalign:** Although justified, Vecalign is the only aligner used, limiting a more robust analysis with other alignment tools.

    *   **Fixed Penalties:**  The penalty for null alignments is fixed, which may not be optimal in all cases. A more nuanced approach based on the severity of the over/under-translation could improve the evaluation.

    *   **Human Judgement Setup**: The human judgement set-up needs improvements. The judgment doesn't explicitly instruct the annotators to consider discourse-level information.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and accessible method for evaluating long-form MT. The evaluation scheme is likely to be adopted by researchers and practitioners working on LLM-based MT, and the findings regarding context length limitations can guide future research in LLM architecture and training.

*   **Justification for the Score:** The paper contributes a novel and practical method for evaluating document-level MT by LLMs. While the method is modular and adaptable, the dependence on external segmentation and alignment tools, along with the use of fixed penalties for translation errors, limit its potential. The paper is a solid contribution that addresses an important practical problem, the identified limitations leave room for improvement.
    Overall, it is a good paper and offers significant contributions.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Graph Signal Generative Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17250v1)
- **Authors**: Yigit Berkay Uslu, Samar Hadou, Sergio Rozada, Shirin Saeedi Bidokhti, Alejandro Ribeiro
- **Abstract**: We introduce U-shaped encoder-decoder graph neural networks (U-GNNs) for stochastic graph signal generation using denoising diffusion processes. The architecture learns node features at different resolutions with skip connections between the encoder and decoder paths, analogous to the convolutional U-Net for image generation. The U-GNN is prominent for a pooling operation that leverages zero-padding and avoids arbitrary graph coarsening, with graph convolutions layered on top to capture local dependencies. This technique permits learning feature embeddings for sampled nodes at deeper levels of the architecture that remain convolutional with respect to the original graph. Applied to stock price prediction -- where deterministic forecasts struggle to capture uncertainties and tail events that are paramount -- we demonstrate the effectiveness of the diffusion model in probabilistic forecasting of stock prices.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a U-shaped Graph Neural Network (U-GNN) architecture within a denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) framework for generating graph signals. The U-GNN combines encoder pooling, decoder upsampling, and skip connections (similar to U-Nets in image generation) while adapting graph convolutions through zero-padding and nested sampling matrices.  The proposed model is applied to stock price prediction, aiming to capture uncertainties often missed by deterministic forecasts. The paper demonstrates the potential of diffusion models for stochastic graph signal generation and U-GNNs for capturing multi-resolution dependencies in graph-structured data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Contribution C1 (Generative Diffusion for Graph Signals):** While the paper claims to unify existing application-driven approaches for graph signal diffusion, DDPMs have already been applied to various graph-related tasks (generation, signal processing, etc.) as outlined in the references themselves.  The contribution here lies more in *formalizing* the problem within a U-Net-inspired framework rather than introducing an entirely novel concept. Therefore, the claim of unification is overstated.
*   **Contribution C2 (U-GNN Architecture):** The U-GNN architecture *is* a significant contribution. The core idea of adapting U-Nets to graph data is not entirely new; however, the zero-padding technique for node selection and the nested sampling matrices to maintain a consistent graph structure are novel and well-engineered. This addresses a critical challenge in applying U-Net-like architectures to graph domains where node order and connectivity are important.
*   **Contribution C3 (Stock Price Forecasting Application):** The application to stock price forecasting, while important, is primarily used to validate the model. The novelty is limited to demonstrating that the generative model can capture uncertainty in stock prices, which is not a groundbreaking application of generative models in finance.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing Uncertainty:** The focus on capturing uncertainty in graph signals is valuable. Traditional deterministic methods often struggle with modeling complex, real-world phenomena, especially those with tail events.
*   **Generalizable Architecture:** The U-GNN architecture has the potential to be generalized across various graph signal processing tasks beyond the specific application of stock price forecasting. The zero-padding and nested sampling matrix approach is a valuable technique for adapting convolutional architectures to graph domains.
*   **Impact on Graph Signal Processing:** The paper's primary influence will be in the graph signal processing and graph representation learning communities. It offers a structured way to combine GNNs with diffusion models and addresses practical challenges of node selection and signal downsampling.

**Strengths:**

*   The paper provides a well-defined problem statement and clear methodological explanation.
*   The U-GNN architecture is technically sound and addresses the node support issue during downsampling.
*   The experimental results, while not groundbreaking, demonstrate the utility of the proposed model.
*   The paper is well-written and provides a comprehensive set of references.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The claim of unification of existing approaches is exaggerated.
*   The novelty of the application to stock price forecasting is limited. The paper could have explored more sophisticated or novel financial applications to showcase the model's capabilities.
*   The performance gains over the GRW baseline are not always substantial. The paper would be strengthened by comparisons to more sophisticated baselines that capture temporal dependencies.
*   The paper would benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the impact of the nested sampling parameter 'y' and a more detailed explanation for hyperparameter choices.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution by adapting U-Net-like architectures to graph domains for generating graph signals within a diffusion framework. The key strength is the U-GNN architecture, particularly the approach for handling node selection, zero-padding, and nested sampling matrices to maintain graph structure. However, the claim of unification of existing approaches is overstated, and the application to stock price forecasting is not particularly novel. The results demonstrate the model's usefulness, but there is room for improvement with comparisons to more challenging baselines. Overall, the U-GNN architecture contributes a significant advancement.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Probabilistic Token Alignment for Large Language Model Fusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17276v1)
- **Authors**: Runjia Zeng, James Chenhao Liang, Cheng Han, Zhiwen Cao, Jiahao Liu, Xiaojun Quan, Yingjie Victor Chen, Lifu Huang, Tong Geng, Qifan Wang, Dongfang Liu
- **Abstract**: Training large language models (LLMs) from scratch can yield models with unique functionalities and strengths, but it is costly and often leads to redundant capabilities. A more cost-effective alternative is to fuse existing pre-trained LLMs with different architectures into a more powerful model. However, a key challenge in existing model fusion is their dependence on manually predefined vocabulary alignment, which may not generalize well across diverse contexts, leading to performance degradation in several evaluation. To solve this, we draw inspiration from distribution learning and propose the probabilistic token alignment method as a general and soft mapping for alignment, named as PTA-LLM. Our approach innovatively reformulates token alignment into a classic mathematical problem: optimal transport, seamlessly leveraging distribution-aware learning to facilitate more coherent model fusion. Apart from its inherent generality, PTA-LLM exhibits interpretability from a distributional perspective, offering insights into the essence of the token alignment. Empirical results demonstrate that probabilistic token alignment enhances the target model's performance across multiple capabilities. Our code is avaliable at https://runjia.tech/neurips_pta-llm/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Probabilistic Token Alignment for Large Language Model Fusion (PTA-LLM), a novel approach for fusing pre-trained large language models (LLMs).  It addresses the limitations of existing knowledge fusion methods, which rely on manually defined vocabulary alignments that can lead to performance degradation across diverse contexts. PTA-LLM reformulates token alignment as an optimal transport problem, leveraging distribution-aware learning to facilitate a more coherent and adaptable model fusion process. The method exhibits interpretability, demonstrating enhanced performance across multiple capabilities in experiments.  The key idea is to leverage the global generative distributions of each model's logits during token alignment, externalizing collective knowledge and facilitating more precise mapping via optimal transport.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to token alignment in the context of LLM fusion.  The reformulation of token alignment as an optimal transport problem is a significant departure from existing hard-coded alignment techniques. While the optimal transport framework is not new in machine learning, its application to token alignment in this specific manner is novel.  The idea of leveraging the global generative distributions of logits for alignment adds another layer of novelty. However, optimal transport is not cutting edge, so while its application here is inventive, it isn't groundbreaking.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a key bottleneck in existing model fusion techniques: the limitations of manual or hard-coded token alignment. By providing a more adaptive and distribution-aware approach, the paper opens the door to more robust and efficient LLM fusion. The experimental results, while not earth-shattering, demonstrate consistent improvements across various benchmarks, suggesting a real-world potential. The interpretability aspect, which allows insights into token alignment, is also important. The improvement, however, is incremental compared to prior fusion methods and doesn't introduce significantly new capabilities or change the scope of LLMs.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem definition and motivation.
    * Novel application of optimal transport to token alignment.
    * Improved performance compared to existing methods (though incremental).
    * Enhanced interpretability of the token alignment process.
    * Well-defined architecture details of PTA-LLM.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The improvements are incremental rather than transformative.
    * The reliance on the Sinkhorn algorithm is a potential limitation regarding computational complexity, although this is acknowledged in the paper. The runtime complexity in Section 11, however, appears to be incorrect. Sinkhorn-Knopp converges in O(n^2 log(n)) *without* accounting for the dependence on error tolerance. The convergence with error tolerance is more complex (and generally not linear with 1/e), especially for ill-conditioned cost matrices.
    * The paper only uses other models that all share similar pretraining data, it would be useful to show the benefits with other base models as well.
    * There aren't significantly new capabilities or scope.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper can potentially influence future research on LLM fusion by promoting a more principled approach to token alignment. It could inspire further investigations into using distribution-aware methods and the optimal transport framework for various NLP tasks. The interpretability insights might also lead to a better understanding of LLM behavior. However, whether or not the proposed improvements actually make it into production models is not clear.

**Rigorous Rationale for the score:**
The paper presents a worthwhile contribution to LLM fusion by addressing a relevant issue with a technically sound and innovative approach. However, the incremental nature of the performance improvements and the limitations regarding computational complexity and the dependence on Sinkhorn, prevent it from being considered a truly groundbreaking work.
