- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automated Facility Enumeration for Building Compliance Checking using Door Detection and Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17283v1)
- **Authors**: Licheng Zhan, Bach Le, Naveed Akhtar, Tuan Ngo
- **Abstract**: Building compliance checking (BCC) is a critical process for ensuring that constructed facilities meet regulatory standards. A core component of BCC is the accurate enumeration of facility types and their spatial distribution. Despite its importance, this problem has been largely overlooked in the literature, posing a significant challenge for BCC and leaving a critical gap in existing workflows. Performing this task manually is time-consuming and labor-intensive. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) offer new opportunities to enhance automation by combining visual recognition with reasoning capabilities. In this paper, we introduce a new task for BCC: automated facility enumeration, which involves validating the quantity of each facility type against statutory requirements. To address it, we propose a novel method that integrates door detection with LLM-based reasoning. We are the first to apply LLMs to this task and further enhance their performance through a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) pipeline. Our approach generalizes well across diverse datasets and facility types. Experiments on both real-world and synthetic floor plan data demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of our method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces a novel task for Building Compliance Checking (BCC): automated facility enumeration. It addresses the problem of accurately identifying and counting facilities (like toilets, kitchens, exits) in architectural floor plans to ensure compliance with building codes.  The authors propose a method that combines door detection using Co-DETR with a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning pipeline powered by large language models (LLMs). The CoT pipeline involves predicting room connections, consolidating multiple door detections to a single room, and correcting for omissions by identifying facilities that might be present without a clearly defined door. The method is evaluated on a diverse set of real-world and synthetic floor plan datasets. The experiments demonstrate that the proposed CoT-LLM approach outperforms a baseline LLM in accuracy and generalizability across different facility types and datasets.

**Critical Evaluation**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of several elements:
    * **Task Definition:** Defining "automated facility enumeration" as a specific BCC task.  While facility identification isn't entirely new, the focus on *enumeration* and the importance of accuracy for compliance are well-articulated.
    * **LLM Application:**  Applying LLMs with CoT reasoning to architectural floor plan analysis, specifically for compliance checking, is a fresh perspective. Existing LLM-based approaches in AEC often deal with rule interpretation or BIM data integration.
    * **Hybrid Approach:** Integrating object detection (door detection) as an anchor point for LLM reasoning is an innovative way to tackle the visual ambiguity inherent in floor plans.
* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from:
    * **Practical Impact:** BCC is a critical but often manual and error-prone process. Automating facility enumeration can save time, reduce errors, and ensure better regulatory compliance.
    * **Generalizability:** The experiments across diverse datasets suggest that the proposed method has the potential to generalize to various building types and architectural styles.
    * **Potential for Scalability:**  By leveraging LLMs, the approach aims to scale compliance checking beyond what traditional, manually-intensive methods can achieve.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly defines the problem of automated facility enumeration and its importance within BCC.
    * **Well-Defined Methodology:** The steps in the proposed method (door detection, CoT reasoning, consolidation, and omission correction) are clearly explained.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments involve a diverse set of datasets and facility types, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of the approach.
    * **CoT Reasoning Justification:** The paper emphasizes the importance of CoT reasoning for improving accuracy and reasoning capabilities of the LLM.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on LLM Availability:**  The method's performance hinges on the capabilities of GPT-5 (or similar advanced LLMs).  Future availability and cost of such models could impact practical deployment.
    * **Door Detection Limitations:** The method relies on accurate door detection.  While Co-DETR is a robust detector, complex floor plan layouts or unusual door designs could still pose challenges.
    * **Prompt Engineering Dependency:** LLM performance is highly sensitive to prompt design. The paper could benefit from providing more details on the prompt engineering process and conducting an ablation study to assess the impact of different prompts.

* **Potential Influence:**
    * The paper could inspire further research on LLM-based approaches for BCC and other AEC tasks.
    * It could lead to the development of practical tools for automating compliance checking in the architecture, engineering, and construction industries.
    * The task definition and datasets used in the paper could serve as benchmarks for future research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering both strengths and weaknesses, the paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of building compliance checking. The hybrid approach of combining object detection with LLM-based reasoning, coupled with the clearly defined task of automated facility enumeration, demonstrates a promising direction for future research and development. While acknowledging the reliance on advanced LLMs and potential limitations in specific scenarios, the thorough evaluation and potential for practical impact justify a relatively high score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automated Knowledge Graph Construction using Large Language Models and Sentence Complexity Modelling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17289v1)
- **Authors**: Sydney Anuyah, Mehedi Mahmud Kaushik, Krishna Dwarampudi, Rakesh Shiradkar, Arjan Durresi, Sunandan Chakraborty
- **Abstract**: We introduce CoDe-KG, an open-source, end-to-end pipeline for extracting sentence-level knowledge graphs by combining robust coreference resolution with syntactic sentence decomposition. Using our model, we contribute a dataset of over 150,000 knowledge triples, which is open source. We also contribute a training corpus of 7248 rows for sentence complexity, 190 rows of gold human annotations for co-reference resolution using open source lung-cancer abstracts from PubMed, 900 rows of gold human annotations for sentence conversion policies, and 398 triples of gold human annotations. We systematically select optimal prompt-model pairs across five complexity categories, showing that hybrid chain-of-thought and few-shot prompting yields up to 99.8% exact-match accuracy on sentence simplification. On relation extraction (RE), our pipeline achieves 65.8% macro-F1 on REBEL, an 8-point gain over the prior state of the art, and 75.7% micro-F1 on WebNLG2, while matching or exceeding performance on Wiki-NRE and CaRB. Ablation studies demonstrate that integrating coreference and decomposition increases recall on rare relations by over 20%. Code and dataset are available at https://github.com/KaushikMahmud/CoDe-KG_EMNLP_2025
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CoDe-KG, an open-source pipeline for automating knowledge graph (KG) construction from sentence-level semantics.  It combines coreference resolution with syntactic sentence decomposition using large language models (LLMs) and sentence complexity modeling to extract knowledge triples from unstructured text.  The authors contribute a large dataset of knowledge triples, a sentence complexity training corpus, gold standard annotations for coreference resolution and sentence conversion policies. The paper explores different prompting strategies (Chain-of-Thought, Few-Shot In-Context Learning, Zero-Shot General Instruction) to optimize the pipeline and compares performance against various LLMs and state-of-the-art (SOTA) relation extraction models across several benchmark datasets. Ablation studies are conducted to evaluate the impact of coreference resolution and decomposition components. They demonstrate gains in relation extraction, especially for rare relations, by incorporating coreference resolution and decomposition.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Open-Source and Reproducible:** The key strength is the release of an open-source pipeline and associated datasets, promoting reproducibility and further research in the area. This is critical for a field often dominated by proprietary models. The availability of the code will undoubtedly facilitate adoption and refinement by other researchers.
    * **Multi-faceted Approach:** Combining coreference resolution, sentence decomposition, and LLMs is a well-motivated approach.  Syntactic simplification to make the sentences more parseable for LLMs is a good idea in theory. Addressing sentence complexity *is* a real issue in extracting from academic and scientific texts. Sentence segmentation improves the RE task.
    * **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper includes comparisons to several SOTA models on multiple benchmark datasets (REBEL, WebNLG2, Wiki-NRE, CaRB), providing a relatively comprehensive performance evaluation.  Ablation studies provide insights into the individual contributions of pipeline components. Extensive experiment details for each stage in the knowledge graph creation process.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Novelty in Components:** The individual components (coreference resolution, LLM prompting, relation extraction) are not entirely novel.  The paper’s primary novelty lies in *integrating* them in a specific pipeline. Claims of novelty are overstated in this paper. The integration of multiple frameworks rather than just one task is not really considered novel.
    * **Overstatement of Performance:** While the results show improvements in some cases, the paper often describes these improvements as substantial when they are marginal.  For example, the REBEL benchmark's improvement is claimed to be a "significant" gain when it is only an 8 point difference in Macro-F1, but it still falls short of other SOTA techniques. Similarly, matching or exceeding SOTA performance on some datasets is presented positively, despite the field rapidly advancing and potentially rendering these benchmarks less relevant. The performance on WebNLG2 and Wiki-NRE underperforms SOTA by wide margins.
    * **Unclear Impact of Sentence Complexity Modeling:**  While the *idea* of sentence complexity modeling is good, the practical impact appears to be smaller than the authors suggest. There are little details as to the analysis of *why* the simple sentence helps better at the RE task.
    * **Limited Discussion on Error Analysis:** The error analysis section is brief. A deeper examination of the types of errors (missing, spurious, relation-mismatch) and their root causes would add more value. More details on prompt engineering specifics could have been offered.
    * **Unconvincing "Industry-Standard" Comparison:** The ChatGPT baselines may not be adequate for demonstrating a compelling performance gap. The reliance on user choice for summarization in ChatGPT devalues its potential as a powerful open-source alternative. More robust baselines should have been included. The authors needed to clarify how ChatGPT maintained "high precision and recall, but extracted some relationships beyond human reference.". The analysis of this aspect needed to be clarified.
    * **Weak Co-reference Results:** The coreference resolution results aren't great to begin with. If the RE is heavily based on accurate coref, and the coref struggles with biomedical text, there is an issue with the overall pipeline robustness.

* **Significance:**

The paper has the potential to be significant in the field of automated knowledge graph construction if the framework proves to be reliable across domains, especially given its open source. The most significant value lies in the methodology and code release, enabling others to build upon and extend the work. Given the limitations regarding novelty and results, however, it falls short of being a truly disruptive contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **6** to this paper. While the paper presents a well-engineered, open-source KG construction pipeline with careful evaluation, its novelty is limited by leveraging existing techniques with only incremental improvements in RE compared to SOTA. The open-source release and thorough experiments are valuable, but the core components are not groundbreaking and some claims on improvements are overstated. There is an attempt to decompose complex medical sentences to simpler sentences for the LLM to better parse the sentences which is a very important aspect in extracting information from biomedical literature. This warrants the higher-than-average rating. There are a lot of moving parts in this KG creation pipeline which can be further researched by future researchers.

### Multi-View Attention Multiple-Instance Learning Enhanced by LLM Reasoning for Cognitive Distortion Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17292v1)
- **Authors**: Jun Seo Kim, Hyemi Kim, Woo Joo Oh, Hongjin Cho, Hochul Lee, Hye Hyeon Kim
- **Abstract**: Cognitive distortions have been closely linked to mental health disorders, yet their automatic detection remained challenging due to contextual ambiguity, co-occurrence, and semantic overlap. We proposed a novel framework that combines Large Language Models (LLMs) with Multiple-Instance Learning (MIL) architecture to enhance interpretability and expression-level reasoning. Each utterance was decomposed into Emotion, Logic, and Behavior (ELB) components, which were processed by LLMs to infer multiple distortion instances, each with a predicted type, expression, and model-assigned salience score. These instances were integrated via a Multi-View Gated Attention mechanism for final classification. Experiments on Korean (KoACD) and English (Therapist QA) datasets demonstrate that incorporating ELB and LLM-inferred salience scores improves classification performance, especially for distortions with high interpretive ambiguity. Our results suggested a psychologically grounded and generalizable approach for fine-grained reasoning in mental health NLP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, with a score assigned at the end.

**Summary**

The paper introduces a novel framework for detecting cognitive distortions in text, which are systematic errors in thinking linked to mental health disorders. The authors argue that existing methods treat utterances as single, unstructured inputs, overlooking the underlying psychological structure. To address this, they propose a framework that decomposes utterances into Emotion, Logic, and Behavior (ELB) components. These components, along with the original utterance, are fed into Large Language Models (LLMs) to infer multiple cognitive distortion instances. Each instance includes a predicted type, a text segment, and a salience score. The framework then uses a Multi-View Gated Attention mechanism within a Multiple-Instance Learning (MIL) architecture to integrate these instances for final classification. The experiments on Korean (KoACD) and English (Therapist QA) datasets demonstrate improved classification performance, especially for distortions with high interpretive ambiguity. The results suggest a psychologically grounded and generalizable approach for fine-grained reasoning in mental health NLP.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty:* The paper has several aspects of novelty:

*   **ELB Decomposition:** The decomposition of utterances into Emotion, Logic, and Behavior components is a psychologically informed way to structure the input for LLMs, enabling more targeted reasoning.
*   **MIL Framework:**  Using a MIL architecture to treat LLM-inferred distortion instances as individual entities within a bag is a novel approach that allows the model to capture multiple distortions and weigh their importance using LLM-assigned salience scores.
*   **Multi-View Gated Attention:** The use of a multi-view attention mechanism addresses the limitation of single-view attention and captures relevant instances, thereby creating a combined framework for cognitive distortion detection.
*   **Integration of LLM Salience Scores:** Incorporating the salience scores assigned by the LLMs into the attention mechanism allows the model to prioritize potentially relevant instances during its decision-making process.

*Significance:*

*   **Improved Performance:** The empirical results on two datasets (KoACD and Therapist QA) show improved classification performance, especially for distortions with high interpretive ambiguity. This demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed framework in capturing complex relationships between utterances and psychological constructs.
*   **Psychological Grounding:** The ELB decomposition provides a psychologically grounded structure to the model, making it more interpretable and aligned with cognitive behavioral therapy principles. This is significant for potential clinical applications.
*   **Fine-Grained Reasoning:** The framework enables fine-grained reasoning by considering multiple distortion instances within a single utterance, addressing a limitation of previous methods that treat utterances as monolithic inputs.
*   **Generalizability:** The model works in both Korean and English, suggesting some level of generalizability across languages.
* **Limitations:**

    * The definition of emotion, behaviour and logic might be subjective and hence affect the quality of final predictions.
    * It would be good if the authors added some quantitative metrics to validate the effectiveness of the model.
    * Generalizing across different language datasets is challenging and hence there is still a limitation to the models applicability.

*Overall Assessment:*

The paper presents a well-motivated and executed approach to cognitive distortion detection. The combination of ELB decomposition, LLM-based instance inference, and MIL with multi-view attention is a novel and effective way to improve performance and interpretability. The empirical results support the claims, and the psychological grounding of the framework makes it potentially useful for clinical applications. However, the authors should address the limitations to add to the rigour of the paper.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper introduces a novel and well-executed framework for cognitive distortion detection, integrating psychologically informed utterance decomposition with LLM-based instance inference and a multi-view attention MIL architecture. The empirical results demonstrate improved performance, particularly for distortions with high interpretive ambiguity. The limitations are well addressed and do not have a major affect on the model's performance. All this means the authors have made a significant contribution.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Rational Multi-Modal Transformers for TCR-pMHC Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17305v1)
- **Authors**: Jiarui Li, Zixiang Yin, Zhengming Ding, Samuel J. Landry, Ramgopal R. Mettu
- **Abstract**: T cell receptor (TCR) recognition of peptide-MHC (pMHC) complexes is fundamental to adaptive immunity and central to the development of T cell-based immunotherapies. While transformer-based models have shown promise in predicting TCR-pMHC interactions, most lack a systematic and explainable approach to architecture design. We present an approach that uses a new post-hoc explainability method to inform the construction of a novel encoder-decoder transformer model. By identifying the most informative combinations of TCR and epitope sequence inputs, we optimize cross-attention strategies, incorporate auxiliary training objectives, and introduce a novel early-stopping criterion based on explanation quality. Our framework achieves state-of-the-art predictive performance while simultaneously improving explainability, robustness, and generalization. This work establishes a principled, explanation-driven strategy for modeling TCR-pMHC binding and offers mechanistic insights into sequence-level binding behavior through the lens of deep learning.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Rational Multi-Modal Transformers for TCR-pMHC Prediction." Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

This paper proposes a novel, explainability-driven approach to building transformer models for predicting T cell receptor (TCR) – peptide-MHC (pMHC) binding.  The key idea is to use post-hoc explainability methods (specifically QCAI and AttnLRP) to inform architectural design choices, focusing on input modality selection, cross-attention design, loss function strategies, and training strategies.  The authors systematically evaluate various design configurations, using both performance metrics (AUC, BRHR) and explainability analysis, ultimately arriving at a model ("EGM-2") that achieves state-of-the-art predictive performance while improving explainability, robustness, and generalization. The paper analyzes the contributions of CDR regions, non-CDR regions, and different cross-attention strategies in modeling TCR-pMHC binding. They also demonstrate the impact of auxiliary loss functions and explanation-based training strategies on model performance and generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper's primary novelty lies in its **systematic, explainability-driven approach** to TCR-pMHC model design. While transformer models have been applied to this problem before (TULIP, MixTCRpred, BERTrand), this paper makes a strong claim in that it uses explainability to *guide* the design of the model architecture, rather than simply applying a standard architecture and then trying to explain it afterward. The application of a post-hoc explainability method *during* model building is a useful novelty. It’s rare to find a systematic approach to architectural search guided by XAI. The decomposition of the model construction into four key stages is also a novel and useful aspect of the work. The use of a binding-region hit rate using the TCR-XAI dataset developed in their previous work to evaluate explainability is an important contribution. The study of auxiliary loss functions and customized training methods to improve generalization and explanation is also notable.

*Significance:*

The paper has the potential to be significantly influential for a few reasons:

1.  **Improved Performance:** Achieving state-of-the-art performance in TCR-pMHC binding prediction is intrinsically valuable for immunology and immunotherapy. Accurately predicting these interactions is crucial for developing effective vaccines and personalized cancer therapies.
2.  **Enhanced Explainability:** The explicit focus on explainability is important. "Black box" models are less likely to be trusted and adopted in critical applications like drug discovery.  By making the model more interpretable, the authors increase its potential for real-world use and also enable researchers to gain deeper insights into the underlying biological mechanisms.
3.  **Generalizability of the Approach:** The systematic design strategy, centered on explainability, could be generalized to other complex biological problems involving multi-modal data and transformer models. This makes the paper's contribution broader than just TCR-pMHC prediction. The explicit study of interactions for CDR and non-CDR regions provides mechanistic insights into binding.
4.  **TCR-XAI benchmark:** The TCR-XAI benchmark, along with BRHR, facilitates the direct evaluation of model explanation quality.

*Weaknesses:*

1.  **Incremental Improvements:** While state-of-the-art, the performance improvements over existing methods may be viewed as incremental (4-6% in AUC). It would be useful to analyze the situations in which EGM-2 makes a correct prediction but a previous method fails. Understanding this would increase confidence that it’s more than an incremental improvement.
2.  **Computational Cost:** The process of exploring a multitude of models using explainability methods is computationally expensive. The paper should more explicitly state the computational overhead of this approach and compare it to other model design strategies. It's important to acknowledge that the "explainability-guided" approach may be more time-consuming than other methods. This will be an important tradeoff for researchers when they weigh whether or not to use this approach.
3.  **Reliance on Post-hoc Explanations:** Post-hoc explanation methods have known limitations, including potential unfaithfulness to the model's actual decision-making process. The paper acknowledges some of these limitations, it would be interesting to see how well their approach generalizes with other explanation techniques.
4.  **Dataset Dependency:** The conclusions regarding the importance of various CDR regions/cross-attention strategies are to some extent dataset-dependent.  It would strengthen the paper to analyze other datasets and demonstrate the consistency of the findings. The authors should acknowledge this.

*Score Justification:*

The paper demonstrates a novel and systematic approach to transformer model design, yielding state-of-the-art results with improved explainability in an important biological problem. While the performance improvements may be viewed as incremental and the computational cost can be substantial, the generalizable strategy of incorporating explainability into model design carries significant weight, as does the benchmark they developed and tested against. Because of the potential for the general method to be applied to different problem domains, and because explainability provides added trust to models, this paper represents an important advance.

### Clotho: Measuring Task-Specific Pre-Generation Test Adequacy for LLM Inputs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17314v1)
- **Authors**: Juyeon Yoon, Somin Kim, Robert Feldt, Shin Yoo
- **Abstract**: Software increasingly relies on the emergent capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs), from natural language understanding to program analysis and generation. Yet testing them on specific tasks remains difficult and costly: many prompts lack ground truth, forcing reliance on human judgment, while existing uncertainty and adequacy measures typically require full inference. A key challenge is to assess input adequacy in a way that reflects the demands of the task, ideally before even generating any output. We introduce CLOTHO, a task-specific, pre-generation adequacy measure that estimates input difficulty directly from hidden LLM states. Given a large pool of unlabelled inputs for a specific task, CLOTHO uses a Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM) to adaptively sample the most informative cases for human labelling. Based on this reference set the GMM can then rank unseen inputs by their likelihood of failure. In our empirical evaluation across eight benchmark tasks and three open-weight LLMs, CLOTHO can predict failures with a ROC-AUC of 0.716, after labelling reference sets that are on average only 5.4% of inputs. It does so without generating any outputs, thereby reducing costs compared to existing uncertainty measures. Comparison of CLOTHO and post-generation uncertainty measures shows that the two approaches complement each other. Crucially, we show that adequacy scores learnt from open-weight LLMs transfer effectively to proprietary models, extending the applicability of the approach. When prioritising test inputs for proprietary models, CLOTHO increases the average number of failing inputs from 18.7 to 42.5 out of 100, compared to random prioritisation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CLOTHO, a novel approach to measure task-specific pre-generation test adequacy for Large Language Model (LLM) inputs.  CLOTHO estimates input difficulty directly from the LLM's hidden states, without requiring full inference or ground truth labels for all inputs. It employs a Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM) to adaptively sample informative cases for human labeling. The GMM is then used to rank unseen inputs based on their likelihood of failure. The authors empirically evaluate CLOTHO on various benchmark tasks and LLMs, demonstrating its ability to predict failures with limited labeling effort and its complementarity with post-generation uncertainty measures.  A key finding is that adequacy scores learned from open-weight LLMs can be effectively transferred to proprietary models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in its pre-generation approach to assessing input adequacy. Existing uncertainty measures rely on post-generation signals, incurring significant computational costs. CLOTHO directly addresses this limitation by leveraging hidden states, offering a more efficient assessment mechanism. The adaptive sampling strategy to minimize labeling effort is also a valuable contribution, particularly given the expense of obtaining ground truth for LLM outputs.

*   **Significance:** LLM testing is a critical challenge. The cost associated with validation and the opacity of LLM inner workings necessitate more efficient testing methods. CLOTHO presents a practical solution that reduces the cost of testing by prioritizing problematic inputs for labeling, allowing developers to focus efforts where it matters most. Its ability to transfer learned adequacies from open-weight models to proprietary ones greatly increases its practicality and real-world applicability where access to model internals is limited.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Pre-generation Assessment:** Avoids the computational cost of full inference.
    *   **Adaptive Sampling:** Reduces the burden of manual labeling.
    *   **Task-Specificity:** Focuses on relevant aspects for a defined task, enhancing performance.
    *   **Transferability:** Works with internal states from open-weight models while applying insights to closed-weight models.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Comprehensive experiments across a variety of tasks and models.
    *   **Complementarity:** Integrates well with post-generation analysis methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on Hidden States:** While the transferability to closed models mitigates this, the core method depends on accessing hidden states from *some* model during training. This may limit its applicability in certain scenarios.
    *   **GMM Limitations:** GMM is a well-established method, but it might not capture all the complexities in the hidden state space of LLMs. More advanced density estimation techniques could potentially further improve performance.
    *   **Limited Task Diversity:** The evaluation focuses primarily on tasks where correctness is reasonably easy to assess automatically. Assessing adequacy for more nuanced tasks (e.g., those requiring human evaluation of creativity or style) might present additional challenges.

*   **Potential Influence:** CLOTHO has the potential to significantly impact the field of LLM testing. By reducing the cost and complexity of input validation, it could enable more robust and reliable LLM-based applications. Its emphasis on pre-generation assessment could inspire further research into lightweight, efficient testing techniques. The transfer learning component broadens it's usability.

**Justification for Score:**

CLOTHO presents a significant advancement in LLM testing by addressing the cost and complexity associated with traditional post-generation approaches. The novelty of its pre-generation assessment, combined with its empirical validation and practical transfer learning capabilities, makes it a valuable contribution to the field. While some limitations exist regarding the dependency on *some* access to internal states and the complexity of latent space modelling techniques, the benefits outweigh these drawbacks. It also integrates well with existing approaches. The work also benefits from clear writing. The experiments section, in particular, contains useful discussion to contextualise the results and not just report numbers.

### CogAtom: From Cognitive Atoms to Olympiad-level Mathematical Reasoning in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17318v1)
- **Authors**: Zhuofan Chen, Jiyuan He, Yichi Zhang, Xing Hu, Haoxing Wen, Jun Bai, Wenge Rong
- **Abstract**: Mathematical reasoning poses significant challenges for Large Language Models (LLMs) due to its demand for multi-step reasoning and abstract conceptual integration. While recent test-time scaling techniques rely heavily on high-quality, challenging problems, the scarcity of Olympiad-level math problems remains a bottleneck. We introduce CogAtom, a novel cognitive atom-based framework for synthesizing mathematically rigorous and cognitively diverse problems. Unlike prior approaches, CogAtom models problem construction as a process of selecting and recombining fundamental reasoning units, cognitive atoms, extracted from human-authored solutions. A diversity-promoting random walk algorithm enables exploration of the cognitive atom space, while a constraint-based recombination mechanism ensures logical soundness and structural validity. The combinatorial nature of the graph structure provides a near-infinite space of reasoning paths, and the walk algorithm systematically explores this space to achieve large-scale synthesis of high-quality problems; meanwhile, by controlling the number of cognitive atoms, we can precisely adjust problem difficulty, ensuring diversity, scalability, and controllability of the generated problems. Experimental results demonstrate that CogAtom outperforms existing methods in accuracy, reasoning depth, and diversity, generating problems that closely match the difficulty of AIME while exceeding it in structural variation. Our work offers a cognitively grounded pathway toward scalable, high-quality math problem generation.Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/Icarus-1111/CogAtom.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CogAtom: From Cognitive Atoms to Olympiad-level Mathematical Reasoning in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CogAtom, a novel framework for synthesizing challenging mathematical problems suitable for training and evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs).  CogAtom departs from linear generation approaches by modeling problem construction as a structured synthesis process centered on the "Cognitive Association Graph". The key innovations include:

1.  **Reasoning Atoms:** Extraction of fundamental reasoning units (cognitive atoms) from human-authored solutions to serve as building blocks.
2.  **Cognitive Association Graph:**  Construction of a graph representing the relationships between these atoms, enabling exploration of reasoning paths.
3.  **Cognitive Transfer Operators:**  Three operators (Path Extension, Bridge Replacement, and Counterfactual Perturbation) to ensure logical soundness, structural validity, and conceptual diversity of the synthesized problems.
4. Automated Quality and Complexity Assessment Protocol: a new protocol that assesses the reasoning complexity by levereging LLMs to evaluate candidate problems.

The framework demonstrates its effectiveness through experiments showing that LLMs trained on CogAtom-generated data outperform those trained on data from existing methods in terms of accuracy, reasoning depth, and diversity, particularly on challenging Olympic-level problems. The paper also investigates the scalability of the data generation engine and its potential for cross-domain generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of several ideas: grounding problem generation in cognitive atoms, the graph-based representation of reasoning paths, and the cognitive transfer operators to guide the synthesis process. The emphasis on capturing high-level cognitive connections, as opposed to just statistical co-occurrences, is a significant departure from prior work. The Automated Quality and Complexity Assessment Protocol is also novel.
*   **Significance:** The scarcity of high-quality, diverse, and scalable math problems is a major bottleneck in advancing LLMs for reasoning. CogAtom addresses this problem directly. The reported performance gains on challenging benchmarks, particularly AIME, demonstrate the potential to significantly improve the mathematical reasoning capabilities of LLMs. The ablation studies further solidify the importance of each proposed component of the method. Also, the data scalability analysis is very important to see how the method is capable of improving LLMs as the data grows.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper presents a well-defined and theoretically motivated framework.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive and compelling, demonstrating the superiority of CogAtom over existing methods across multiple benchmarks and model architectures.
    *   The ablation studies effectively isolate the contribution of each component of the framework.
    *   The code is publicly available, facilitating reproducibility and further research.
    *   The Automated Quality and Complexity Assessment Protocol offers a novel approach to measure reasoning skills that are usually done by humans.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The complexity of the CogAtom framework and reliance on prompting could make the approach expensive or complex to implement for some use cases.
    *   The reliance on GPT-4 for expert knowledge extraction may introduce biases present in the LLM.
    *   Although the cross-domain generalization results are promising, further exploration of diverse domains beyond physics is warranted.
    *   The method focuses mostly on the text data modality.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLMs for mathematical reasoning. The cognitive atom approach could be adopted by other researchers for generating high-quality training data, leading to improved reasoning capabilities in LLMs. The emphasis on scalable data generation is also crucial for the continued advancement of the field.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a strong understanding of the problem, proposes a novel and well-justified solution, and provides compelling evidence of its effectiveness. The cognitive atom approach, the graph-based representation, and the transfer operators are significant innovations that address the critical need for high-quality, scalable math problem data. While there are some limitations in terms of complexity, reliance on LLMs, and scope of cross-domain evaluation, the overall contribution of the paper is substantial.

### DiffQ: Unified Parameter Initialization for Variational Quantum Algorithms via Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17324v1)
- **Authors**: Chi Zhang, Mengxin Zheng, Qian Lou, Fan Chen
- **Abstract**: Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs) are widely used in the noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) era, but their trainability and performance depend critically on initialization parameters that shape the optimization landscape. Existing machine learning-based initializers achieve state-of-the-art results yet remain constrained to single-task domains and small datasets of only hundreds of samples. We address these limitations by reformulating VQA parameter initialization as a generative modeling problem and introducing DiffQ, a parameter initializer based on the Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model (DDPM). To support robust training and evaluation, we construct a dataset of 15,085 instances spanning three domains and five representative tasks. Experiments demonstrate that DiffQ surpasses baselines, reducing initial loss by up to 8.95 and convergence steps by up to 23.4%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DiffQ: Unified Parameter Initialization for Variational Quantum Algorithms via Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DiffQ, a novel framework for initializing parameters in Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs). It tackles the limitations of existing machine learning-based initializers, which are often task-specific and trained on small datasets. DiffQ reformulates parameter initialization as a generative modeling problem, utilizing Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPMs). The framework encodes optimized VQA parameters into tensors, trains a DDPM to denoise corrupted versions conditioned on textual prompts describing the VQA task, and then generates high-quality initial parameters for new tasks based on their descriptions. The authors construct a large dataset of 15,085 VQA instances across diverse domains and tasks to support training and evaluation. Experiments demonstrate that DiffQ outperforms random initialization in terms of reducing initial loss and convergence steps, especially in structured, domain-informed applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** Applying DDPMs to VQA parameter initialization is a relatively novel approach.  It shifts from discriminative to generative learning, potentially capturing underlying distributions of good parameter sets.
*   **Addressing Limitations:**  The paper clearly identifies and addresses the limitations of existing ML-based initializers, such as their task-specificity and reliance on small datasets.
*   **Large-Scale Dataset:**  The construction of a large, diverse VQA dataset is a significant contribution. This dataset facilitates more robust training and evaluation compared to prior works. The public release of this dataset would be extremely beneficial.
*   **Improved Performance:** The reported results show promising improvements over random initialization in terms of initial loss and convergence steps, indicating that DiffQ can potentially accelerate VQA training.
*   **Clear Architecture and Methodology:** The paper clearly outlines the architecture of DiffQ, the training strategy, and the inference procedure, making it relatively easy to understand and reproduce.
* **Task-Specific Guidance:** Conditioning the DDPM on textual prompts that describe the VQA task is an intelligent way to incorporate domain-specific information and improve the quality of the generated parameters.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalization:** The paper acknowledges that DiffQ performs less well on unstructured or trivial tasks (Random VQE). This suggests that the framework's effectiveness is highly dependent on the presence of underlying structure and correlations in the problem, potentially limiting its general applicability.  The dependency on domain-specific structure makes it more like "domain-specific transfer learning."
*   **Computational Cost:** DDPMs are known to be computationally expensive to train and sample from. The paper doesn't provide a detailed analysis of the computational overhead of DiffQ compared to simpler initialization methods or other machine-learning-based approaches.
*   **Comparison to Other ML Initializers:** The paper primarily compares DiffQ against random initialization. A more rigorous evaluation would involve comparisons with existing state-of-the-art ML-based initializers (e.g., those mentioned in the introduction but expanded upon). Why is DiffQ better?
*   **Limited Analysis of Parameter Encoding:** The paper provides a brief description of the parameter encoding scheme. A more in-depth discussion on the impact of different encoding strategies on the performance of DiffQ would be valuable. This could be discussed in the appendix.
*   **Text Prompt Sensitivity:** The performance relies on carefully crafted text prompts. The paper lacks a discussion of the sensitivity of DiffQ to the choice of prompt and the potential challenges in designing prompts for new or complex tasks.
* **Missing Evaluation Metric:** The paper does not evaluate the performance of DiffQ on circuits with noise. It is important to see the impact of the initialization scheme in a noisy environment.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its demonstration of the potential of generative models, specifically DDPMs, for addressing a critical challenge in VQA: parameter initialization.  By leveraging the generative capabilities of DDPMs and conditioning them on task-specific information, DiffQ offers a promising approach for improving VQA trainability and performance, especially for structured domain problems.  The large dataset and clear methodology also contribute to the field by providing a valuable resource for future research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel application of DDPMs to VQA parameter initialization, supported by a large, diverse dataset and promising experimental results. However, the limitations regarding generalization to unstructured tasks, computational cost, lack of comparisons to SOTA ML methods, and sensitivity to prompt design temper enthusiasm. It fills a gap and does improve on random initialization but is not quite generalizable. Also, there is a lack of discussion regarding noise and how that impacts performance. Thus, a reasonable score is:

**Score: 6**
- **Classification**: cs.ET
- **Score**: 6/10

### Generalizable End-to-End Tool-Use RL with Synthetic CodeGym
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17325v1)
- **Authors**: Weihua Du, Hailei Gong, Zhan Ling, Kang Liu, Lingfeng Shen, Xuesong Yao, Yufei Xu, Dingyuan Shi, Yiming Yang, Jiecao Chen
- **Abstract**: Tool-augmented large language models (LLMs), hereafter LLM agents, leverage external tools to solve diverse tasks and interface with the real world. However, current training practices largely rely on supervised fine-tuning (SFT) over static trajectories or reinforcement learning (RL) on narrow tasks, and generalize poorly beyond development settings, leading to brittleness with new tools and unseen workflows. Because code execution reflects many structures of real-world workflows, coding problems provide a natural basis for building agent training environments. Motivated by this, we introduce CodeGym, a scalable framework that synthesizes diverse, verifiable, and controllable multi-turn tool-use environments for agent RL, enabling LLM agents to explore and master various workflows actively. CodeGym rewrites static coding problems into interactive environments by extracting atomic functions or logic into callable tools, yielding verifiable tasks that span various tool-execution workflows. Models of varying sizes and chain-of-thought configurations, trained in CodeGym, exhibit consistent out-of-distribution generalizability; for example, Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct achieves an absolute accuracy gain of 8.7 points on the OOD benchmark $\tau$-Bench. These results highlight CodeGym as a step toward scalable general-purpose RL environments that align with real-world agent workflows.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CodeGym, a novel framework for training tool-augmented Large Language Model (LLM) agents.  CodeGym automatically transforms static coding problems into interactive, multi-turn tool-use environments suitable for reinforcement learning (RL).  It achieves this by extracting atomic functions from coding solutions and exposing them as callable tools. The environment provides verifiable rewards, enabling agents to actively explore and master different workflows. The authors demonstrate that LLMs trained with CodeGym show improved out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization on benchmarks like T-Bench compared to models trained via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) alone.  The paper details the CodeGym pipeline, covering environment synthesis, quality control, and a distributed RL training framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of automatically generating tool-use environments from coding problems is relatively novel. While synthetic environments for agent training exist, CodeGym's focus on verifiable tool-use workflows derived from the inherent logic of code execution is a unique contribution. The approach addresses the limitations of static SFT data by enabling active exploration and learning.
*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to create scalable and diverse RL environments for LLM agents.  The documented improvements in OOD generalization suggest that CodeGym can address a critical bottleneck in applying LLM agents to real-world tasks, which often involve novel tool combinations and workflows. The work offers a promising direction for general-purpose agent training.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Automated Environment Generation:** Automating environment generation is a crucial step toward scalable RL training.
    *   **Verifiable Rewards:** The code-based foundation provides intrinsic verifiability, enabling robust reward signals for RL.
    *   **Improved Generalization:** Empirical evidence shows significant OOD performance gains over SFT baselines, demonstrating the benefits of the approach.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The paper provides a detailed explanation of the entire pipeline, from environment synthesis to distributed training.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the coding domain offers a structured approach, the applicability to all real-world tasks might be limited. Not all tasks can be easily translated into a coding-like structure with discrete tool calls and deterministic outcomes.
    *   **Metric Bias:** While using coding tasks offers a verifiable reward, it is worth noting whether the success metric for different tasks is skewed towards coding-related skills.
    *   **Evaluation Rigor:** While significant gains are shown, a thorough ablation study with a broader set of benchmarks and a more in-depth analysis of the types of tasks where CodeGym is most effective would strengthen the results.

*   **Potential Influence:** CodeGym has the potential to influence the field by providing a new paradigm for training generalizable tool-use LLM agents.  It could inspire further research into automated environment generation techniques and the use of structured domains like code to create robust RL training data.  The released codebase and datasets should facilitate further experimentation and development in this area.
*   **Room for improvement:** The analysis and discussion of some results are somewhat limited and warrant further elaboration and discussion. For instance, the study of models' action patterns might offer insights for future developments.

**Justification:**

The score of 7.5 reflects a solid contribution with clear novelty and potential impact, but with some limitations. The paper addresses a critical problem (OOD generalization in tool-use LLMs) with a novel and automated approach. The empirical results are promising, but further validation with more diverse tasks and a deeper understanding of the learned representations and behaviors would be beneficial. Although the study demonstrated improved performance compared with the baseline, there may be issues with limited scope, evaluation rigor, and discussion of some results.

### LLaVul: A Multimodal LLM for Interpretable Vulnerability Reasoning about Source Code
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17337v1)
- **Authors**: Ala Jararweh, Michael Adams, Avinash Sahu, Abdullah Mueen, Afsah Anwar
- **Abstract**: Increasing complexity in software systems places a growing demand on reasoning tools that unlock vulnerabilities manifest in source code. Many current approaches focus on vulnerability analysis as a classifying task, oversimplifying the nuanced and context-dependent real-world scenarios. Even though current code large language models (LLMs) excel in code understanding, they often pay little attention to security-specific reasoning. We propose LLaVul, a multimodal LLM tailored to provide fine-grained reasoning about code through question-answering (QA). Our model is trained to integrate paired code and natural queries into a unified space, enhancing reasoning and context-dependent insights about code vulnerability. To evaluate our model performance, we construct a curated dataset of real-world vulnerabilities paired with security-focused questions and answers. Our model outperforms state-of-the-art general-purpose and code LLMs in the QA and detection tasks. We further explain decision-making by conducting qualitative analysis to highlight capabilities and limitations. By integrating code and QA, LLaVul enables more interpretable and security-focused code understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LLaVul: A Multimodal LLM for Interpretable Vulnerability Reasoning about Source Code":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLaVul, a multimodal Large Language Model (LLM) designed for improved vulnerability reasoning in source code. It integrates code and natural language Question Answering (QA) into a unified space. The model is trained on pairs of code and security-focused questions and answers. A new benchmark, LLaVul-QA, is introduced, built from real-world code vulnerabilities paired with human-generated (via a LLM) questions and answers.  The authors demonstrate that LLaVul outperforms general-purpose and code-specific LLMs in both QA and vulnerability detection tasks. They also provide qualitative analysis to highlight the model's capabilities and limitations. The goal is to create a more interpretable and security-focused code understanding system.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific integration of a multimodal LLM for *security-focused* QA, tailored to vulnerability reasoning. While existing works use LLMs for code understanding or vulnerability *classification*, LLaVul directly focuses on providing deeper insights and context-dependent explanations regarding vulnerabilities through a question-answering paradigm. The creation of the LLaVul-QA dataset is also a notable contribution, addressing the lack of comprehensive and security-specific benchmarks beyond binary classification. Adapting the LLaVA architecture to code is not fundamentally novel, but the problem focus and the comprehensive evaluation are. Using an LLM to *generate* the QA pairs, though necessary to scale data creation, introduces a level of artificiality which is a limitation.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve software security practices. By providing a more interpretable and reasoning-driven approach to vulnerability analysis, LLaVul could assist developers in understanding and mitigating security flaws more effectively. The QA format allows for a more nuanced understanding compared to simple classification models, which only label code as 'vulnerable' or 'not vulnerable'.  Furthermore, a public LLaVul-QA dataset will provide a resource to the community to enhance LLM abilities in code security. A key weakness is that the dataset itself is synthetic since the QA pairs are generated using an LLM. This potentially limits the dataset's ability to capture the full complexity of real-world vulnerability scenarios.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined model architecture based on LLaVA.
    *   The new LLaVul-QA benchmark addresses a gap in available security-focused datasets.
    *   Quantitative results demonstrate superior performance compared to baselines.
    *   Qualitative analysis provides insights into model behavior.
    *   Ablation studies highlight the importance of different components.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The use of an LLM to create the QA pairs introduces a degree of artificiality and potential limitations in capturing the full complexity of real-world vulnerabilities.
    *   Lack of human evaluation on the quality of the generated QA pairs.
    *   The focus on single-file vulnerabilities limits the scope of the approach.
    *   The model's potential for 'hallucinations' needs further investigation and mitigation strategies.
    *   The dataset's size is a potential limitation, and further scaling could improve the model's generalizability.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by promoting the use of multimodal LLMs for security-specific reasoning. The LLaVul-QA dataset could become a valuable resource for training and evaluating future models. The interpretable QA approach could inspire new tools and techniques for vulnerability analysis, ultimately leading to more secure software systems.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by presenting LLaVul and its associated benchmark. The integration of multimodal LLMs for interpretable vulnerability reasoning is a promising direction. The reported improvements over baseline models are compelling. However, the reliance on synthetically generated QA pairs and limitations in scope (single-file vulnerabilities, potential for hallucinations) prevent a higher score. The LLaVul-QA dataset has significant value, but its synthetic nature is a key constraint.  The work is a strong incremental step, but falls short of being transformative.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### AIMMerging: Adaptive Iterative Model Merging Using Training Trajectories for Language Model Continual Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17348v1)
- **Authors**: Yujie Feng, Jian Li, Xiaoyu Dong, Pengfei Xu, Xiaohui Zhou, Yujia Zhang, Zexin LU, Yasha Wang, Alan Zhao, Xu Chu, Xiao-Ming Wu
- **Abstract**: Continual learning (CL) is essential for deploying large language models (LLMs) in dynamic real-world environments without the need for costly retraining. Recent model merging-based methods have attracted significant attention, but they still struggle to effectively manage the trade-off between learning new knowledge and preventing forgetting, a challenge largely stemming from suboptimal number of merges and merging frequency. In this paper, we introduce Adaptive Iterative Model Merging (AimMerging), a novel CL framework that utilizes learning and forgetting signals from the training trajectory to dynamically monitor the model's training status. Guided by dynamic monitoring, the training trajectory-guided merge controller adaptively determines the timing and frequency of iterative fusion, while the rehearsal-based knowledge fusion module computes the merging weights and executes the fusion. Comprehensive experiments on three CL benchmarks with various model sizes (from 770M to 13B) demonstrate that AimMerging achieves significant performance improvements over existing state-of-the-art methods, with an average relative improvement of 80% and 59% on FWT and BWT, respectively. The source code is provided for reproducibility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AIMMerging, a novel continual learning (CL) framework designed for large language models (LLMs). AIMMerging addresses the challenge of balancing new knowledge acquisition and preventing catastrophic forgetting (CF) by adaptively adjusting model merging frequency and weights during training. The core idea is to use learning and forgetting signals derived from the training trajectory to dynamically monitor the model's status. A training trajectory-guided merge controller schedules merges, and a rehearsal-based knowledge fusion module handles the merging operation itself. The paper presents extensive experiments on three CL benchmarks with different model sizes, demonstrating significant improvements over existing methods in Forward Transfer (FWT) and Backward Transfer (BWT).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the adaptive and dynamic nature of the merging process, driven by training trajectory signals (learning and forgetting). Prior work on model merging in CL often relies on fixed intervals or single-round merges. While multi-round merging has been explored, the approach of using training dynamics to *guide* the merging process is a distinct contribution. However, the novelty of individual components may be argued. The use of rehearsal is standard, and the learning/forgetting signals are somewhat intuitive. The main contribution is the clever integration and application of these signals for adaptive merging.
*   **Significance:** Continual learning is a critical area for deploying LLMs in real-world settings. Model merging offers a promising pathway to achieve CL capabilities. The paper demonstrates substantial performance gains compared to existing state-of-the-art methods, particularly in mitigating CF and facilitating knowledge transfer. This suggests that AIMMerging could have a practical impact on how LLMs are updated and deployed in dynamic environments. The consistent performance gains across different model sizes and datasets strengthens the significance. However, the evaluation focuses primarily on performance metrics.  A deeper analysis of the *types* of knowledge transferred or retained would provide greater insight.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined framework with distinct modules.
    *   Extensive experimental validation on diverse benchmarks.
    *   Significant performance improvements over baselines.
    *   Ablation studies to validate the importance of individual components.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The individual components (rehearsal, learning/forgetting signals) are not inherently novel; the novelty lies in their integration.
    *   The choice of hyperparameters is guided by heuristic design choices rather than fully automated optimization.
    *   The analysis is primarily quantitative. A qualitative analysis of the knowledge transferred or retained would strengthen the paper.
    *   The reliance on a memory buffer is a limitation, especially given the memory constraints when dealing with very large models. Addressing this would improve scalability and practical applicability.
*   **Impact:** AIMMerging introduces a potentially impactful approach to model merging in CL. The adaptive merging strategy guided by training trajectory signals could inspire further research in this area. The demonstrated performance improvements warrant attention from researchers and practitioners interested in deploying LLMs in dynamic environments.

**Overall Score:**

Score: 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-validated CL framework that improves upon existing model merging techniques. The adaptive merging strategy is a significant contribution, and the experimental results are compelling. However, the novelty of individual components is not particularly high, and the dependence on a memory buffer and heuristics design of the hyperparameters are limitations. A fully automated hyperparameter optimization and memory-free variants would further solidify its impact. The score reflects the balance of these strengths and weaknesses, recognizing the potential of the work while acknowledging its limitations and potential for improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Medical AI Consensus: A Multi-Agent Framework for Radiology Report Generation and Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17353v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmed T. Elboardy, Ghada Khoriba, Essam A. Rashed
- **Abstract**: Automating radiology report generation poses a dual challenge: building clinically reliable systems and designing rigorous evaluation protocols. We introduce a multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that serves as both a benchmark and evaluation environment for multimodal clinical reasoning in the radiology ecosystem. The proposed framework integrates large language models (LLMs) and large vision models (LVMs) within a modular architecture composed of ten specialized agents responsible for image analysis, feature extraction, report generation, review, and evaluation. This design enables fine-grained assessment at both the agent level (e.g., detection and segmentation accuracy) and the consensus level (e.g., report quality and clinical relevance). We demonstrate an implementation using chatGPT-4o on public radiology datasets, where LLMs act as evaluators alongside medical radiologist feedback. By aligning evaluation protocols with the LLM development lifecycle, including pretraining, finetuning, alignment, and deployment, the proposed benchmark establishes a path toward trustworthy deviance-based radiology report generation.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's analyze this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "Medical AI Consensus: A Multi-Agent Framework for Radiology Report Generation and Evaluation" introduces a multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) framework designed for both generating and evaluating radiology reports. This framework integrates Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Vision Models (LVMs) into a modular architecture comprising ten specialized agents responsible for various tasks, including image analysis, feature extraction, report generation, and evaluation. The aim is to provide a comprehensive benchmark and evaluation environment for multimodal clinical reasoning within radiology. The paper demonstrates an implementation using GPT-4 on public radiology datasets and proposes aligning evaluation protocols with the LLM development lifecycle to improve the reliability and trustworthiness of the AI systems. It aims to create a benchmark for "trustworthy deviance-based radiology report generation." The authors curate a dataset of multi-sequence brain MRI scans from cancer patients. It assesses performance at the agent and system levels using metrics like accuracy, clinical completeness, and human-in-the-loop review.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of a multi-agent system for radiology report generation isn't entirely new. Several existing works (referenced in the paper itself) explore MAS in medical imaging. However, this paper's novelty lies in the specific architecture (integration of 10 specialized agents with LLMs and LVMs in this way is not previously done in this manner) and the emphasis on creating a comprehensive *evaluation* framework that aligns with the LLM lifecycle.  The concurrent focus on *both* report generation and rigorous evaluation, particularly incorporating LLM-as-judge mechanisms alongside human experts, is a differentiating factor. The LLM lifecycle specific alignment for radiological tasks is also a novel contribution. The concept of having individual evaluation metrics for each agent and composite metrics for overall performance is valuable for benchmarking. The curated dataset is a positive aspect, but the significance depends on its size, uniqueness, and availability, which are not explicitly described in detail.

*   **Significance:**  Radiology report generation is a high-impact area with the potential to improve efficiency, reduce errors, and ultimately enhance patient care. The proposed framework, if successful, could provide a standardized way to develop, benchmark, and deploy AI systems in this domain.  The ability to evaluate individual agents and the overall system rigorously is crucial for building trust in AI-driven solutions. Aligning evaluation to the LLM lifecycle stages is critical to building systems with clinical utility. Addressing the gap in systematic quantitative benchmarks is important.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Architecture:** The detailed breakdown of the system into ten specialized agents covering different stages of the process demonstrates careful consideration of the radiology workflow.
    *   **Emphasis on Evaluation:** The focus on both generation and evaluation is a key strength. The paper proposes concrete methods for measuring performance at various levels.
    *   **LLM Lifecycle Integration:**  Aligning the evaluation with different stages of LLM development (pretraining, finetuning, alignment, deployment) is a forward-thinking approach.
    *   **Human-in-the-Loop:** The inclusion of a quality assurance agent with a human-in-the-loop component is crucial for ensuring clinical relevance and safety.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Detailed Results:** While the paper presents some results on the RHUH-GBM dataset, the level of detail is limited. The paper says it achieved an accuracy of 68.6% *without using patient metadata*. This is odd, since metadata, especially tumour type, is critical for diagnosis. More in depth experimental designs and results should be reported. The specific details and impact of the LLM as a judge, needs to be clearly elaborated.
    *   **Dataset Specificity:** The focus on a single dataset (RHUH-GBM) limits the generalizability of the findings. More detailed about data and experiments are warranted
    *   **Limited Discussion of Limitations:** While the paper addresses some potential limitations, a more thorough discussion of the challenges associated with each agent and the overall framework would be beneficial.
    *   **RLHF Loop:** The RLHF Loop simulations are not fully supported with data.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Providing a standardized benchmark for radiology report generation systems.
    *   Encouraging the development of more transparent and interpretable AI models.
    *   Facilitating the integration of LLMs and LVMs into clinical workflows.
    *   Promoting collaboration between AI researchers and radiologists.

**Score Rationale:**

While the paper presents a promising framework with several innovative aspects, the limited experimental results and the lack of in-depth analysis hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution. The novelty lies in the combination of components and the focus on systematic evaluation, but the impact is contingent on demonstrating its effectiveness on a wider range of datasets and clinical scenarios. Given these considerations, a score of **7** seems appropriate. The paper offers a significant contribution to the field by proposing a comprehensive framework and addressing a critical need for robust evaluation methods. However, it requires further validation and refinement to fully realize its potential.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multi-Scenario Highway Lane-Change Intention Prediction: A Physics-Informed AI Framework for Three-Class Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17354v1)
- **Authors**: Jiazhao Shi, Yichen Lin, Yiheng Hua, Ziyu Wang, Zijian Zhang, Wenjia Zheng, Yun Song, Kuan Lu, Shoufeng Lu
- **Abstract**: Lane-change maneuvers are a leading cause of highway accidents, underscoring the need for accurate intention prediction to improve the safety and decision-making of autonomous driving systems. While prior studies using machine learning and deep learning methods (e.g., SVM, CNN, LSTM, Transformers) have shown promise, most approaches remain limited by binary classification, lack of scenario diversity, and degraded performance under longer prediction horizons. In this study, we propose a physics-informed AI framework that explicitly integrates vehicle kinematics, interaction feasibility, and traffic-safety metrics (e.g., distance headway, time headway, time-to-collision, closing gap time) into the learning process. lane-change prediction is formulated as a three-class problem that distinguishes left change, right change, and no change, and is evaluated across both straight highway segments (highD) and complex ramp scenarios (exiD). By integrating vehicle kinematics with interaction features, our machine learning models, particularly LightGBM, achieve state-of-the-art accuracy and strong generalization. Results show up to 99.8% accuracy and 93.6% macro F1 on highD, and 96.1% accuracy and 88.7% macro F1 on exiD at a 1-second horizon, outperforming a two-layer stacked LSTM baseline. These findings demonstrate the practical advantages of a physics-informed and feature-rich machine learning framework for real-time lane-change intention prediction in autonomous driving systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper presents a physics-informed AI framework for predicting lane-change intentions of vehicles on highways.  Unlike many existing approaches that focus on binary classification (change vs. no change), this work addresses the problem as a three-class classification task (left, right, or no lane change). The framework integrates vehicle kinematics, interaction feasibility, and traffic-safety metrics (e.g., time-to-collision) into the learning process. The authors evaluate their approach on the highD and exiD datasets, representing straight highway segments and ramp scenarios, respectively. They compare LightGBM and XGBoost against a two-layer stacked LSTM, finding that, under the current data conditions, traditional machine learning models offer superior prediction accuracy, training efficiency, and generalization. The results show the physics-informed LightGBM model achieving high accuracy and strong generalization, particularly in the presence of class imbalance.

**Critical Evaluation**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its physics-informed AI framework, the use of a three-class classification approach, and the systematic evaluation across diverse highway scenarios with class imbalance considerations. The combination of traffic-safety metrics and vehicle kinematics is a sensible and valuable approach. While physics-informed approaches aren't entirely new in other domains, its application in this specific context, with this feature set, is a relevant contribution. The observation that classic methods outperform deep learning is also valuable, as it argues for a more pragmatic approach to the problem. However, the individual components are not groundbreaking on their own.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its improved lane-change intention prediction accuracy and robustness, which are crucial for the safety and efficiency of autonomous driving systems. The performance achieved across different highway segments is promising. Critically, the study highlights a significant result: under current data conditions and with careful feature engineering, simpler models like LightGBM can outperform deep learning models, which challenges the common assumption that deep learning is always superior for complex tasks. It also provides a more realistic treatment of the problem with its multi-class approach. The open question of whether this conclusion will hold as training datasets improve and grow significantly diminishes the long-term impact of the study.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Physics-Informed Approach:** Integrates domain knowledge effectively.
    * **Three-Class Classification:**  More nuanced than binary classification.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Evaluated on both highD and exiD datasets, demonstrating generalization.
    * **Imbalance Handling:** Addresses the class imbalance issue thoughtfully.
    * **Comparison with Deep Learning:** The finding that simpler models can outperform deep learning is important.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvement:** While the framework is effective, the individual components are not radically novel. The gains made are incremental rather than revolutionary.
    * **Limited Deep Learning exploration:** The paper benchmarks against a relatively simple two-layer stacked LSTM. More advanced deep learning architectures, specifically designed for time series modeling, could yield different results. The conclusion of deep learning underperformance relies on this particular comparison.
    * **Dependency on feature engineering:** The success heavily depends on manual feature engineering.  The authors acknowledge the need for further exploration of approaches that can automate feature extraction.
    * **Scalability assumptions:** The paper demonstrates success on existing datasets, but the method has yet to be tested in more diverse and unpredictable conditions with complex interactions and higher traffic densities.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by encouraging a more pragmatic approach to lane-change intention prediction, focusing on incorporating domain knowledge and carefully considering feature engineering, instead of blindly applying deep learning methods.  The focus on cross-scenario robustness is important, and this work could motivate further research in this direction.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the paper's strengths and weaknesses, I assign it a score of **6**.

While the paper presents a solid and well-executed study with a valuable physics-informed approach and meaningful experimental results, its novelty is somewhat limited. The observation about simpler models outperforming deep learning is valuable and challenges common assumptions, but it is dependent on specific data conditions and the selected deep learning baseline. The results show improvements in lane-change intention predictions. Future investigation should focus on integrating stronger physics and traffic theory.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### CMOS Implementation of Field Programmable Spiking Neural Network for Hardware Reservoir Computing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17355v1)
- **Authors**: Ckristian Duran, Nanako Kimura, Zolboo Byambadorj, Tetsuya Iizuka
- **Abstract**: The increasing complexity and energy demands of large-scale neural networks, such as Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) and Large Language Models (LLMs), challenge their practical deployment in edge applications due to high power consumption, area requirements, and privacy concerns. Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs), particularly in analog implementations, offer a promising low-power alternative but suffer from noise sensitivity and connectivity limitations. This work presents a novel CMOS-implemented field-programmable neural network architecture for hardware reservoir computing. We propose a Leaky Integrate-and-Fire (LIF) neuron circuit with integrated voltage-controlled oscillators (VCOs) and programmable weighted interconnections via an on-chip FPGA framework, enabling arbitrary reservoir configurations. The system demonstrates effective implementation of the FORCE algorithm learning, linear and non-linear memory capacity benchmarks, and NARMA10 tasks, both in simulation and actual chip measurements.
