The data generation process involves generating responses to questions with LLMs, decomposing those responses into claims, assessing check-worthiness, retrieving supporting/refuting evidence, and finally, labeling the veracity of each claim. The authors conduct experiments using various LLMs in both in-context learning (ICL) and fine-tuning settings, revealing the capabilities and limitations of current models on this task.  An in-depth error analysis identifies key areas for future research, including addressing medical ambiguity, semantic containment, and understanding medical synonymy. The dataset is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in the creation of the MEDFACT dataset.  While medical fact-checking and LLM factuality evaluation are established areas, the combination of *Chinese language*, *LLM-generated medical content*, and *evidence-based verification* makes this dataset a unique and valuable resource. The paper claims to be the *first* of its kind, and based on the related work section, this seems to be a valid claim.  Existing datasets are smaller in scale and predominantly focus on human-generated content. However, the individual components of the pipeline are largely borrowed from existing work, which limits the novelty of the entire process.
* **Significance:** The significance stems from the increasing reliance on LLMs for medical information.  Verifying the accuracy of LLM-generated medical content is crucial for public health, making this dataset a timely and relevant contribution. By providing a benchmark for LLMs in a critical domain, the paper facilitates the development of more reliable and trustworthy medical AI systems. The detailed error analysis offers actionable insights for future research directions. The dataset creation process is well-documented, which makes it easy to use by other researchers.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **High-Quality Dataset:** The dataset creation pipeline is rigorous, combining LLM assistance with human annotation to ensure quality.  The detailed statistical analysis of the dataset provides insights into its characteristics and potential biases. The Inter-Annotator Agreement ensures that the veracity labels are correct.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering a range of LLMs and training paradigms.  This allows for a thorough evaluation of the current state-of-the-art. The analysis of both ICL and fine-tuning settings shows the capability of large models and the adaptability of smaller models.
    *   **Actionable Error Analysis:** The error analysis identifies specific challenges in medical fact-checking, providing clear directions for future research.
    *   **Publicly Available Dataset:**  Making the dataset publicly available fosters further research and development in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Language Scope:** The dataset is currently limited to Chinese, restricting its applicability to other languages. While a choice for focusing on specific population/language is justified, this constraint needs to be recognized.
    *   **Imbalanced Data:** The inherent imbalance in the veracity labels reflects the real-world distribution of medical information but poses challenges for model training and evaluation. The paper acknowledges this as a limitation but does not propose specific solutions beyond mentioning synthetic data generation or adversarial learning, which are merely ideas.
    *   **Dependency on External APIs**: Using the Google Search API for evidence retrieval introduces dependency and possible reproducibility issues. The quality of search results can change over time, which affect the veracity labelling.
    *   **Limited Model Performance:** Model performance is still significantly lower than human performance, indicating a lot of room for improvement.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field by providing a valuable resource for researchers working on medical fact-checking and LLM reliability. It could spur the development of more specialized techniques for verifying medical content generated by LLMs and may lead to improvements in LLM safety in medical applications. However, it is to be noted that the overall approach relies on known methodologies and dataset creation techniques. The impact will also be based on the usage of the dataset by the research community.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7/10**.

*   The creation of the MEDFACT dataset is novel and valuable, addressing a crucial need in the age of LLMs. Its unique focus on Chinese medical content and LLM-generated responses makes it a significant contribution to the field.
*   The comprehensive experiments and error analysis provide valuable insights and future directions for research.
*   However, the novelty of individual components is limited. The exclusive focus on the Chinese language limits the generalizability of the dataset, and the model performance is only okay.
*   The paper's impact will depend on the community's adoption and utilization of the dataset.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### GeoPQA: Bridging the Visual Perception Gap in MLLMs for Geometric Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17437v1)
- **Authors**: Guizhen Chen, Weiwen Xu, Hao Zhang, Hou Pong Chan, Deli Zhao, Anh Tuan Luu, Yu Rong
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in reinforcement learning (RL) have enhanced the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs), yet the impact on multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) is limited. Particularly in vision-intensive tasks like geometric reasoning, MLLMs hallucinate frequently, leading to inaccurate reasoning. We attribute this to the perceptual bottleneck in MLLMs, which caps the benefits of reasoning training. To quantify this, we design a Geo-Perception Question-Answering (GeoPQA) benchmark, targeting basic geometric concepts and spatial relationships. Experiments on GeoPQA reveal significant shortcomings of MLLMs in visual perception, which constrain RL reward signals for effective training. To address this bottleneck, we propose a two-stage RL training framework by first enhancing the visual perception of geometric structures, then fostering reasoning capabilities. Applied to Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct, our two-stage training improves geometric reasoning by 9.7% and geometric problem solving by 9.1%, compared to the direct reasoning training approach. Our method also generalizes to other vision-intensive domains like figure understanding, highlighting the importance of perceptual grounding in effective MLLM reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GeoPQA: Bridging the Visual Perception Gap in MLLMs for Geometric Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of inaccurate geometric reasoning in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). It posits that a "perceptual bottleneck" – deficiencies in visual understanding of geometric concepts – limits the effectiveness of reinforcement learning (RL) training used to enhance reasoning. To quantify this, the authors create the Geo-Perception Question-Answering (GeoPQA) benchmark, focusing on basic geometric elements and spatial relationships. Experiments reveal significant shortcomings in MLLMs' visual perception. To address this, they propose a two-stage RL training framework: first, improving visual perception using GeoPQA, then fostering reasoning capabilities. Experiments on Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct show improved performance in geometric reasoning and problem-solving compared to direct reasoning training. The method also generalizes to other vision-intensive domains like figure understanding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *explicit* identification and quantification of the visual perception gap as a key bottleneck for geometric reasoning in MLLMs. While others have observed limitations in reasoning with geometric images, this paper directly tackles *why* these limitations persist. The creation of GeoPQA as a targeted benchmark to specifically assess geometric perception is a significant contribution, providing a tool for future research. The two-stage RL training framework, while not entirely revolutionary (staged training is common), is specifically tailored to address the identified perceptual bottleneck and demonstrates measurable improvements.
*   **Significance:**  The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the overall performance of MLLMs on tasks requiring visual understanding, not just in geometry. By highlighting the importance of foundational perceptual skills, the paper encourages a shift in focus from simply scaling models or employing more complex reasoning architectures towards ensuring a solid base of visual comprehension. The gains demonstrated on MathVista, as well as the broader generalization to other vision-intensive tasks, suggest a practical impact on real-world applications. It also provides a clear direction for future research: improving the "vision" component of vision-language models.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the visual perception bottleneck.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:**  The GeoPQA benchmark provides a quantitative assessment tool. The two-stage RL framework is well-designed and evaluated.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  Experiments show statistically significant improvements on standard benchmarks.
    *   **Generalization:** Demonstration of improved performance beyond geometry suggests broader applicability.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Judge Reliance:** Stage 1 training depends on GPT-4o-mini to parse free-form MLLM responses, which incurs extra expense and might introduce unintended biases.
    *   **Model Specificity:** The focus on Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct models limits broad generalizability. While the concept should be applicable, the magnitude of improvement may vary with different architectures.
    *   **Synthetic data issues**: While the method is tested on both real world and synthetic data, synthetic data might have its own biases that will impact the model in a negative way.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution by identifying, quantifying, and addressing a key limitation in MLLMs for geometric reasoning. While the core idea of staged training isn't entirely new, its application to address a specifically defined *visual perception* problem in MLLMs, along with the creation of a targeted benchmark, makes the contribution valuable. The empirical results strongly support the claim that improving visual perception is essential for enhancing geometric reasoning abilities. The paper is well-written, clearly organized, and provides a solid foundation for future research in this area. However, the issues with external dependency (judge LLM) and the model specificity bring the final score down.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### WildClaims: Information Access Conversations in the Wild(Chat)
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17442v1)
- **Authors**: Hideaki Joko, Shakiba Amirshahi, Charles L. A. Clarke, Faegheh Hasibi
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs) has transformed conversational systems into practical tools used by millions. However, the nature and necessity of information retrieval in real-world conversations remain largely unexplored, as research has focused predominantly on traditional, explicit information access conversations. The central question is: What do real-world information access conversations look like? To this end, we first conduct an observational study on the WildChat dataset, large-scale user-ChatGPT conversations, finding that users' access to information occurs implicitly as check-worthy factual assertions made by the system, even when the conversation's primary intent is non-informational, such as creative writing. To enable the systematic study of this phenomenon, we release the WildClaims dataset, a novel resource consisting of 121,905 extracted factual claims from 7,587 utterances in 3,000 WildChat conversations, each annotated for check-worthiness. Our preliminary analysis of this resource reveals that conservatively 18% to 51% of conversations contain check-worthy assertions, depending on the methods employed, and less conservatively, as many as 76% may contain such assertions. This high prevalence underscores the importance of moving beyond the traditional understanding of explicit information access, to address the implicit information access that arises in real-world user-system conversations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "WILDCLAIMS: Information Access Conversations in the Wild(Chat)" introduces a novel perspective on information access in real-world user-system conversations, particularly within the context of Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT. It argues that information access often occurs *implicitly* through check-worthy factual assertions made by the system, even when the user's primary intent isn't explicitly information-seeking (e.g., creative writing, editing).  The authors present the WILDCLAIMS dataset, a collection of 121,905 factual claims extracted from 7,587 utterances across 3,000 user-ChatGPT conversations, each annotated for check-worthiness. Through analysis of this dataset, the paper estimates that a significant proportion of conversations contain these implicit information access points, highlighting the limitations of existing definitions of conversational information access. It proposes a refined definition that incorporates the concept of information needs being satisfied by exchanged, verified information, regardless of the user's explicit intent.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its shift in focus from explicit information-seeking dialogues to the more subtle realm of *implicit* information access.  The observation that LLMs make factual claims even in seemingly non-informational conversations, and that these claims may require verification, is a valuable and relatively unexplored angle. Existing conversational information access research has largely concentrated on explicit queries and question-answering scenarios. The introduction of the WILDCLAIMS dataset is also novel and valuable, providing a resource to study this implicit information access. However, the concept of "check-worthiness" isn't entirely new, as prior work has addressed fact-checking and claim verification, but applying it to this conversational context *is* a fresh perspective. The refinement of the definition of information access is incremental.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its potential to reshape how we understand, design, and evaluate conversational AI systems. By highlighting the prevalence of implicit information access, the paper calls for more comprehensive models that account for the need for fact verification and reliable knowledge transfer across a wider range of conversational tasks. The WILDCLAIMS dataset facilitates research into automatic fact verification within conversations, and in user modeling to detect when users may be acting on misinformation without realizing it. Understanding this dynamic could lead to better systems that proactively surface potential inaccuracies or provide supporting evidence. The insights gained are particularly relevant in the era of LLMs, which are prone to generating plausible but incorrect information (hallucinations).

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Basis:**  The WILDCLAIMS dataset provides a concrete foundation for the paper's claims, moving beyond anecdotal observations.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing definitions of conversational information access and proposes a well-defined alternative.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The analysis is thorough, considering different methods for factual claim extraction and check-worthiness classification.
    *   **Reproducibility:**  The authors make their dataset and methods publicly available, promoting reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Limitations:** The dataset, while significant, is based on user-ChatGPT conversations, which might not fully generalize to other types of human-computer interaction. The types of tasks users perform with ChatGPT (e.g., creative writing) may be overrepresented compared to other conversational scenarios.
    *   **Automatic Annotation Challenges:** While automatic methods are used for check-worthiness annotation, the paper acknowledges their limitations and relies on manual validation for a subset of the data. This introduces potential bias, and a more rigorous validation approach would be beneficial.
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** The analysis, while comprehensive, mainly focuses on prevalence estimation. Further investigation into the *nature* of the check-worthy claims (e.g., their complexity, source, or impact on user behavior) would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Refined Definition:** While helpful, the paper's refined definition of information access conversation could be viewed as incremental.
    *   **Novelty limitations:** While the study adds to the field, it also leverages many tools that were previously in existence, therefore there isn't a particularly novel approach for data analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in conversational AI, information retrieval, and fact-checking.  It could spur the development of new evaluation metrics for conversational systems that incorporate factuality and reliability, as well as new user models that account for implicit information needs and susceptibility to misinformation.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable shift in perspective on information access in conversational systems, supported by empirical evidence and a new dataset. It tackles a problem of growing importance in the age of LLMs and has the potential to influence future research directions. The WILDCLAIMS dataset stands out as a substantial contribution. The weaknesses primarily relate to the limitations of the dataset and the depth of analysis in certain areas. The novelty is above average, but limited by the existing claim detection methods. Considering these factors, a score of 7 reflects the paper's significant contributions, potential impact, and areas for improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Semantic Reformulation Entropy for Robust Hallucination Detection in QA Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17445v1)
- **Authors**: Chaodong Tong, Qi Zhang, Lei Jiang, Yanbing Liu, Nannan Sun, Wei Li
- **Abstract**: Reliable question answering with large language models (LLMs) is challenged by hallucinations, fluent but factually incorrect outputs arising from epistemic uncertainty. Existing entropy-based semantic-level uncertainty estimation methods are limited by sampling noise and unstable clustering of variable-length answers. We propose Semantic Reformulation Entropy (SRE), which improves uncertainty estimation in two ways. First, input-side semantic reformulations produce faithful paraphrases, expand the estimation space, and reduce biases from superficial decoder tendencies. Second, progressive, energy-based hybrid clustering stabilizes semantic grouping. Experiments on SQuAD and TriviaQA show that SRE outperforms strong baselines, providing more robust and generalizable hallucination detection. These results demonstrate that combining input diversification with multi-signal clustering substantially enhances semantic-level uncertainty estimation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Semantic Reformulation Entropy (SRE) for robust hallucination detection in question answering tasks using large language models (LLMs).  SRE addresses limitations of existing semantic entropy methods by enhancing both the input and output sides of uncertainty estimation.  On the input side, it uses semantic reformulation to generate paraphrases, expanding the estimation space and reducing biases from superficial decoder tendencies. On the output side, it employs hybrid semantic clustering (HSC), which combines exact matches, embedding similarity, and bidirectional NLI via progressive, energy-based clustering, to stabilize semantic grouping.  Experiments on SQuAD and TriviaQA demonstrate that SRE outperforms strong baselines, providing more robust and generalizable hallucination detection. The key idea is that combining input diversification with multi-signal clustering substantially improves semantic-level uncertainty estimation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of two main ideas: input semantic reformulation and hybrid semantic clustering.  While semantic entropy for hallucination detection isn't entirely new, the systematic use of semantic reformulation *before* sampling and the progressive hybrid clustering approach offer a distinct contribution.  The combination isn't a trivial application of existing techniques; rather, it's a thoughtful integration designed to mitigate specific weaknesses of earlier methods. The energy-based refinement step in HSC is a novel component that helps stabilize clusters. The approach of progressively integrating diverse signals is a useful contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from the increasing need for reliable hallucination detection in LLMs. The proposed method addresses a critical gap in existing techniques, which often suffer from sampling biases and unstable clustering. If the claimed improvements hold up in broader applications, SRE could contribute significantly to building more trustworthy QA systems based on LLMs.  The reported gains on SQuAD and TriviaQA are encouraging, showing the practical relevance of the approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a real problem:** Hallucination detection is crucial for deploying reliable LLMs.
    *   **Sound methodology:** The combination of semantic reformulation and hybrid clustering is well-motivated and technically sound.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on standard datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of SRE.
    *   **Ablation studies:** Ablation studies effectively isolate the contributions of the different components of SRE (semantic reformulation and hybrid semantic clustering).
    *   **Parameter sensitivity analysis:** The paper includes a useful parameter sensitivity analysis to show the robustness of the proposed model
    *   **Distribution analysis:** The paper showcases that SRE is effective in differentiating hallucinated and faithful samples.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational cost:** The semantic reformulation and the NLI computations within HSC could be computationally expensive, potentially limiting the scalability of SRE for very large-scale applications. While Llama3 and Qwen are used, the paper does not detail runtime comparisons against baselines.
    *   **Dataset limitations:**  While SQuAD and TriviaQA are standard QA datasets, they may not fully represent the diversity of real-world QA scenarios, potentially affecting the generalizability of the results.
    *   **NLI dependency:** The hybrid clustering heavily relies on NLI. While NLI has improved significantly, it is still imperfect and may introduce errors into the clustering process.
    *   **Few-shot prompting dependency:** The semantic reformulation step depends on few-shot prompting. The quality and diversity of the few-shot examples can influence the diversity and quality of the paraphrases. The paper does not analyze how this process affects the quality of the overall performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in hallucination detection.  The idea of systematically diversifying inputs before uncertainty estimation could be adopted in other tasks. The hybrid clustering approach could also inspire more robust clustering methods for variable-length text data. The performance achieved on open-domain questions is particularly encouraging and could lead to increased adoption. The paper provides a practical, implementable approach that builds upon existing techniques in semantic entropy, which is a useful addition to the field.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper presents a valuable and well-executed approach to hallucination detection in QA. The combination of input reformulation and hybrid clustering addresses limitations of existing methods. The empirical results are promising and support the effectiveness of SRE. While computational cost and dataset limitations are concerns, the benefits of the approach outweigh the drawbacks.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**  The paper provides a novel and well-validated approach to hallucination detection. It addresses a significant problem with a clear methodology and strong empirical results. While it isn't a complete paradigm shift, it represents a substantial improvement over existing techniques and makes a valuable contribution to the field. The computational cost and dataset dependency slightly reduce the score from 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CARINOX: Inference-time Scaling with Category-Aware Reward-based Initial Noise Optimization and Exploration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17458v1)
- **Authors**: Seyed Amir Kasaei, Ali Aghayari, Arash Marioriyad, Niki Sepasian, Shayan Baghayi Nejad, MohammadAmin Fazli, Mahdieh Soleymani Baghshah, Mohammad Hossein Rohban
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image diffusion models, such as Stable Diffusion, can produce high-quality and diverse images but often fail to achieve compositional alignment, particularly when prompts describe complex object relationships, attributes, or spatial arrangements. Recent inference-time approaches address this by optimizing or exploring the initial noise under the guidance of reward functions that score text-image alignment without requiring model fine-tuning. While promising, each strategy has intrinsic limitations when used alone: optimization can stall due to poor initialization or unfavorable search trajectories, whereas exploration may require a prohibitively large number of samples to locate a satisfactory output. Our analysis further shows that neither single reward metrics nor ad-hoc combinations reliably capture all aspects of compositionality, leading to weak or inconsistent guidance. To overcome these challenges, we present Category-Aware Reward-based Initial Noise Optimization and Exploration (CARINOX), a unified framework that combines noise optimization and exploration with a principled reward selection procedure grounded in correlation with human judgments. Evaluations on two complementary benchmarks covering diverse compositional challenges show that CARINOX raises average alignment scores by +16% on T2I-CompBench++ and +11% on the HRS benchmark, consistently outperforming state-of-the-art optimization and exploration-based methods across all major categories, while preserving image quality and diversity. The project page is available at https://amirkasaei.com/carinox/{this URL}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CARINOX, a novel inference-time framework for improving compositional alignment in text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models. CARINOX addresses the limitations of existing approaches, which either rely on noise optimization (prone to stalling) or noise exploration (inefficient sampling), and often use poorly chosen reward functions. The framework combines noise optimization and exploration with a principled reward selection procedure based on correlations with human judgments.  CARINOX iteratively refines multiple noise samples while leveraging a robust reward function to guide the image generation process, resulting in improved compositional alignment and higher quality images. Experiments on T2I-CompBench++ and HRS benchmarks demonstrate that CARINOX consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods across diverse compositional categories, preserving image quality and diversity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *integration* of several existing ideas into a unified framework *and* in the *principled reward function selection*. Combining noise optimization and exploration isn't entirely new in the grand scheme of T2I research, but CARINOX offers a well-engineered and justified approach to the synergy.  The explicit data-driven selection of reward functions based on correlation with human judgement is a more significant contribution, as many previous approaches simply use off-the-shelf metrics (like CLIPScore) without questioning their suitability for compositional alignment. The per-reward gradient clipping and latent space regularization steps also add to the robustness of the optimization procedure.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a meaningful contribution to the field by addressing a critical limitation of T2I diffusion models: poor compositional understanding. By providing a framework that *reliably* improves alignment without requiring computationally expensive fine-tuning, it has practical value. Demonstrating strong improvements across multiple benchmarks and backbones solidifies this significance.  The ablation studies are crucial to understanding the contribution of each component, and the analysis of reward function correlations provides valuable insights for future research. The human evaluation lends significant weight to the claim that CARINOX improves alignment in a way that is actually perceived as better by humans, not just based on automated metrics.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem statement.
    *   Clear and thorough experimental evaluation on established benchmarks.
    *   Strong empirical results showing consistent improvements across different models and datasets.
    *   Careful ablation studies to isolate the contributions of different components.
    *   Human evaluation validating the improvements in perceived alignment quality.
    *   Principled reward function selection.
    *   Detailed analysis of the computational overhead.
    *   Well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on a one-step diffusion model may limit the framework's broader applicability to more advanced (multi-step) diffusion architectures.  The authors mention this as a future direction, but it is still a limitation.
    *   While the computational overhead is analyzed, the memory requirements, especially for the SD-Turbo version is quite high which is a potential adoption obstacle.  The high VRAM usage might restrict its accessibility to users with limited hardware resources.
    *   The reward function selection, while data-driven, relies on existing human annotations. Its effectiveness might be limited if the dataset is biased or doesn't adequately cover specific types of compositional challenges. The generalizability of the selected reward function set to out-of-distribution data or novel tasks could be a point of concern.
    *   The paper would benefit from discussing potential failure cases or limitations of the method more explicitly. While the results are strong, it is unlikely to solve all compositional problems, and discussing the types of prompts or scenarios where CARINOX might struggle would provide a more balanced view.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has a high potential to influence the field of T2I research. The demonstrated improvements in compositional alignment and the principled approach to reward function selection are likely to inspire future work in this area.  The analysis of reward function correlations could become a standard practice in evaluating T2I models. Other researchers might build upon this work to develop more sophisticated reward functions or explore alternative ways of combining noise optimization and exploration. Also, a lot of research focuses on fine-tuning the diffusion models to improve compositional alignment, but this method gives a good tradeoff to improve without training.

*Score: 8*

**Justification:**

CARINOX demonstrates a significant advancement in addressing the compositional alignment problem in text-to-image generation. The approach combines multiple existing ideas into a robust and effective framework while adding a strong focus on reward functions that are validated against human perception. The results are convincing, and the analysis provides valuable insights. The limitations related to the reliance on one-step diffusion models and high VRAM requirements prevent it from receiving a higher score, but the overall contribution is significant and likely to have a lasting impact on the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### PRINCIPLES: Synthetic Strategy Memory for Proactive Dialogue Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17459v1)
- **Authors**: Namyoung Kim, Kai Tzu-iunn Ong, Yeonjun Hwang, Minseok Kang, Iiseo Jihn, Gayoung Kim, Minju Kim, Jinyoung Yeo
- **Abstract**: Dialogue agents based on large language models (LLMs) have shown promising performance in proactive dialogue, which requires effective strategy planning. However, existing approaches to strategy planning for proactive dialogue face several limitations: limited strategy coverage, preference bias in planning, and reliance on costly additional training. To address these, we propose PRINCIPLES: a synthetic strategy memory for proactive dialogue agents. PRINCIPLES is derived through offline self-play simulations and serves as reusable knowledge that guides strategy planning during inference, eliminating the need for additional training and data annotation. We evaluate PRINCIPLES in both emotional support and persuasion domains, demonstrating consistent improvements over strong baselines. Furthermore, PRINCIPLES maintains its robustness across extended and more diverse evaluation settings. See our project page at https://huggingface.co/spaces/kimnamssya/Principles.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PRINCIPLES: Synthetic Strategy Memory for Proactive Dialogue Agents":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PRINCIPLES, a novel approach to strategy planning for proactive dialogue agents. The core idea is to create a synthetic strategy memory through offline self-play simulations. Inspired by human learning from both successes and failures, the method analyzes successful dialogue turns to identify positive principles and revises failed turns through backtracking and re-simulation to derive principles that capture both successes and failures. These PRINCIPLES, structured as "when [situation], you should [successful strategy] rather than [failed strategy] because [reason]", guide strategy selection during inference, eliminating the need for additional training or data annotation. The approach is evaluated across emotional support and persuasion domains, demonstrating consistent improvements over strong baselines. The paper also conducts comprehensive ablation studies and evaluations in extended and more diverse scenarios to assess the robustness of the proposed method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of leveraging self-play simulations to create a synthetic strategy memory is relatively novel. Prior work has explored self-play for dialogue policy learning, but this paper's focus on extracting and structuring PRINCIPLES as explicit, reusable knowledge is a distinctive contribution. The structured format ("when...should...rather than...because...") is also a key component that facilitates context-aware strategy selection and helps mitigate bias.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses several important limitations in existing strategy planning approaches, including limited strategy coverage, preference bias in planning, and reliance on costly additional training. By creating a synthetic strategy memory, PRINCIPLES expands strategy coverage without the need for extensive human-annotated data or model fine-tuning. The explicit contrast between effective and ineffective strategies helps to reduce bias and enhance the agent's ability to identify optimal strategies. The results demonstrate significant performance improvements over strong baselines, highlighting the potential of PRINCIPLES to advance the state-of-the-art in proactive dialogue agents.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation of PRINCIPLES across multiple datasets (ESConv, P4G, ExTES) and evaluation settings.
    *   **Detailed Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of different components of PRINCIPLES, such as structured representation, retrieval mechanisms, and the combination of successful and failed experiences.
    *   **Robustness:** The experiments show that PRINCIPLES maintains its effectiveness in more challenging scenarios (ExTES and P4G+), demonstrating its robustness and generalization ability.
    *   **Cost-Effectiveness:** The approach requires no additional training data or model fine-tuning, making it a cost-effective solution for improving strategy planning.
    *   **Human Evaluation:** The paper includes human evaluation to verify if gpt-4 achieves good quality feedback.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The approach relies heavily on the capabilities of LLMs for strategy generation, simulation, principle extraction, and reinterpretation. The quality of PRINCIPLES and the overall performance of the system depend on the performance of the underlying LLMs.
    *   **Scalability:** The self-play simulation process can be computationally expensive, especially for complex dialogue scenarios. It would be important to investigate more efficient methods for generating PRINCIPLES and scaling the approach to larger and more diverse dialogue settings.
    *   **Limited Expressiveness of PRINCIPLES:** While the "when...should...rather than...because..." format is effective for capturing basic strategy knowledge, it may not be sufficient to represent more complex or nuanced strategies. More expressive knowledge representation techniques may be needed to capture the full range of strategic possibilities.

**Overall:**

Despite some limitations, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field of proactive dialogue agents. The idea of synthetic strategy memory is novel and promising, and the experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of PRINCIPLES in improving strategy planning and reducing bias. The paper is well-written and provides a thorough evaluation of the proposed method.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### CSDformer: A Conversion Method for Fully Spike-Driven Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17461v1)
- **Authors**: Yuhao Zhang, Chengjun Zhang, Di Wu, Jie Yang, Mohamad Sawan
- **Abstract**: Spike-based transformer is a novel architecture aiming to enhance the performance of spiking neural networks while mitigating the energy overhead inherent to transformers. However, methods for generating these models suffer from critical limitations: excessive training costs introduced by direct training methods, or unavoidably hardware-unfriendly operations in existing conversion methods. In this paper, we propose CSDformer, a novel conversion method for fully spike-driven transformers. We tailor a conversion-oriented transformer-based architecture and propose a new function NReLU to replace softmax in self-attention. Subsequently, this model is quantized and trained, and converted into a fully spike-driven model with temporal decomposition technique. Also, we propose delayed Integrate-andFire neurons to reduce conversion errors and improve the performance of spiking models. We evaluate CSDformer on ImageNet, CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 datasets and achieve 76.36% top-1 accuracy under 7 time-steps on ImageNet, demonstrating superiority over state-of-the-art models. Furthermore, CSDformer eliminates the need for training SNNs, thereby reducing training costs (reducing computational resource by 75% and accelerating training speed by 2-3$\times$). To the best of our knowledge, this is the first fully spike-driven transformer-based model developed via conversion method, achieving high performance under ultra-low latency, while dramatically reducing both computational complexity and training overhead.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the CSDformer paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CSDformer, a novel conversion method designed to generate fully spike-driven transformers. This approach aims to overcome the limitations of direct training (high computational cost) and existing conversion methods (hardware-unfriendly operations like softmax and layer normalization). CSDformer proposes a conversion-oriented transformer architecture, replacing softmax with a new "NReLU" function and employing a temporal decomposition technique along with a delayed Integrate-and-Fire (DIF) neuron model. The authors demonstrate CSDformer's performance on ImageNet, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100 datasets, achieving competitive accuracy with reduced training costs and hardware-friendly operations. The method claims to reduce computational resource by 75% and accelerate training by 2-3x compared to direct training approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The main novelty lies in the comprehensive approach to converting standard transformers into fully spike-driven ones. The individual components (NReLU, temporal decomposition, DIF neurons) aren't entirely new in isolation. However, their integration within a conversion-based training strategy to achieve a fully spiking transformer *without* hardware-unfriendly operations is noteworthy.  The use of LSQ for quantization-aware training is a standard technique, but its application to the specific architecture and conversion process is relevant.
*   **Significance:** CSDformer addresses a critical challenge in the field of spiking neural networks: bridging the gap between high-performing transformer architectures and energy-efficient spike-based computation.  The elimination of softmax and layer normalization is a significant step towards deploying spiking transformers on neuromorphic hardware.  The demonstrated performance, particularly with low latency, is promising. The claim of reducing training costs is important, making spike-based transformers more accessible. However, the results section could provide more details and thoroughness to the performance, making it unclear if CSDformer is better than SOTA spike-based approaches.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured methodology.
    *   Emphasis on hardware-friendly operations, which is essential for realizing the energy efficiency benefits of SNNs.
    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating competitive accuracy and reduced training cost.
    *   The conversion-based approach saves significant resources.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The individual components are not completely novel, it's mostly an integrative effort.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the NReLU function, how it compares to other softmax approximations.
    *   The gains may not justify the level of complexity introduced with the new components in comparison to standard architectures.
    *   Limited discussion on the practical challenges of deploying the resulting models on real neuromorphic hardware.
    *   Although comparisons are given, it would be helpful if they were more rigorous.

**Justification of Score:**

CSDformer is a solid contribution to the field of spike-based transformers. The approach offers a practical pathway for creating efficient spiking models, reducing reliance on computationally intensive training or hardware-unfriendly operations. While the individual components are not revolutionary, their thoughtful integration and the resulting performance improvements warrant a positive assessment. The potential to reduce training costs and enable deployment on specialized hardware gives this work a tangible impact.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Stable Video-Driven Portraits
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17476v1)
- **Authors**: Mallikarjun B. R., Fei Yin, Vikram Voleti, Nikita Drobyshev, Maksim Lapin, Aaryaman Vasishta, Varun Jampani
- **Abstract**: Portrait animation aims to generate photo-realistic videos from a single source image by reenacting the expression and pose from a driving video. While early methods relied on 3D morphable models or feature warping techniques, they often suffered from limited expressivity, temporal inconsistency, and poor generalization to unseen identities or large pose variations. Recent advances using diffusion models have demonstrated improved quality but remain constrained by weak control signals and architectural limitations. In this work, we propose a novel diffusion based framework that leverages masked facial regions specifically the eyes, nose, and mouth from the driving video as strong motion control cues. To enable robust training without appearance leakage, we adopt cross identity supervision. To leverage the strong prior from the pretrained diffusion model, our novel architecture introduces minimal new parameters that converge faster and help in better generalization. We introduce spatial temporal attention mechanisms that allow inter frame and intra frame interactions, effectively capturing subtle motions and reducing temporal artifacts. Our model uses history frames to ensure continuity across segments. At inference, we propose a novel signal fusion strategy that balances motion fidelity with identity preservation. Our approach achieves superior temporal consistency and accurate expression control, enabling high-quality, controllable portrait animation suitable for real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new diffusion-based framework for high-quality portrait animation from a single source image and a driving video.  The core ideas involve: (1) using masked facial regions (eyes, nose, mouth) from the driving video as strong motion control cues; (2) employing cross-identity supervision during training to avoid appearance leakage and encourage better generalization; (3) incorporating minimal new parameters into a pre-trained Diffusion Transformer (DiT) model to leverage its existing prior knowledge; (4) using spatial-temporal attention mechanisms for improved temporal consistency; and (5) a novel inference strategy to balance motion fidelity with identity preservation.  The authors demonstrate through qualitative and quantitative experiments that their approach outperforms existing methods in generating temporally smooth, accurate, and high-quality portrait animations, including superior lip synchronization and generalization capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the paper builds upon existing ideas in portrait animation and diffusion models, it combines them in a novel way that addresses several limitations of previous methods.
    *   The use of masked facial regions as direct control signals is not entirely new (X-Portrait), but the paper argues they utilize them within a DiT architecture, minimal new parameters, and a refined fusion and inference strategy which constitutes incremental novelty.
    *   Cross-identity supervision is also not completely new (LivePortrait), but the combination with masked facial regions for driving and the DiT architecture, combined with spatiotemporal attention offers innovation.
    *   The architectural choices, specifically the minimal modifications to the pre-trained DiT and the addition of spatiotemporal attention, are significant contributions. The minimal changes to the base network for adaptation to new tasks and datasets are valuable in terms of efficient and practical usage.
    *   The inference strategy combining multiple configurations based on dropped control signals seems to be a unique contribution and a smart way to balance identity and motion.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a relevant and challenging problem with significant practical applications (telepresence, VR, entertainment). The improvements in temporal consistency and generalization, especially to stylized images, are valuable contributions. The quantitative results clearly demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on established benchmarks. The code isn't released with the paper.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results with comprehensive qualitative and quantitative comparisons against multiple baselines.
    *   Well-motivated technical choices and clear explanations of the method.
    *   The efficient adaptation of a pre-trained DiT model with minimal parameter overhead is a significant advantage.
    *   Addresses a clear set of limitations of prior work, notably temporal inconsistency and generalization.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on a large, internally collected dataset might hinder reproducibility and fair comparison with other methods if data not publicly availaible.
    *   Some components, like the basic idea of masking the face and cross-identity supervision, were borrowed from other works, but are enhanced by their method.
    *   The limitations are clearly stated, though further exploration into handling extreme pose/proportional deviations is warranted. A discussion of parameters versus performance for other methods could improve the paper.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in portrait animation and related areas like video synthesis and editing. The approach of minimally adapting powerful pre-trained models and using strong, localized control signals could be a generalizable strategy for other conditional image/video generation tasks. The focus on temporal consistency is essential for realistic video generation.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:** The paper offers a good blend of incremental novelty, strong empirical results, and clear technical contributions. The method represents a significant advancement in portrait animation, especially considering the efficient use of a pre-trained DiT model. However, the individual components are not revolutionary, and some build directly on existing work. The impact is therefore solid, though not fundamentally paradigm-shifting. The lack of public datasets for training or code will also limit adoption and reproducibility in the near term.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### AttnComp: Attention-Guided Adaptive Context Compression for Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17486v1)
- **Authors**: Lvzhou Luo, Yixuan Cao, Ping Luo
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-augmented generation improves the factual accuracy of Large Language Models (LLMs) by incorporating external context, but often suffers from irrelevant retrieved content that hinders effectiveness. Context compression addresses this issue by filtering out irrelevant information from context before LLM generation. However, existing methods struggle to adaptively adjust compression rates for different context, maintain low latency and integrate information across multiple documents. To overcome these limitations, We introduce AttnComp, an adaptive, efficient and context-aware compression framework. By leveraging the attention mechanism of LLMs to identify relevant information, AttnComp employs a Top-P compression algorithm to retain the minimal set of documents whose cumulative attention weights exceeds a predefined threshold. In addition to compression, AttnComp estimates response confidence by assessing the overall relevance of the retrieved content, enabling users to gauge response reliability. Experiments demonstrate that AttnComp outperforms existing compression methods and uncompressed baselines, achieving higher accuracy with substantial compression rates and lower latency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AttnComp, a novel attention-guided adaptive context compression framework for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). AttnComp leverages the inherent attention mechanisms within large language models (LLMs) to identify and filter out irrelevant information from the retrieved context before generation. It uses a Top-P compression algorithm based on the cumulative attention weights assigned to different documents. The framework is designed to be adaptive, efficient, and context-aware.  Furthermore, AttnComp provides a method for estimating the confidence of RAG responses based on attention scores assigned to the instruction itself. Experiments on various QA datasets demonstrate that AttnComp outperforms existing compression methods and uncompressed baselines in terms of accuracy, compression rate, and end-to-end latency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty by introducing an attention-guided approach to context compression that integrates with the inherent functionality of LLMs. Most existing methods rely on summarization or fixed-length extraction, and AttnComp distinguishes itself by adaptively adjusting compression based on attention weights and providing a confidence score. Fine-tuning cross-attention layers is a sensible way to boost performance. However, the core concept of using attention weights for relevance filtering is not entirely new. Several other papers leverage attention for similar purposes, though not always in the same context of context compression.
*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it addresses a critical challenge in practical RAG applications: the presence of irrelevant retrieved context. The results indicate substantial improvements in accuracy, compression, and latency compared to existing approaches. The introduction of a confidence estimation capability is a valuable addition, allowing users to assess the reliability of RAG responses. The study's focus on multi-hop QA highlights its applicability to complex knowledge-intensive tasks. The 17x compression rate is substantial, and the 49% latency reduction is a practical benefit. However, the long contexts and large numbers of input documents are not always encountered in practice.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Adaptive compression based on attention mechanisms.
    *   Incorporates a confidence estimation capability.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on multiple QA datasets.
    *   Demonstrates superior performance in terms of accuracy, compression rate, and latency.
    *   Addresses a practical challenge in RAG applications.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on attention for relevance is not entirely novel, although its implementation and integration are.
    *   Experiments are limited to LLMs up to 8 billion parameters; results on larger models are needed.
    *   Although they touch on robustness, bias and fairness issues are only briefly mentioned.
    *   Some of the experimental setups might not directly translate to real-world RAG implementations.
    *   Automated annotation can lead to issues if LLMs hallucinate, even if they use the proposed LLM as well.

*   **Potential Influence:** AttnComp has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and reliable RAG systems. The adaptive compression and confidence estimation capabilities could be valuable for various knowledge-intensive applications. The findings may stimulate further research on attention-guided techniques and confidence estimation in LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the paper's strengths and weaknesses, it warrants a score of 7. While the underlying concept of using attention for relevance filtering is not entirely new, AttnComp introduces a well-designed framework that integrates effectively with LLMs. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating significant improvements compared to existing methods. The confidence estimation capability is a valuable addition. However, the limitations regarding model size, annotation, and practical considerations reduce the overall score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MapCoder-Lite: Squeezing Multi-Agent Coding into a Single Small LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17489v1)
- **Authors**: Woongkyu Lee, Junhee Cho, Jungwook Choi
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have advanced code generation from single-function tasks to competitive-programming problems, but existing multi-agent solutions either rely on costly large-scale ($>$ 30B) models or collapse when downsized to small open-source models. We present MapCoder-Lite, which upgrades a single 7B model into four role-specialised agents-retriever, planner, coder, and debugger-using only rank-32, role-specific LoRA adapters ($<3\%$ extra parameters). Three lightweight techniques make this possible: (i) trajectory distillation from strong LLMs fixes format fragility in retrieval and debugging, (ii) supervisor-guided correction strengthens planning and coding agents, and (iii) agent-wise LoRA fine-tuning delivers memory-efficient specialisation. Comprehensive evaluation on xCodeEval, APPS, and CodeContests shows that MapCoder-Lite more than doubles xCodeEval accuracy (from $13.2\%$ to $28.3\%$), eliminates all format failures, and closes to within six points of a 32B baseline while cutting GPU memory and token-generation time by $4\times$. These results demonstrate that careful agent-wise fine-tuning unleashes high-quality multi-agent coding on a small language model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MapCoder-Lite: Squeezing Multi-Agent Coding into a Single Small LLM":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MapCoder-Lite, a novel multi-agent code generation framework that operates within the constraints of a single, relatively small (7B parameter) language model. MapCoder-Lite cleverly transforms this single LLM into a system of four specialized agents – retriever, planner, coder, and debugger – using low-rank adaptation (LoRA) adapters. To overcome the challenges of reduced model size, the authors introduce three key techniques: trajectory distillation (using outputs from stronger LLMs to guide smaller models), supervisor-aided cross-agent refinement (using a larger model to provide feedback and correct errors in the smaller agents' outputs), and memory-efficient LoRA specialization. The paper demonstrates that MapCoder-Lite achieves significantly improved code generation accuracy, format adherence, and computational efficiency compared to a similarly sized baseline, approaching the performance of much larger models (32B) while significantly reducing GPU memory and token generation time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty in multiple dimensions. The core idea of squeezing a multi-agent system into a small LLM is not entirely new, however, their specific combination of techniques -- trajectory distillation *combined* with supervisor-aided refinement *and* memory-efficient LoRA for *code generation*, represents a significant contribution. The way they've managed to integrate all of these aspects in a *single* 7B architecture is a clever engineering achievement. Previous works often focus on single agent improvements or require extensive human intervention or are solely focussed on single stage/monolithic code generation. This represents a valuable step forward.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to democratize access to high-quality code generation. The vast computational resources required by current state-of-the-art models limit their accessibility. MapCoder-Lite provides a viable pathway for researchers and developers with limited resources to leverage the benefits of multi-agent code generation. The reduction in GPU memory and token generation time makes the approach practical for real-world applications. Furthermore, the work paves the way for further research into efficient fine-tuning and specialization techniques for smaller LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation of MapCoder-Lite on multiple code generation benchmarks (xCodeEval, APPS, CodeContests) and compares it against various baselines, including different model sizes and prompting techniques.
    *   **Clear Explanation of Techniques:** The paper clearly describes the proposed techniques (trajectory distillation, supervisor-aided refinement, LoRA specialization) and provides a well-reasoned justification for their effectiveness.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies demonstrate the individual contributions of each component of MapCoder-Lite, providing valuable insights into the design choices.
    *   **Case Studies:** The inclusion of case studies helps to illustrate the practical benefits of fine-tuning and demonstrates how the model can address challenges.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Strong LLMs for Distillation and Supervision:** While the paper reduces overall resource usage, the system still depends on larger LLMs for trajectory distillation and supervisor guidance, which can be a limitation depending on use case.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Agent Interaction:** The multi-agent framework is relatively pipeline-based. The paper could benefit from exploring more dynamic agent interactions and feedback loops.
    *   **Generalization Beyond Specific Benchmarks:** While the benchmarks used are established, further evaluation on a wider range of coding tasks and real-world software development scenarios would strengthen the paper's claims about generalizability.
    *   **Potential for Scaling Issues:** While the authors successfully scaled to a few agents, what is the cost to add additional agents to the current 7B architecture without losing performance. Is there a maximum?

*   **Potential Impact:** This paper has strong potential to influence the field. The success of squeezing multi-agent system into a small LLM is inspiring. Other researchers may adapt this strategy to other problems.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid and novel contribution to the field of code generation. The novel integration of trajectory distillation, supervision, and LoRA, along with comprehensive evaluation, are clear strengths. It significantly reduces the resource requirements for multi-agent code generation. While the reliance on larger models for distillation and supervision and the limited exploration of agent interactions constitute weaknesses, the overall impact and potential for future research are substantial. The limitations present opportunities for further work. The rigorous demonstration of improvement over established baselines and thorough ablation studies support the assigned score. The successful approach enables more accessibility to high-quality code generation through lower cost.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Enhancing Cross-Lingual Transfer through Reversible Transliteration: A Huffman-Based Approach for Low-Resource Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17493v1)
- **Authors**: Wenhao Zhuang, Yuan Sun, Xiaobing Zhao
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are trained on increasingly diverse and extensive multilingual corpora, they demonstrate cross-lingual transfer capabilities. However, these capabilities often fail to effectively extend to low-resource languages, particularly those utilizing non-Latin scripts. While transliterating low-resource languages into Latin script presents a natural solution, there currently lacks a comprehensive framework for integrating transliteration into LLMs training and deployment. Taking a pragmatic approach, this paper innovatively combines character transliteration with Huffman coding to design a complete transliteration framework. Our proposed framework offers the following advantages: 1) Compression: Reduces storage requirements for low-resource language content, achieving up to 50% reduction in file size and 50-80% reduction in token count. 2) Accuracy: Guarantees 100% lossless conversion from transliterated text back to the source language. 3) Efficiency: Eliminates the need for vocabulary expansion for low-resource languages, improving training and inference efficiency. 4) Scalability: The framework can be extended to other low-resource languages. We validate the effectiveness of our framework across multiple downstream tasks, including text classification, machine reading comprehension, and machine translation. Experimental results demonstrate that our method significantly enhances the model's capability to process low-resource languages while maintaining performance on high-resource languages. Our data and code are publicly available at https://github.com/CMLI-NLP/HuffmanTranslit.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel Huffman-based transliteration framework for improving cross-lingual transfer in low-resource languages (Tibetan, Mongolian, Uyghur). The framework combines character transliteration with Huffman coding, offering advantages such as compression, accuracy (reversible transliteration), efficiency (no vocabulary expansion), and scalability. The method involves analyzing character frequencies, designing custom encodings, training on transliterated corpora, and using an end-to-end processing pipeline that includes language classification. The authors validate their approach across multiple downstream tasks like text classification, machine reading comprehension, and machine translation. The core idea is to overcome limitations associated with vocabulary expansion in LLMs and the curse of multilinguality by using a reversible and compressive transliteration method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining Huffman coding principles with transliteration for low-resource languages is potentially novel. The reversible nature is a definite advantage over previous transliteration techniques, which often sacrificed reversibility for simplicity. The detailed engineering to fit this into LLM workflows (tokenizer awareness, vocabulary augmentation strategy) is also a contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a key bottleneck in using LLMs for low-resource languages: how to efficiently and effectively represent non-Latin scripts without exploding vocabulary sizes and compromising performance. If proven robust, the framework offers a scalable and practical solution. The performance improvements on benchmark tasks, while not revolutionary, do demonstrate the method's potential.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper addresses a relevant and important problem in multilingual NLP.
    *   The Huffman coding approach is theoretically sound and provides several advantages in terms of compression and reversibility.
    *   The experimental setup is reasonably comprehensive, covering different downstream tasks and comparing against relevant baselines.
    *   The end-to-end framework makes the approach practical for real-world deployment.
    *   The code and data being publicly available are a strong positive for reproducibility and future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The novelty, while present, is somewhat incremental.  Transliteration and Huffman coding are established techniques; the contribution is in the specific *combination* and adaptation for the low-resource LLM setting. The design space of such combination is not explored/evaluated in detail.
    *   The "untapped potential" section feels somewhat speculative.  The authors suggest better encoding strategies could further improve results, but don't provide concrete examples or evidence to support this.
    *   The gains achieved, while consistent, are not dramatically large. This makes it more difficult to definitively conclude that the method is *significantly* better than existing techniques.
    *   The experiments are somewhat limited in scope. It is not validated on languages beyond Chinese, Uyghur, Tibetan, and Mongolian. The lack of a more diverse set of low-resource language families makes it hard to judge the generalizability of the approach.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the limitations, especially concerning the computational overhead of the transliteration/restoration processes.

*   **Potential Influence:** The work can influence future research in several ways:
    *   It provides a practical framework for integrating transliteration into LLM pipelines.
    *   It demonstrates the potential of combining lossless data compression techniques with transliteration to improve cross-lingual transfer.
    *   It highlights the importance of tokenizer awareness in designing transliteration methods.
    *   It could inspire the development of more sophisticated encoding strategies for low-resource languages, potentially incorporating linguistic features beyond simple character frequencies.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the idea is interesting and the experimental results are positive, the novelty isn't groundbreaking and the gains aren't substantial enough to warrant a very high score. A score of 6 reflects the good combination of existing knowledge and the lack of significant results which are somewhat overshadowed by a fairly solid methodology. Further work could build on this to achieve more significant results, better justifying a greater score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Chat-CBM: Towards Interactive Concept Bottleneck Models with Frozen Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17522v1)
- **Authors**: Hangzhou He, Lei Zhu, Kaiwen Li, Xinliang Zhang, Jiakui Hu, Ourui Fu, Zhengjian Yao, Yanye Lu
- **Abstract**: Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) provide inherent interpretability by first predicting a set of human-understandable concepts and then mapping them to labels through a simple classifier. While users can intervene in the concept space to improve predictions, traditional CBMs typically employ a fixed linear classifier over concept scores, which restricts interventions to manual value adjustments and prevents the incorporation of new concepts or domain knowledge at test time. These limitations are particularly severe in unsupervised CBMs, where concept activations are often noisy and densely activated, making user interventions ineffective. We introduce Chat-CBM, which replaces score-based classifiers with a language-based classifier that reasons directly over concept semantics. By grounding prediction in the semantic space of concepts, Chat-CBM preserves the interpretability of CBMs while enabling richer and more intuitive interventions, such as concept correction, addition or removal of concepts, incorporation of external knowledge, and high-level reasoning guidance. Leveraging the language understanding and few-shot capabilities of frozen large language models, Chat-CBM extends the intervention interface of CBMs beyond numerical editing and remains effective even in unsupervised settings. Experiments on nine datasets demonstrate that Chat-CBM achieves higher predictive performance and substantially improves user interactivity while maintaining the concept-based interpretability of CBMs.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Chat-CBM, a novel approach to Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) that replaces the traditional score-based classifier with a language-based classifier driven by frozen large language models (LLMs). By grounding prediction in concept semantics, Chat-CBM enables richer and more intuitive user interventions beyond simple numerical adjustments. These interventions include concept correction, addition, removal, incorporation of external knowledge, and high-level reasoning guidance. Experiments across nine datasets demonstrate that Chat-CBM achieves higher predictive performance, improves user interactivity, and maintains concept-based interpretability, even in unsupervised settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in substituting the numerical classifier of CBMs with an LLM that reasons directly on concept semantics. This shift allows for a dramatically expanded intervention interface. While other works have explored richer concept representations or intervention mechanisms, this approach addresses the fundamental limitation of score-based classifiers in CBMs, especially in unsupervised settings where concept activations can be noisy and less interpretable. Existing work on incorporating LLMs into CBMs have often focused on generating concepts, or on transforming a LLM to CBM architecture. In contrast, this paper focuses on using a frozen LLM for the prediction part of the CBM, allowing for more flexible intervention.

**Significance:** The significance stems from overcoming key limitations of traditional CBMs, particularly in interactive and unsupervised settings. By enabling richer interventions, Chat-CBM moves beyond simple score adjustments, making CBMs more practical for real-world applications where users need to incorporate domain knowledge or correct model errors. The ability to effectively intervene in unsupervised CBMs is also significant, as it reduces the reliance on expensive concept annotations. Furthermore, the performance improvements, while not always drastic compared to state-of-the-art *supervised* CBMs, are notable considering the use of frozen LLMs and the increased interactivity they afford. This represents a beneficial trade-off in settings where interpretability and control are paramount. The paper demonstrates that language-based classifiers can improve the utility and applicability of CBMs.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of score-based classifiers in CBMs and proposes a concrete solution.
*   **Significant technical contribution:** The replacement of the classifier with a language-based model and the exploration of various intervention types represent a significant technical contribution.
*   **Comprehensive experiments:** The experiments cover a wide range of datasets, including both supervised and unsupervised settings, demonstrating the effectiveness of Chat-CBM.
*   **Detailed analysis and ablation studies:** The paper provides a thorough analysis of the results, including ablation studies that validate the design choices.
*   **Practical benefits:** The proposed method offers practical benefits in terms of increased interactivity and reduced reliance on concept annotations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational cost:** Using LLMs as classifiers can be computationally expensive, limiting scalability and real-time performance. The paper acknowledges this, but further discussion of potential optimizations would be beneficial.
*   **Reliance on LLM capabilities:** The performance of Chat-CBM depends heavily on the capabilities of the underlying LLM. The sensitivity to prompt engineering and the potential for LLMs to exhibit biases or generate nonsensical outputs are potential limitations. This dependence is not thoroughly addressed in the analysis of limitations.
*   **Marginal Performance gains:**  Performance gains are significant for unsupervised CBMs, however, performance gains in supervised CBMs are marginal and primarily attributed to improved interventions.
*   **Limited novel ablation/analysis**: the paper could perform ablation on prompting design or LLM fine-tuning methods to justify the freezing of the LLM.
*   **Generalization over concepts:** While the study demonstrates better accuracy when intervention on *existing* concepts, it does not discuss the case where *completely new* concepts outside the pre-defined set are needed for optimal prediction.

**Potential Influence:**

Chat-CBM has the potential to influence the development of more interactive and user-friendly interpretable models. The language-based classification approach could be adopted in other areas of interpretable machine learning, not just CBMs. The work also highlights the potential of LLMs for improving the usability and accessibility of AI systems.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the identified weaknesses, the paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of interpretable machine learning. The shift from score-based to language-based classification in CBMs opens up new possibilities for user interaction and improves the usability of these models, especially in unsupervised settings. The experimental results provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The computational cost and reliance on LLM capabilities are valid concerns, but the benefits in terms of interactivity and interpretability justify a high score. While there are limitations, this paper provides a compelling proof-of-concept that language can meaningfully augment the capabilities of inherently interpretable models such as CBMs.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Multimodal Conversational Assistant for the Characterization of Agricultural Plots from Geospatial Open Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17544v1)
- **Authors**: Juan Cañada, Raúl Alonso, Julio Molleda, Fidel Díez
- **Abstract**: The increasing availability of open Earth Observation (EO) and agricultural datasets holds great potential for supporting sustainable land management. However, their high technical entry barrier limits accessibility for non-expert users. This study presents an open-source conversational assistant that integrates multimodal retrieval and large language models (LLMs) to enable natural language interaction with heterogeneous agricultural and geospatial data. The proposed architecture combines orthophotos, Sentinel-2 vegetation indices, and user-provided documents through retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), allowing the system to flexibly determine whether to rely on multimodal evidence, textual knowledge, or both in formulating an answer. To assess response quality, we adopt an LLM-as-a-judge methodology using Qwen3-32B in a zero-shot, unsupervised setting, applying direct scoring in a multi-dimensional quantitative evaluation framework. Preliminary results show that the system is capable of generating clear, relevant, and context-aware responses to agricultural queries, while remaining reproducible and scalable across geographic regions. The primary contributions of this work include an architecture for fusing multimodal EO and textual knowledge sources, a demonstration of lowering the barrier to access specialized agricultural information through natural language interaction, and an open and reproducible design.
