The paper introduces PG-CE (Progressive Generation with Constraint Enhancement), a new method for controllable text generation (CTG). PG-CE decomposes the CTG task into three steps: type prediction, constraint construction, and guided generation. It utilizes constraint generation models to build multi-dimensional constraints dynamically (covering tone, style, and thematic focus) to guide output. The paper also introduces a new dataset of 90,000 constraint-text pairs. Experiments demonstrate that PG-CE improves generation quality while maintaining controllability, thematic relevance, and practicality. The authors performed toxicity reduction and readability-controlled summarization experiments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a well-structured approach by breaking down the controllable text generation problem into type prediction, constraint construction, and guided generation. While constraint-based approaches are not entirely new, the progressive nature and focus on adaptability based on scenario are potentially valuable contributions. The paper's novelty is further strengthened by the introduction of a new dataset explicitly designed for constraint enhancement. However, it is important to determine whether this constraint construction and guidance is fundamentally distinct from prior approaches, or simply an incremental improvement. The use of a progressively constructed constraints for controllable text generation shows novelty in its systematic approach to manage generation through explicit guidance.

*   **Significance:** Controllable text generation is a crucial technology for ensuring the safety, reliability, and utility of language models. The PG-CE method addresses the trade-off between controllability and practicality, a significant challenge in current CTG research. The introduced dataset could facilitate further research in this area. While the experiments provide some evidence for the method's effectiveness, further evaluation across a broader range of datasets and evaluation metrics would strengthen the claims. The successful implementation of these concepts into large language models also contributes to the significance of the research. However, the paper's significance hinges on its ability to outperform existing techniques in practice, which is assessed through toxicity control and readability management in text generation tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and proposed solution.
    *   Well-structured method with a logical decomposition of the CTG task.
    *   Introduction of a new dataset for constraint-enhanced CTG.
    *   Experimental validation showing improvements in generation quality and controllability.
    *   Consideration of practical applications and real-world requirements.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited novelty in the individual components. It is not clear whether the models' architecture or the training regimes have been developed or adopted from existing works.
    *   The experimental evaluation is limited in scope (only a few datasets and evaluation metrics).
    *   Lack of detailed comparison to other constraint-based approaches in the experimental section. The work is measured only against general methods. A comparative evaluation with alternative methods in constraint-based generation would strengthen the claims.
    *   The paper does not fully explain what types of fine-grained constraint templates were designed, and on what basis, which is important for reproducibility.
    *   The details of some important aspects are vague, such as construction of the evaluation dataset itself and the precise methods used in data screening.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in CTG by providing a practical and adaptable method for building constraint-based generation systems. The new dataset is also a valuable resource that could spur further development in this area. The potential impact hinges on the method's scalability, generalizability, and real-world applicability.

**Justification:**

The paper proposes a worthwhile and well-structured approach to a significant problem. However, the lack of strong novelty and the limited experimental evaluation prevent it from achieving a higher score. A more detailed analysis of the relationship of PG-CE to other constraint-based methods, including direct comparisons in the experiments, is necessary to fully assess its contribution.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Turk-LettuceDetect: A Hallucination Detection Models for Turkish RAG Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17671v1)
- **Authors**: Selva Taş, Mahmut El Huseyni, Özay Ezerceli, Reyhan Bayraktar, Fatma Betül Terzioğlu
- **Abstract**: The widespread adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) has been hindered by their tendency to hallucinate, generating plausible but factually incorrect information. While Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems attempt to address this issue by grounding responses in external knowledge, hallucination remains a persistent challenge, particularly for morphologically complex, low-resource languages like Turkish. This paper introduces Turk-LettuceDetect, the first suite of hallucination detection models specifically designed for Turkish RAG applications. Building on the LettuceDetect framework, we formulate hallucination detection as a token-level classification task and fine-tune three distinct encoder architectures: a Turkish-specific ModernBERT, TurkEmbed4STS, and multilingual EuroBERT. These models were trained on a machine-translated version of the RAGTruth benchmark dataset containing 17,790 instances across question answering, data-to-text generation, and summarization tasks. Our experimental results show that the ModernBERT-based model achieves an F1-score of 0.7266 on the complete test set, with particularly strong performance on structured tasks. The models maintain computational efficiency while supporting long contexts up to 8,192 tokens, making them suitable for real-time deployment. Comparative analysis reveals that while state-of-the-art LLMs demonstrate high recall, they suffer from low precision due to over-generation of hallucinated content, underscoring the necessity of specialized detection mechanisms. By releasing our models and translated dataset, this work addresses a critical gap in multilingual NLP and establishes a foundation for developing more reliable and trustworthy AI applications for Turkish and other languages.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Turk-LettuceDetect: A Hallucination Detection Models for Turkish RAG Applications."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Turk-LettuceDetect, a suite of hallucination detection models specifically designed for Turkish Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) applications. It addresses the issue of LLM hallucination, which is exacerbated in low-resource and morphologically complex languages like Turkish. The models are built upon the LettuceDetect framework, using a token-level classification approach and fine-tuning three encoder architectures: ModernBERT (Turkish-specific), TurkEmbed4STS, and multilingual EuroBERT. The models are trained on a machine-translated version of the RAGTruth dataset. The experimental results show that the ModernBERT-based model achieves the best performance, particularly on structured tasks, while maintaining computational efficiency for real-time deployment.  The paper provides the translated dataset and fine-tuned models under an open-source license.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by creating the first hallucination detection models specifically tailored for Turkish RAG applications. Adapting the existing LettuceDetect framework to a low-resource language is non-trivial, requiring careful consideration of linguistic complexities. The development of Turkish-specific dataset by translating existing RAGTruth dataset is also a novelty.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its addressing a critical gap in multilingual NLP, particularly for languages like Turkish that have been under-represented in hallucination detection research. By providing open-source models and a translated dataset, the authors facilitate further research and development in this area. The analysis of different encoder architectures offers insights into the effectiveness of Turkish-specific pre-training for hallucination detection in RAG settings.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly identifies and addresses a relevant problem.
    *   The methodology is well-defined and builds upon existing work in a logical way.
    *   The experimental results are presented clearly and provide a thorough evaluation of the models.
    *   The open-source release of models and data enhances the impact and reproducibility of the research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on machine translation for creating the Turkish training dataset is a potential limitation. While the authors describe protocols to maintain structural integrity, machine translation may introduce errors or nuances that affect model performance. A more ideal approach would involve human-annotated data, but is more costly.
    *   While the paper shows good results, the comparison against other fine-tuned methods is quite limited. Comparing the proposed method against zero-shot transfer would also add benefit the result.
    *   The results indicate relatively weaker results in summarization tasks. The paper hints at needing adaptation of training data to improve summarization performance. A more detailed analysis of the causes would further enhance the paper.
*   **Justification of Score:**

The work has a well-defined problem and experiments, and also the dataset is released as open-source for further development. Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses discussed above, I would rate this paper a **7**. It provides a novel and significant contribution to a crucial area in multilingual NLP, while acknowledging the limitations related to data quality. Future work can explore improved data collection and fine-tuning techniques to further enhance the models' performance and generalizability. The open-source contribution enhances the impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### EngiBench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models on Engineering Problem Solving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17677v1)
- **Authors**: Xiyuan Zhou, Xinlei Wang, Yirui He, Yang Wu, Ruixi Zou, Yuheng Cheng, Yulu Xie, Wenxuan Liu, Huan Zhao, Yan Xu, Jinjin Gu, Junhua Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on mathematical reasoning under well-posed conditions. However, real-world engineering problems require more than mathematical symbolic computation -- they need to deal with uncertainty, context, and open-ended scenarios. Existing benchmarks fail to capture these complexities. We introduce EngiBench, a hierarchical benchmark designed to evaluate LLMs on solving engineering problems. It spans three levels of increasing difficulty (foundational knowledge retrieval, multi-step contextual reasoning, and open-ended modeling) and covers diverse engineering subfields. To facilitate a deeper understanding of model performance, we systematically rewrite each problem into three controlled variants (perturbed, knowledge-enhanced, and math abstraction), enabling us to separately evaluate the model's robustness, domain-specific knowledge, and mathematical reasoning abilities. Experiment results reveal a clear performance gap across levels: models struggle more as tasks get harder, perform worse when problems are slightly changed, and fall far behind human experts on the high-level engineering tasks. These findings reveal that current LLMs still lack the high-level reasoning needed for real-world engineering, highlighting the need for future models with deeper and more reliable problem-solving capabilities. Our source code and data are available at https://github.com/EngiBench/EngiBench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "EngiBench" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EngiBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate Large Language Models (LLMs) on their ability to solve real-world engineering problems.  It addresses the limitations of existing mathematical reasoning benchmarks, which often fail to capture the complexities of engineering contexts, such as dealing with uncertainty, context-dependence, and open-ended scenarios. EngiBench is a hierarchical benchmark with three levels of increasing difficulty: foundational knowledge retrieval, multi-step contextual reasoning, and open-ended modeling.  The benchmark covers diverse engineering subfields and includes controlled problem variants (perturbed, knowledge-enhanced, and math abstraction) to analyze model robustness, domain-specific knowledge, and mathematical reasoning skills. Experiments on a range of LLMs reveal performance gaps, particularly in higher-level engineering tasks, suggesting that current LLMs lack the high-level reasoning required for practical engineering applications. The dataset and code are available publicly.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has high novelty for several reasons. Firstly, it directly targets the *application* of LLMs to *real-world* engineering problems, moving beyond abstract mathematical reasoning. Secondly, its hierarchical structure is designed not just to measure overall performance, but to *diagnose* *specific* reasoning weaknesses related to different aspects of engineering problem-solving (information extraction, domain-specific reasoning, multi-objective decision-making, uncertainty handling). This contrasts with many prior benchmarks that simply offer a collection of tasks with a single overall score. The systematic generation of problem variants (perturbed, knowledge-enhanced, math-abstraction) is a significant contribution that allows for a fine-grained analysis of LLM capabilities and potential contamination issues. Finally, the inclusion of open-ended engineering modeling tasks with rubric-based evaluation is a novel attempt to capture the complexities of real-world engineering practice.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its practical relevance and its potential to shape future LLM research and development. Identifying that existing LLMs underperform on complex engineering tasks provides clear directions for future research to focus on addressing these specific reasoning deficiencies. Moreover, by releasing a comprehensive and well-designed benchmark, EngiBench allows for more rigorous and ecologically-valid comparisons between different models and approaches to improving LLM performance in engineering domains. The release of the dataset and source code will accelerate research efforts.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Defined Scope:**  The paper carefully defines the scope of "engineering problem-solving capability" and provides a well-structured taxonomy.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:**  The dataset construction and evaluation methods are well-defined, and the generation of problem variants is systematic. The use of rubrics for open-ended tasks adds rigor to the evaluation process. The discussion of potential contamination is also a strength.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The paper evaluates a wide range of LLMs, providing a good overview of the state-of-the-art.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The benchmark addresses a critical gap in the application of LLMs to real-world problems.
    *   **Public Release:** The public release of the dataset and code promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Human-in-the-loop Bias:** While the human oversight ensures quality, it also introduces a potential bias.  It would be valuable to perform further analysis on inter-annotator agreement and potential biases in scoring rubrics.
    *   **Limited Context Window:** The paper acknowledges the limitation of not including long-context problems. This restricts the complexity of the evaluated tasks and limits the benchmark's ability to assess LLMs' ability to handle large amounts of engineering information.
    *   **Lack of Multimodality:**  The absence of multimodal data limits the ability to evaluate models with complex visual aids. While the dataset is restricted to text only, there is no discussion on the implications or an alternative format that could address multimodal evaluation.
    *   **Simplified Engineering Tasks:** Even with open-ended problems, the level of simplification required to make them tractable for current LLMs might still limit the ecological validity. Future work should consider more complex, simulation-based tasks. The difficulty of the tasks are evaluated but do not contain information about the time for the average human to evaluate the performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** EngiBench has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating LLMs in engineering. It could drive progress in areas such as: improved handling of uncertainty and context, better integration of domain-specific knowledge, and more reliable reasoning on open-ended problems. It also has implications for how engineering education can be augmented with LLMs.

*   **Justification for Score:** Considering the paper's high novelty, practical significance, well-defined methodology, and potential for future impact, despite the identified limitations, a score of 8 is justified. The rigorous dataset and method of approach will significantly inform the future of language models in real-world use cases.

Score: 8

### When TableQA Meets Noise: A Dual Denoising Framework for Complex Questions and Large-scale Tables
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17680v1)
- **Authors**: Shenghao Ye, Yu Guo, Dong Jin, Yikai Shen, Yunpeng Hou, Shuangwu Chen, Jian Yang, Xiaofeng Jiang
- **Abstract**: Table question answering (TableQA) is a fundamental task in natural language processing (NLP). The strong reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) have brought significant advances in this field. However, as real-world applications involve increasingly complex questions and larger tables, substantial noisy data is introduced, which severely degrades reasoning performance. To address this challenge, we focus on improving two core capabilities: Relevance Filtering, which identifies and retains information truly relevant to reasoning, and Table Pruning, which reduces table size while preserving essential content. Based on these principles, we propose EnoTab, a dual denoising framework for complex questions and large-scale tables. Specifically, we first perform Evidence-based Question Denoising by decomposing the question into minimal semantic units and filtering out those irrelevant to answer reasoning based on consistency and usability criteria. Then, we propose Evidence Tree-guided Table Denoising, which constructs an explicit and transparent table pruning path to remove irrelevant data step by step. At each pruning step, we observe the intermediate state of the table and apply a post-order node rollback mechanism to handle abnormal table states, ultimately producing a highly reliable sub-table for final answer reasoning. Finally, extensive experiments show that EnoTab achieves outstanding performance on TableQA tasks with complex questions and large-scale tables, confirming its effectiveness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "When TableQA Meets Noise: A Dual Denoising Framework for Complex Questions and Large-scale Tables":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EnoTab, a dual denoising framework for Table Question Answering (TableQA) tasks that addresses challenges posed by complex questions and large-scale tables containing noisy data. The framework consists of two main components: (1) Evidence-based Question Denoising (EQD), which decomposes questions into minimal semantic units (Evidences) and filters out irrelevant units based on consistency and usability criteria, and (2) Evidence Tree-guided Table Denoising (ETD), which builds an explicit table pruning path (Evidence Tree) to remove irrelevant data while preserving essential content using a post-order node rollback mechanism. The paper claims EnoTab achieves state-of-the-art performance on TableQA tasks involving complex questions and large tables.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of a dual denoising framework is not entirely novel in the realm of TableQA. Existing methods have explored both question simplification and table pruning to improve performance. The novelty of this paper hinges on specific implementations within EQD and ETD, primarily: the Evidence-based Question Denoising approach; the Evidence Tree-guided Denoising; and the post-order node rollback mechanism. The use of "Evidence" to decompose the problem is also useful. However, question decomposition is not entirely new either.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed by the paper – handling noisy data in TableQA involving complex questions and large tables – is highly relevant in real-world scenarios. Real-world data often contains noise, and scalability is crucial for practical applications. Improving the robustness and accuracy of TableQA systems in such scenarios is, therefore, a significant contribution. The paper attempts to do this by improving Relevance Filtering, and Table Pruning, which are considered the key reasons for performance degradation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The **dual denoising approach** is well-motivated and addresses two critical aspects of the noisy TableQA problem: irrelevant information in both the question and the table.
    *   The **Evidence-based Question Denoising (EQD)** seems to provide a fine-grained method for identifying spurious correlations in the question. The two assessment criteria – consistency and usability – are reasonable and provide a structured approach to relevance filtering.
    *   The **Evidence Tree-guided Table Denoising (ETD)** offers a more transparent and controllable table pruning process compared to black-box approaches like generating SQL or Python programs. The post-order node rollback mechanism provides a reasonable way to handle abnormal table states and prevent the loss of important data.
    *   The paper reports strong experimental results across various datasets, including large-scale ones. Ablation studies confirm the importance of each component of EnoTab. Adaptability tests reveal that EnoTab is more robust than existing methods and can cope with different models.
    *   The authors comprehensively discuss potential limitations to the approach as well.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The implementation relies heavily on LLMs for several components, including evidence generation, tree construction, and semantic discrimination. Therefore, EnoTab's performance is tightly coupled with the capabilities of these LLMs. Using different or weaker LLMs might significantly affect the results. Though adaptable, the performance of the underlying methods is highly dependent on the effectiveness of the LLMs used.
    *   The computational cost of EnoTab might be high, especially for very large tables, due to multiple LLM calls for evidence generation and table verification. While it addresses noisy data, scalability in terms of computational resources is a concern. There is no information on the computational cost.
    *   Some of the specific implementations, like the And2Or operation, seem ad-hoc and may not be optimal for all scenarios.
    *   Some design choices may be unnecessarily complex, such as the Evidence Tree, which requires logical operations for intermediate nodes. SQL generation might be simpler, more effective, and easier to debug.
    *   The evaluation is primarily based on the exact match metric, which can be sensitive to minor variations in the generated answers. It would be helpful to include other metrics that assess the semantic correctness of the answers.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could potentially influence future research in TableQA by highlighting the importance of relevance filtering and transparent table pruning. The ideas of evidence-based question denoising and rollback mechanisms may inspire new approaches to handle noisy data in other NLP tasks.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:** The paper presents a reasonably novel and well-motivated framework for addressing a relevant problem in TableQA. The proposed components have interesting design choices, particularly the evidence-based question denoising and the transparent table pruning with rollback, and there is adequate explanation of the limitations of the approach. The experimental results are strong, and ablation studies confirm the importance of the individual components.

However, the reliance on LLMs for several core components, the potential computational cost, and some of the less-than-elegant implementation details limit the overall impact and innovation. More comparisons with SQL approaches would be useful, and it is important to further test computational costs. Thus, while representing a solid contribution, it does not reach the level of a groundbreaking advancement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating LLM-Generated Versus Human-Authored Responses in Role-Play Dialogues
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17694v1)
- **Authors**: Dongxu Lu, Johan Jeuring, Albert Gatt
- **Abstract**: Evaluating large language models (LLMs) in long-form, knowledge-grounded role-play dialogues remains challenging. This study compares LLM-generated and human-authored responses in multi-turn professional training simulations through human evaluation ($N=38$) and automated LLM-as-a-judge assessment. Human evaluation revealed significant degradation in LLM-generated response quality across turns, particularly in naturalness, context maintenance and overall quality, while human-authored responses progressively improved. In line with this finding, participants also indicated a consistent preference for human-authored dialogue. These human judgements were validated by our automated LLM-as-a-judge evaluation, where Gemini 2.0 Flash achieved strong alignment with human evaluators on both zero-shot pairwise preference and stochastic 6-shot construct ratings, confirming the widening quality gap between LLM and human responses over time. Our work contributes a multi-turn benchmark exposing LLM degradation in knowledge-grounded role-play dialogues and provides a validated hybrid evaluation framework to guide the reliable integration of LLMs in training simulations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance of large language models (LLMs) in knowledge-grounded role-play dialogues over multiple turns.  It compares LLM-generated responses with human-authored responses in professional training simulations, using both human evaluations and an LLM-as-a-judge approach.  The key findings are that LLM-generated responses degrade in quality over longer dialogues, particularly in naturalness, context maintenance, and overall quality, while human-authored responses tend to improve.  The human evaluators consistently preferred human-authored dialogues.  The LLM-as-a-judge evaluation, particularly using GEMINI 2.0 FLASH, validated these human judgments, confirming the widening quality gap over time. The study contributes a multi-turn benchmark for exposing LLM degradation and provides a hybrid evaluation framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on the multi-turn degradation of LLM performance in knowledge-grounded role-play dialogues. While LLM evaluation is a well-trodden field, the emphasis on *long-form* dialogue and its impact on specific quality metrics like naturalness and context maintenance represents a valuable contribution. It moves beyond simple two-turn evaluations to a more ecologically valid scenario. The combination of human evaluation with an LLM-as-a-judge approach for validation is also a plus, although the LLM-as-a-judge paradigm is becoming increasingly common. The benchmarking of LLM response quality over multiple turns is the key contribution.

**Significance:**  The findings are significant because they highlight a practical limitation of current LLMs in complex, real-world applications like professional training simulations.  If LLMs are to be effectively integrated into these kinds of systems, understanding and mitigating the degradation in performance over time is critical.  The paper's benchmark and evaluation framework provide a valuable tool for researchers working on improving LLM long-context capabilities and dialogue coherence. The validation of LLM-as-a-judge strengthens the belief that LLMs can be used for automating evaluation tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Question:**  The paper articulates a clear and relevant research question.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The study employs a well-defined methodology, combining human evaluation with automated LLM-as-a-judge assessment.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a thorough analysis of the data, including statistical testing and qualitative insights from focus groups.
*   **Practical Implications:**  The findings have practical implications for the design and implementation of LLM-based training simulations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The human evaluation focuses on a single scenario.  While the automated evaluation extends to additional scenarios, the initial human judgment anchors primarily on the first use case.
*   **Model Choice:** Focusing on a specific model for response generation (LLAMA 3) can limit the generalizability of the findings. Though justified by the need for fine-tuning, it would be beneficial to demonstrate similar degradation trends with other LLMs in future work.
*   **Ceiling effect with "Understandable":** The consistent maximum score for the "Understandable" construct limits this construct's discrimitive power to provide meaningful insights into the generated responses.

**Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

*   **Long-Context Modeling:** It underscores the need for improved long-context modeling in LLMs.
*   **Evaluation Metrics:** It provides a valuable benchmark for evaluating LLMs in multi-turn dialogue settings.
*   **Hybrid Evaluation Frameworks:** It demonstrates the effectiveness of combining human evaluation with LLM-as-a-judge approaches.

**Overall Assessment:**

This paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by highlighting a critical issue with LLM performance in long-form dialogue. The methodology is strong, and the findings have practical implications for the design of LLM-based training systems. The emphasis on multi-turn degradation and the introduction of a benchmark justifies a positive score. While there are some limitations in scope and model choice, the findings are robust and well-supported.

### Investigating Bias: A Multilingual Pipeline for Generating, Solving, and Evaluating Math Problems with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17701v1)
- **Authors**: Mariam Mahran, Katharina Simbeck
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for educational support, yet their response quality varies depending on the language of interaction. This paper presents an automated multilingual pipeline for generating, solving, and evaluating math problems aligned with the German K-10 curriculum. We generated 628 math exercises and translated them into English, German, and Arabic. Three commercial LLMs (GPT-4o-mini, Gemini 2.5 Flash, and Qwen-plus) were prompted to produce step-by-step solutions in each language. A held-out panel of LLM judges, including Claude 3.5 Haiku, evaluated solution quality using a comparative framework. Results show a consistent gap, with English solutions consistently rated highest, and Arabic often ranked lower. These findings highlight persistent linguistic bias and the need for more equitable multilingual AI systems in education.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper introduces an automated multilingual pipeline for generating, solving, and evaluating math problems across different languages (English, German, and Arabic) using Large Language Models (LLMs). The pipeline aims to uncover linguistic biases that affect the quality of LLM-generated math solutions. The authors generated a dataset of 628 math exercises aligned with the German K-10 curriculum and translated them into the specified languages. Three commercial LLMs (GPT-40-mini, Gemini 2.5 Flash, and Qwen-plus) were used to generate step-by-step solutions in each language. An LLM-based evaluation framework, using comparative assessment by a panel of LLM judges (including Claude 3.5 Haiku), was implemented to evaluate the quality of solutions. The results consistently showed a performance disparity, with English solutions rated highest and Arabic often ranked lower, highlighting persistent linguistic biases in LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its automated multilingual pipeline for a thorough and scalable evaluation of LLM-generated math solutions. While previous studies have explored language bias in LLMs, this work combines generation, translation, step-by-step solution generation, and comparative LLM-based evaluation in a single, automated framework, specifically focusing on math problems. The use of a held-out judging strategy to prevent indirect bias is also a thoughtful addition. The creation of a dataset aligned with a specific curriculum and translated to multiple languages also adds value.

**Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they highlight the potential for linguistic bias to exacerbate educational inequalities. As LLMs are increasingly integrated into educational tools, it is crucial to address these biases to ensure equitable access to quality education regardless of language background. The paper's methodology offers a valuable approach for identifying and quantifying these biases, providing insights that can inform future research and development efforts aimed at creating more linguistically inclusive AI systems for education. The analysis of LLM justifications provides deeper insight than simple accuracy metrics alone. The paper also provides a concrete tool and dataset that can be used for future research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Automated and Scalable Pipeline:** The automated pipeline enables consistent multilingual comparison across a large number of exercises.
*   **Careful Dataset Creation and Translation:** Manual review and auditing of the generated and translated datasets ensure quality and consistency.
*   **Robust Evaluation Framework:** The comparative assessment methodology, held-out judging strategy, and analysis of LLM justifications enhance the reliability and interpretability of the evaluation results.
*   **Clear and Consistent Findings:** The results consistently highlight performance disparities across languages, with English favored and Arabic disadvantaged.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper's findings provide practical implications for educators and developers regarding the use of LLMs in multilingual educational settings.
*   **Code availability:** Opens the door for future research

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Number of Languages:** The study focuses on only three languages (English, German, and Arabic). Expanding the analysis to more languages, especially those with lower resources, could provide a more comprehensive understanding of linguistic bias.
*   **Specific LLMs:** The evaluation is limited to three specific LLMs. Evaluating a wider range of models, including open-source LLMs, would increase the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Curriculum Specificity:** The dataset is aligned with the German K-10 curriculum. While this provides a specific context, it may limit the generalizability of the findings to other educational systems.
*   **The selection of the "neutral" model may introduce bias**: Claude is known to perform well on English language reasoning tasks and therefore is not completely neutral.
*   **Bias in LLM Evaluation Metrics:** LLM based judges are not neutral and may introduce bias in the evaluation. There are few options for scaling this to a larger number of languages and models, however, so there is limited availability of alternative metrics.
*   **Limited exploration of mitigation strategies:** The paper identifies the bias, but doesn't provide concrete solutions for mitigating this bias.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of AI in education by providing a comprehensive and scalable approach for evaluating linguistic bias in LLM-generated math solutions. While the study has some limitations, the findings are significant and have practical implications for educators and developers. The automated pipeline and evaluation framework provide a valuable tool for future research in this area.

### ConfClip: Confidence-Weighted and Clipped Reward for Reinforcement Learning in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17730v1)
- **Authors**: Bonan Zhang, Zhongqi Chen, Bowen Song, Qinya Li, Fan Wu, Guihai Chen
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a standard paradigm for refining large language models (LLMs) beyond pre-training and instruction tuning. A prominent line of work is RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR), which leverages automatically verifiable outcomes (e.g., correctness or executability) to generate reward signals. While efficient, this framework faces two key limitations: First, its binary feedback is too sparse to capture the quality of the reasoning process. Second, its coarse-grained rewards potentially lead to vanishing gradients. Inspired by observations from human learning, we introduce a RL technique that integrates verifiable outcomes with the model's own confidence estimates. This joint design enriches the reward signal, providing finer-grained feedback and implicitly supervising the reasoning process. Experimental results demonstrate that our proposed method enhances RL performance across multiple datasets and reduces token consumption during inference, while incurring negligible additional training cost. Moreover, it can be used as a plug-in module to enhance other state-of-the-art RL methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ConfClip, a novel reinforcement learning (RL) method designed to refine large language models (LLMs). ConfClip builds upon RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) by integrating the model's confidence estimates into the reward signal. This addresses the limitations of sparse, binary rewards in RLVR, providing finer-grained feedback and implicitly supervising the reasoning process. ConfClip weights the correctness reward by the model's confidence in its answer, penalizing overconfident incorrect answers and rewarding confident correct answers. The paper also identifies and mitigates instability issues that arise when directly using confidence-weighted rewards through a clipping mechanism. Experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate that ConfClip improves RL performance, reduces token consumption, and can be used as a plug-in module for other RL methods, all with minimal additional training cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating model confidence into the reward signal of RLVR is reasonably novel. Prior works have explored using internal signals or self-derived rewards, but the specific approach of weighting verifiable rewards with a clipped confidence score presents a unique combination. The identification and resolution of instability when using confidence-weighted rewards also adds to the novelty. The clipping mechanism, while simple, is presented as a practical solution to a relevant problem.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a real limitation of RLVR – the sparseness and coarseness of binary rewards. By enriching the reward signal with confidence, ConfClip offers a potentially more efficient and effective way to train LLMs for reasoning tasks. The demonstrated improvements in performance, token consumption, and training stability suggest that ConfClip could be a valuable addition to the RL toolkit for LLMs. The plug-in nature of the method further enhances its significance, as it can be easily integrated with existing RLVR frameworks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing RLVR methods and motivates the need for a finer-grained reward signal. The analogy to human learning is intuitive and effective.
    *   **Technical Soundness:** The proposed ConfClip method is technically sound and relatively simple to implement. The clipping mechanism is a pragmatic solution to the identified instability issue.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence supporting the effectiveness of ConfClip across multiple datasets and model sizes. The ablation study highlights the importance of the clipping operation.
    *   **Generalizability:** The plug-in nature of the method and the demonstrated improvements on different datasets suggest good generalizability.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The paper includes sufficient detail on the experimental setup and implementation, increasing the likelihood of reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the paper provides a good intuitive explanation, a more formal theoretical analysis of the convergence properties of ConfClip and the impact of the clipping parameter would strengthen the contribution.
    *   **Simplicity of Clipping:** While effective, the clipping mechanism is relatively simple.  The justification for its specific design could be expanded. Are there other stabilization techniques that could work better or more generally?
    *   **Dependence on Model Confidence:** The method's performance hinges on the quality of the model's confidence estimates. The paper could discuss the limitations of using confidence scores from LLMs and potential strategies for improving confidence calibration.
    *   **Scope of Experiments:** Although the experiments span different datasets and model sizes, the evaluation could be expanded to include more diverse tasks and scenarios.
    *   **Baselines:** More thorough comparisons with other recently published RL methods for LLMs could be useful.

*   **Potential Influence:** ConfClip has the potential to influence the field by providing a simple and effective way to improve the training of LLMs using RL. The idea of integrating model confidence into the reward signal could inspire further research on more sophisticated reward shaping techniques. The plug-in nature of the method makes it easily adoptable by other researchers and practitioners.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to the field of RL for LLMs. The novelty lies in the specific combination of confidence-weighted rewards and a clipping mechanism for stabilization. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating consistent improvements across various datasets and model sizes. While the method is relatively simple, its effectiveness, ease of implementation, and plug-in nature make it a significant contribution.  The paper's weaknesses are primarily related to a lack of deeper theoretical analysis and further comparison. Taking all these aspects into consideration, a score of 7 reflects the paper's strong empirical validation and practical value.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### WISE: Weak-Supervision-Guided Step-by-Step Explanations for Multimodal LLMs in Image Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17740v1)
- **Authors**: Yiwen Jiang, Deval Mehta, Siyuan Yan, Yaling Shen, Zimu Wang, Zongyuan Ge
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown promise in visual-textual reasoning, with Multimodal Chain-of-Thought (MCoT) prompting significantly enhancing interpretability. However, existing MCoT methods rely on rationale-rich datasets and largely focus on inter-object reasoning, overlooking the intra-object understanding crucial for image classification. To address this gap, we propose WISE, a Weak-supervision-guided Step-by-step Explanation method that augments any image classification dataset with MCoTs by reformulating the concept-based representations from Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) into concise, interpretable reasoning chains under weak supervision. Experiments across ten datasets show that our generated MCoTs not only improve interpretability by 37% but also lead to gains in classification accuracy when used to fine-tune MLLMs. Our work bridges concept-based interpretability and generative MCoT reasoning, providing a generalizable framework for enhancing MLLMs in fine-grained visual understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "WISE: Weak-Supervision-Guided Step-by-Step Explanations for Multimodal LLMs in Image Classification" addresses the challenge of generating high-quality, interpretable explanations (Multimodal Chain-of-Thought, MCoT) for image classification tasks performed by Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The authors propose WISE, a method that leverages weak supervision from Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) to create these explanations. WISE reformulates concept-based representations from CBMs into concise reasoning chains, guided by weak supervision to select relevant concepts and organize them into coherent explanations. Experiments across ten image classification datasets demonstrate improved interpretability and classification accuracy when using WISE-generated MCoTs to fine-tune MLLMs. The method bridges concept-based interpretability with generative MCoT reasoning, providing a framework for enhancing MLLMs in fine-grained visual understanding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core contribution – bridging the gap between CBMs and MCoT for MLLMs, using weak supervision for automated MCoT generation for image classification – is genuinely novel. Previous work focused either on CBMs for inherent interpretability or MCoT for complex reasoning, but not the integration for fine-grained image understanding via MLLM fine-tuning. The novelty lies in *how* the authors repurpose CBM representations, incorporating aspects like concept ordering and importance derived from decision trees, and converting it to a generative setting.  The focus on *intra-object* reasoning in image classification, as opposed to prior work that concentrates on *inter-object* relationships, is also a valuable distinction.

*   **Significance:** Improving the interpretability of MLLMs is a crucial step towards building trustworthy AI systems. The potential impact is in areas where visual understanding is paramount but explanation is equally important, such as medical imaging or autonomous driving. By automating the generation of MCoTs, the authors address the scalability problem of creating rationale-rich datasets.  Further, the weak-to-strong generalization approach is significant because it shows how lightweight, interpretable models (CBMs, decision trees) can guide the training of much larger, more powerful (but typically opaque) MLLMs. The demonstrated improvement in both interpretability *and* accuracy strengthens the significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The method is well-motivated, addressing a real need for interpretable MLLMs in image classification.
    *   The technical approach is sound, leveraging existing techniques (CBMs, CLIP, decision trees) in a novel and effective way.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive, covering a variety of datasets and evaluation metrics. The improvement in interpretability is substantial.
    *   The ablation studies are well-designed and provide insights into the contribution of different components.
    *   The explanation generation process integrates positive and negative information, and incorporates prior knowledge about the class.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on prompt-engineered concept banks for datasets without explicit annotations can be a limiting factor. The quality of the MCoTs is directly tied to the quality of these banks.
    *   The template-based approach for converting concepts into natural language may limit the fluency and expressiveness of the generated explanations.
    *   The focus on a relatively small MLLM (Qwen2-VL-7B) raises questions about scalability and generalizability to even larger models. While the approach is likely applicable, more experimental validation would strengthen this claim.
    *   While the experimental results indicate the proposed method improves upon the baselines, a human evaluation component of the MCOT generated by the model would significantly further validate the proposed method.

*   **Rigor:**
    * The paper is well-written with a clear and easy-to-follow structure.
    * The concepts are well motivated and supported with references to other works.
    * The results are reported with appropriate metrics and comparisons against existing literature.

*   **Potential influence on the field:**

    *The paper could influence researchers to explore a more structured approach to incorporating information into MLLMs during fine-tuning.
    * The paper could influence researchers to explore more MLLMs, potentially leading to novel approaches within those models.
    * Future work could use the concept annotations generated by this method to explore a more structured approach to incorporate information into MLLMs during pre-training.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7.5** to this paper.

*   The novelty is high because it presents a new way to integrate concept-based models with MLLMs for generating interpretable explanations.
*   The significance is also high, with potential impact in areas requiring trustworthy AI. The method addresses the scalability issue for rationale creation.
*   However, there are limitations related to the reliance on prompt-engineered concept banks, the template-based natural language generation, and limited validation on very large MLLMs. While the results are good, there is room for further improvement and broader validation. There is also an opportunity to incorporate a more direct human component to improve the quality of the generated MCOTs.
