### Adaptive Fast-and-Slow Visual Program Reasoning for Long-Form VideoQA
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17743v1)
- **Authors**: Chenglin Li, Feng Han, FengTao, Ruilin Li, Qianglong Chen, Jingqi Tong, Yin Zhang, Jiaqi Wang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in generating program workflows for visual tasks. However, previous approaches often rely on closed-source models, lack systematic reasoning, and struggle with long-form video question answering (videoQA). To address these challenges, we introduce the FS-VisPR framework, an adaptive visual program reasoning approach that balances fast reasoning for simple queries with slow reasoning for difficult ones. First, we design efficient visual modules (e.g., key clip retrieval and subtitle retrieval) to support long-form video tasks. Then, we construct a diverse and high-quality fast-slow reasoning dataset with a strong LLM to align open-source language models' ability to generate visual program workflows as FS-LLM. Next, we design a fast-slow reasoning framework with FS-LLM: Simple queries are directly solved by VideoLLMs, while difficult ones invoke visual program reasoning, motivated by human-like reasoning processes. During this process, low-confidence fast-thinking answers will trigger a second-stage slow-reasoning process, and a fallback mechanism to fast reasoning is activated if the program execution fails. Moreover, we improve visual programs through parameter search during both training and inference. By adjusting the parameters of the visual modules within the program, multiple variants are generated: during training, programs that yield correct answers are selected, while during inference, the program with the highest confidence result is applied. Experiments show that FS-VisPR improves both efficiency and reliability in visual program workflows. It achieves 50.4% accuracy on LVBench, surpassing GPT-4o, matching the performance of Qwen2.5VL-72B on VideoMME.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FS-VisPR, an adaptive visual program reasoning framework designed for long-form Video Question Answering (VideoQA). It addresses the limitations of existing approaches that often rely on closed-source models, lack systematic reasoning, and struggle with long videos. FS-VisPR employs a fast-slow reasoning strategy: simple questions are handled directly by a VideoLLM, while complex ones invoke visual program reasoning (using efficient visual modules like key clip retrieval and subtitle retrieval) inspired by human-like reasoning processes.  A key feature is the confidence score assigned to fast-thinking answers, which determines whether to trigger a slower, program-based reasoning stage. The framework also uses a parameter search to improve the robustness of the visual programs by generating multiple variants and selecting the most confident one.  The paper shows that FS-VisPR improves both efficiency and reliability, achieving competitive accuracy on various VideoQA benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach by combining fast and slow reasoning in a VideoQA system and introducing parameter search to improve program robustness. While visual program reasoning itself isn't entirely new, the adaptive confidence-based triggering mechanism and the integration with efficient visual modules specifically designed for *long-form* video analysis are noteworthy.  The fast-slow *data construction* which aligns the LLM with different reasoning strategies is also a strong point.
* **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the efficiency and interpretability of VideoQA, especially in the context of long-form videos. The ability to adaptively choose between fast and slow reasoning strategies can reduce computational costs while maintaining accuracy. The visual program approach inherently provides better interpretability compared to monolithic VideoLLMs. The fact that a relatively small 7B model, enhanced with visual modules and adaptive reasoning, can achieve performance comparable to much larger models (GPT-4o, and even surpassing Qwen2.5VL-72B on some tasks) indicates a significant practical benefit in terms of resource utilization.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Adaptive Reasoning:** The confidence-based fast-slow reasoning paradigm is a key strength, allowing for efficient processing of simpler queries and more thorough analysis of complex ones.
    * **Efficient Visual Modules:** The design of tailored modules for long-form videos, such as key clip retrieval and subtitle retrieval, addresses the specific challenges of this domain.
    * **Parameter Search for Robustness:** The module parameter search method improves the reliability of program execution, a common bottleneck for visual program approaches.
    * **Competitive Results:** Demonstrates strong performance on challenging benchmarks, surpassing strong baselines while using a smaller model.
    * **Clear and well-written:** The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the proposed approach and the experimental results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on GPT-4.1 for Program Generation:** The initial generation of visual programs relies on the closed-source GPT-4.1. While the FS-LLM is then fine-tuned to align open-source models, the dependency on GPT-4.1 for dataset creation might limit reproducibility and accessibility. The fine-tuning process itself could be detailed a little further.
    * **Module Limitations:** While the paper outlines the benefits of the designed modules, it's also important to acknowledge that they might not cover all possible reasoning scenarios. Expanding the module library could further improve performance on particularly challenging queries (as the failure analysis also pointed out).
    * **Limited Ablation of Individual Modules:** While an ablation study is included, a more granular analysis of the individual contribution of *each* module (beyond just the overall "All" setting) would further strengthen the claims about their effectiveness.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in VideoQA by highlighting the importance of adaptive reasoning strategies and efficient visual module design. The parameter search technique could also be adopted by other visual program reasoning systems to improve robustness.  The code release would also be a strong enabler of follow-up work.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid and well-executed approach with demonstrable improvements over existing methods in long-form VideoQA.  The novelty lies primarily in the adaptive fast-slow reasoning strategy and its specific implementation with efficient visual modules and a parameter search. The competitive results, obtained with a relatively small model, make this work practically significant. The reliance on GPT-4.1 for the initial dataset construction and the limited ablation analysis of individual modules slightly detract from the overall score. The contribution is significant enough to warrant a high score, but the identified weaknesses prevent it from being exceptional.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### A State-Update Prompting Strategy for Efficient and Robust Multi-turn Dialogue
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17766v1)
- **Authors**: Ziyi Liu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) struggle with information forgetting and inefficiency in long-horizon, multi-turn dialogues. To address this, we propose a training-free prompt engineering method, the State-Update Multi-turn Dialogue Strategy. It utilizes "State Reconstruction" and "History Remind" mechanisms to effectively manage dialogue history. Our strategy shows strong performance across multiple multi-hop QA datasets. For instance, on the HotpotQA dataset, it improves the core information filtering score by 32.6%, leading to a 14.1% increase in the downstream QA score, while also reducing inference time by 73.1% and token consumption by 59.4%. Ablation studies confirm the pivotal roles of both components. Our work offers an effective solution for optimizing LLMs in long-range interactions, providing new insights for developing more robust Agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of information forgetting and inefficiency in multi-turn dialogues for Large Language Models (LLMs).  It proposes a training-free prompt engineering method called the "State-Update Multi-turn Dialogue Strategy." This strategy uses "State Reconstruction" and "History Reminder" mechanisms to manage dialogue history effectively. The authors demonstrate improved performance on multi-hop QA datasets, specifically showing improvements in information filtering, downstream QA performance, reduced inference time, and token consumption.  Ablation studies are used to confirm the importance of each component of the proposed strategy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core idea of a state-update mechanism within a prompt engineering framework has some novelty. Instead of naively concatenating dialogue history (which is a well-known problem in LLMs), the approach seeks to summarize and explicitly remind the model of key information from prior turns.  While RAG systems have elements of this idea, this is a training-free *prompting* method is more accessible and immediate.  However, the individual components, such as reminder mechanisms or structured outputs, are not entirely novel on their own; the originality lies in the specific *combination* and application of these elements to address the multi-turn dialogue challenge in LLMs.

*   **Significance:** The paper has practical significance. Multi-turn dialogues are crucial for many real-world applications of LLMs (e.g., chatbots, customer service agents, complex information retrieval). The demonstrated improvements in information filtering, QA performance, and efficiency (reduced token consumption, faster inference) are valuable. The training-free nature of the approach is a major strength, allowing for easy implementation across different LLMs without the need for extensive fine-tuning. The detailed ablation study also strengthens the claim that the strategy is well thought out.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly articulates the problem of information forgetting and inefficiency in multi-turn dialogues.
    *   Effective solution: The proposed State-Update Multi-turn Dialogue Strategy shows promising results across multiple datasets.
    *   Efficiency gains:  The significant reduction in token consumption and inference time makes the strategy practically appealing.
    *   Training-free nature: This enhances accessibility and ease of adoption.
    *   Ablation study: Rigorous analysis demonstrating the importance of individual components.
    *   Performance evaluation: The study is conducted on multiple public benchmarks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited scope of experiments: While the paper tests on multiple datasets, they are all QA-style tasks. Testing on more diverse dialogue scenarios (e.g., open-ended conversations, goal-oriented dialogues) would strengthen the generalizability claims.
    *   Model Choice: The paper uses only Qwen2.5 models for experiments. While these models are respectable, the use of other popular models (like Llama3 or GPT series) might make the work more convincing.
    *   Depth of analysis: While the paper includes performance on various settings, there is little analysis that tries to dive deeper into the reasons behind certain outcomes.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper offers a practical and effective solution for a critical problem in LLM-based dialogue systems.  The training-free nature of the approach could lead to its widespread adoption. The work could also inspire further research into more sophisticated state management techniques for long-range interactions in LLMs. The analysis of the forgetting phenomenon and recency bias is also valuable.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a useful technique for improving multi-turn dialogue performance in LLMs.  The novelty, while incremental, lies in the specific combination of state reconstruction and history reminder within a training-free prompting framework. The practical benefits (efficiency gains, improved QA performance) and the clear experimental results are substantial. The main limitations are the limited scope of datasets and a lack of deeper analysis into how the proposed strategy solves the problem. Despite these weaknesses, the paper makes a solid contribution with the potential to influence the development of more robust and efficient dialogue agents. The results are practically useful and the analysis is well-executed (especially the ablation study).

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### I2VWM: Robust Watermarking for Image to Video Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17773v1)
- **Authors**: Guanjie Wang, Zehua Ma, Han Fang, Weiming Zhang
- **Abstract**: The rapid progress of image-guided video generation (I2V) has raised concerns about its potential misuse in misinformation and fraud, underscoring the urgent need for effective digital watermarking. While existing watermarking methods demonstrate robustness within a single modality, they fail to trace source images in I2V settings. To address this gap, we introduce the concept of Robust Diffusion Distance, which measures the temporal persistence of watermark signals in generated videos. Building on this, we propose I2VWM, a cross-modal watermarking framework designed to enhance watermark robustness across time. I2VWM leverages a video-simulation noise layer during training and employs an optical-flow-based alignment module during inference. Experiments on both open-source and commercial I2V models demonstrate that I2VWM significantly improves robustness while maintaining imperceptibility, establishing a new paradigm for cross-modal watermarking in the era of generative video. \href{https://github.com/MrCrims/I2VWM-Robust-Watermarking-for-Image-to-Video-Generation}{Code Released.}
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "I2VWM: Robust Watermarking for Image to Video Generation" addresses the problem of watermarking image-guided video generation (I2V) outputs.  Existing watermarking methods are typically designed for a single modality (image or video) and struggle to maintain robustness when tracing source images used in I2V.  The authors introduce the concept of Robust Diffusion Distance (RDD) to quantify the temporal persistence of watermark signals in generated videos. They propose I2VWM, a cross-modal watermarking framework based on an encoder-decoder architecture.  I2VWM incorporates a video-simulation noise layer during training to improve robustness and an optical-flow-based alignment module during inference to extend the RDD.  The paper presents experimental results on both open-source and commercial I2V models, demonstrating improved robustness while maintaining imperceptibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying and formally defining the challenge of "Robust Diffusion Distance" for watermarking I2V outputs, a setting where existing techniques fall short. Introducing RDD is a novel concept, as is the overall architecture that specifically addresses cross-modal watermarking for generative video. The video simulation noise layer and the optical-flow-based alignment module are also novel components tailored to the challenges of I2V.

*   **Significance:** The rise of I2V models presents significant risks regarding misinformation and fraud.  Effective watermarking techniques are crucial for tracking the origin of generated content.  By improving watermark robustness in the I2V setting, this paper contributes to the development of more secure and reliable mechanisms for content authentication. The experimental results show a clear improvement over existing methods. Further, evaluating I2VWM across both Open-source and commercial platforms increases the practical significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Definition:** Clearly identifies and defines a relevant and timely problem in the I2V domain.
    *   **Technical Soundness:** The proposed I2VWM framework is technically sound and well-motivated. The design choices for the noise layer and alignment module are justified.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The evaluation is comprehensive, including comparisons with multiple baselines and testing on open-source and commercial models.  The use of multiple evaluation metrics (PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS, ACC, FACC, VACC, RDD) provides a detailed assessment of the framework's performance. The inclusion of classic distortion attacks provides additional validation of the watermark's resilience.
    *   **Writing Quality:** The paper is well-written, clearly explaining the problem, proposed solution, and experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Assumption of Temporal Consistency:** The method's reliance on optical flow and the assumption of temporal consistency might be a limiting factor. It won't effectively address scenarios with significant temporal discontinuities (e.g., scene changes).
    *   **Limited Discussion of Commercial Model Architectures:** While testing commercial models is valuable, the paper doesn't provide architectural details or insights into how these models influence the results. This lack of transparency makes it challenging to fully understand the performance differences observed on commercial platforms.
    *   **Over-Reliance on Naive Voting:** The voting scheme for video watermark extraction could be improved with more sophisticated methods. The current majority voting scheme may be sensitive to individual frame extraction errors.
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation Metrics:** The evaluation concentrates on standard and specialized Watermark metrics (PSNR/SSIM/LPIPS/ACC and derived ones). Including metrics pertaining to Video Quality, such as FID (Fréchet Inception Distance) or similar techniques would strengthen the practical implications of the watermark addition as a result of quality preservation.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research on cross-modal watermarking techniques for I2V and related generative models.  The proposed framework provides a strong baseline for future work in this area. It also introduces valuable tools (RDD) to analyse further improvements and evaluate performances on new methods.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7.5. The paper makes significant contributions by identifying and addressing the specific challenges of watermarking image-guided video generation. The I2VWM framework, combined with the RDD metric, presents a novel and technically sound approach to the problem. The evaluation is comprehensive.
While the contributions are significant, there is also room for improvement to broaden the scope by accounting for temporal inconsistencies and more sophisticated voting mechanisms for the final watermark extraction from video and to better analyse Video Generation quality impact. The assumptions are clearly laid out however, increasing the transparency and reliability.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Revealing Multimodal Causality with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17784v1)
- **Authors**: Jin Li, Shoujin Wang, Qi Zhang, Feng Liu, Tongliang Liu, Longbing Cao, Shui Yu, Fang Chen
- **Abstract**: Uncovering cause-and-effect mechanisms from data is fundamental to scientific progress. While large language models (LLMs) show promise for enhancing causal discovery (CD) from unstructured data, their application to the increasingly prevalent multimodal setting remains a critical challenge. Even with the advent of multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), their efficacy in multimodal CD is hindered by two primary limitations: (1) difficulty in exploring intra- and inter-modal interactions for comprehensive causal variable identification; and (2) insufficiency to handle structural ambiguities with purely observational data. To address these challenges, we propose MLLM-CD, a novel framework for multimodal causal discovery from unstructured data. It consists of three key components: (1) a novel contrastive factor discovery module to identify genuine multimodal factors based on the interactions explored from contrastive sample pairs; (2) a statistical causal structure discovery module to infer causal relationships among discovered factors; and (3) an iterative multimodal counterfactual reasoning module to refine the discovery outcomes iteratively by incorporating the world knowledge and reasoning capabilities of MLLMs. Extensive experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of MLLM-CD in revealing genuine factors and causal relationships among them from multimodal unstructured data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Revealing Multimodal Causality with Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of causal discovery (CD) from multimodal unstructured data, a prevalent scenario but one where traditional CD methods struggle.  It introduces MLLM-CD, a novel framework that integrates multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) into the CD pipeline. MLLM-CD comprises three key modules: (1) a contrastive factor discovery (CFD) module, which identifies potential causal variables (factors) by exploring intra- and inter-modal interactions; (2) a statistical causal structure discovery module to infer causal relationships among these factors; and (3) an iterative multimodal counterfactual reasoning (MCR) module, leveraging MLLM's world knowledge to refine the discovered causal structure. The framework is evaluated on synthetic and real-world datasets, demonstrating improved performance in both factor identification and structure discovery compared to baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in the integrated approach of using MLLMs for causal discovery from multimodal *unstructured* data. While LLMs have been applied to CD before, and MLLMs exist, the combination of MLLMs with contrastive learning for factor discovery *and* iterative refinement via counterfactual reasoning, directly applicable to *unstructured* multimodal input, is a significant contribution. It moves beyond simply using LLMs to extract predefined features, instead allowing the model to *discover* the relevant factors itself. This is a key step in making CD applicable to a wider range of real-world scenarios.
*   **Significance:**  The significance is high.  Real-world data is increasingly multimodal and unstructured.  Traditional CD methods are often limited by their reliance on predefined, structured data. MLLM-CD offers a promising approach for tackling this problem, potentially opening up new avenues for causal inference in fields like medicine, finance, and social science. By leveraging the reasoning capabilities of MLLMs, the framework has the potential to move towards automated causal discovery and knowledge generation.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly identifies a critical gap in the field of causal discovery.
    *   **Novel and Integrated Framework:** MLLM-CD combines existing techniques in a novel way, creating a powerful framework. The contrastive learning is particularly effective at revealing implicit factors, and the counterfactual reasoning helps to reduce ambiguities in the causal structure.
    *   **Strong Empirical Evaluation:** The paper includes thorough experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets, with comprehensive comparisons to strong baselines. The ablation studies further validate the contribution of each component.
    *   **Detailed analysis** Provides a meticulous parameter sensitivity evaluation.
    *   **Clear Explanations:**  The paper provides clear explanations of the methods and results.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on MLLM Quality:** The performance of MLLM-CD is directly tied to the quality of the underlying MLLM.  Hallucinations or biases in the MLLM could negatively impact the results. While the counterfactual plausibility checks are used, there’s no guarantee those eliminate all false information that is passed down.
    *   **Complexity and Computational Cost:** The framework involves multiple steps and queries to MLLMs which could be computationally expensive, especially for large datasets. Although the time complexity is analyzed, practical concerns remain, particularly for real-time applications.
    *   **Limited Dataset Scale:** Although the authors create and use real-world datasets, small dataset sizes and dependency on the domain knowledge limit their performance when compared to similar studies.
    *   **Parameter sensitivity** In complex and real-world conditions, ensuring parameter consistency is an issue for robust experiments.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has a high potential to influence future research in causal discovery, multimodal learning, and the application of LLMs to scientific discovery. It provides a solid foundation for future research in those areas by expanding into larger datasets, refining the use of prompt engineering and the MLLM.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a strong, significant contribution to the field of causal discovery by providing a novel and effective approach for handling multimodal unstructured data. Its novel integration of MLLMs, contrastive learning, and counterfactual reasoning addresses a key challenge and opens up new avenues for research. The rigorous experimental evaluation supports the claims and highlights the strengths of the proposed framework. While the reliance on MLLM quality and the computational complexity represent limitations, the significance and novelty of the work warrant a high score. The limitations are also addressed in the current work.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### One Agent to Serve All: a Lite-Adaptive Stylized AI Assistant for Millions of Multi-Style Official Accounts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17788v1)
- **Authors**: Xingyu Fan, Feifei Li, Wenhui Que, Hailong Li
- **Abstract**: Conversational agents deployed in industrial-scale official account platforms must generate responses that are both contextually grounded and stylistically aligned-requirements that existing methods struggle to meet. Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting induces significant latency due to multi-turn reasoning; per-account fine-tuning is computationally prohibitive; and long prompt-based methods degrade the model's ability to grasp injected context and style. In this paper, we propose WeStar, a lite-adaptive framework for stylized contextual question answering that scales to millions of official accounts. WeStar combines context-grounded generation via RAG with style-aware generation using Parametric RAG (PRAG), where LoRA modules are dynamically activated per style cluster. Our contributions are fourfold: (1) We introduce WeStar, a unified framework capable of serving large volumes of official accounts with minimal overhead. (2) We propose a multi-dimensional, cluster-based parameter sharing scheme that enables compact style representation while preserving stylistic diversity. (3) We develop a style-enhanced Direct Preference Optimization (SeDPO) method to optimize each style cluster's parameters for improved generation quality. (4) Experiments on a large-scale industrial dataset validate the effectiveness and efficiency of WeStar, underscoring its pracitical value in real-world deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WeStar, a novel framework designed for stylized contextual question answering (CQSA) at scale, specifically targeting industrial official account platforms.  The framework addresses the challenge of generating responses that are both contextually grounded in the author's articles and stylistically aligned with the author's communication style. WeStar leverages a dual-injection approach: question-specific knowledge (retrieved articles) is injected via prompting, while style-specific knowledge is injected directly into the model's parameters using LoRA and PRAG.  The system pre-processes data by clustering authors based on stylistic similarities using a hierarchical style tree. Each style cluster has its own set of LoRA parameters trained with a style-enhanced Direct Preference Optimization (SeDPO) loss. Experiments on a large-scale industrial dataset demonstrate that WeStar outperforms various baselines in terms of contextual alignment, question relevance, stylistic strength, and fluency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the integrated framework that combines several existing techniques in a specific way to address a practical problem. While the individual components (RAG, LoRA, PRAG, DPO) are not entirely new, their combination, along with the proposed style tree clustering and the Style-enhanced DPO, is a significant contribution. The multi-dimensional style labeling and clustering approach aims to capture more nuanced stylistic differences, which is also novel. Furthermore, the scale of the problem and the real-world evaluation are also considered novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a real-world problem faced by large-scale official account platforms. The ability to generate responses that are both contextually relevant and stylistically consistent is highly desirable. The proposed WeStar framework offers a scalable and efficient solution, potentially improving user engagement and author representation. The experimental results on a large-scale industrial dataset demonstrate the practical value of the approach. One strength lies in offering a method that avoids full fine-tuning per user/account and thus significantly improves scalability. This is significant for applications involving many users. The use of LoRA for style adaptation is appropriate.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper tackles a practical and relevant problem with a well-defined context.
    *   The proposed framework integrates several existing techniques into a coherent and scalable solution.
    *   The style tree clustering and Style-enhanced DPO are novel contributions.
    *   The experiments are conducted on a large-scale industrial dataset, providing strong evidence of the framework's effectiveness.
    *   The detailed ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper relies on LLMs, which have inherent limitations regarding biases, safety, and factual accuracy. The paper should acknowledge these limitations and discuss potential mitigation strategies, although they are not a specific focus of the current work.
    *   Some aspects of the evaluation could be strengthened. While the automated metrics are valuable, including human evaluation would further validate the results, especially considering the subjective nature of style.
    *   The paper might benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the style clusters. How diverse are the clusters? What stylistic characteristics are most important for differentiating the clusters?
    *   More discussion of the computational cost during the Style Tree building and Style-enhanced DPO steps would make the paper more rigorous.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of conversational agents for large-scale platforms. The framework offers a practical approach to personalized and stylized interactions, potentially improving user satisfaction and engagement.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a well-engineered framework that addresses a practical problem with a solid combination of existing and novel techniques. The experiments are conducted on a realistic scale, and the results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach. While there are some limitations regarding human evaluation and a deeper stylistic analysis, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field of conversational AI and has the potential to be highly influential in industry. The integration of LoRA and PRAG for scalable style adaptation is compelling. The style tree clustering adds a useful layer for managing the style variations. The Style-enhanced DPO fine-tuning improves style alignment, and the choice of evaluation metrics is also appropriate, all while addressing the limitations of individual finetuning.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Elucidating the Design Space of FP4 training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17791v1)
- **Authors**: Robert Hu, Carlo Luschi, Paul Balanca
- **Abstract**: The increasing computational demands of foundation models have spurred research into low-precision training, with 4-bit floating-point (\texttt{FP4}) formats emerging as a frontier for maximizing hardware throughput. While numerous techniques have been proposed to stabilize \texttt{FP4} training, they often present isolated solutions with varying, and not always clear, computational overheads. This paper aims to provide a unified view of the design space of \texttt{FP4} training. We introduce a comprehensive, quantisation gradient-based framework for microscaling quantization that allows for a theoretical analysis of the computational costs associated with different stabilization methods on both the forward and backward passes. Using a simulator built on this framework, we conduct an extensive empirical study across a wide range of machine learning tasks, including regression, image classification, diffusion models, and language models. By systematically evaluating thousands of combinations of techniques, such as novel gradient approximations, rounding strategies, and scaling methods, we identify which configurations offer the most favourable performance-to-overhead trade-off. We find that the techniques enabling the best trade-off involve carefully combining Hadamard transformations, tensor scaling and stochastic rounding. We further find that using \texttt{UE5M3} as a scaling factor potentially offers a good compromise between range and precision with manageable computational overhead.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper provides a comprehensive analysis of the design space for training machine learning models using FP4 (4-bit floating-point) quantization.  It introduces a unified framework for analyzing the computational costs associated with various stabilization techniques used in FP4 training, covering forward and backward passes. Through extensive empirical evaluation across various machine learning tasks (regression, image classification, diffusion models, and language models), the paper identifies combinations of techniques that offer the best performance-to-overhead trade-off. Key findings highlight the importance of combining Hadamard transformations, tensor scaling, and stochastic rounding, and explore the UE5M3 scaling factor as a potentially good compromise between range and precision.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper provides a systematic exploration of the FP4 training design space, which is a significant contribution as many prior works focused on isolated solutions.  The unified framework is a valuable tool for understanding the interactions between different techniques.
    *   **Empirical Breadth:** The evaluation spans a wide range of tasks and models, lending credibility to the findings. The scope of the experiments, involving thousands of parameter combinations, is impressive.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The paper identifies specific configurations that offer favorable trade-offs, providing actionable guidance for practitioners working with FP4 training. The practical discussion of the optimal configuration parameters for UE5M3 is beneficial.
    *   **Novelty in UE5M3 Evaluation:** The evaluation of the UE5M3 scaling technique is interesting and valuable. It explores how range and precision can be managed and what considerations are needed for successful FP4 training with this format.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Real-World Scale Demonstrations:** Although the study is extensive, the paper's results are primarily simulation-based and could benefit from demonstrations on more substantial, real-world models (e.g. beyond 1B). The simulation environment isn't fully outlined, which limits the impact of the empirical evaluation.
    *   **Hardware-Specific Optimizations not fully addressed**:  Although the paper discusses fusability it does not outline specific operations on particular hardware architectures. Therefore its utility for hardware implementation is unclear.
    *   **Lack of clear guidance for all tasks/models:** Although the authors test across a range of tasks, the results are not totally consistent (ie. recommendations change for diffusion vs. language modelling) making it difficult to draw broad conclusions.
    *   **Missing Justification of Experimental Design:**  The experimental design and parameter sweeps are not fully justified. While Table 10 outlines parameter sweep values, the rationale behind these choices and their impact on the observed results requires further elaboration.
    *   **No code released:** With no code released the usefulness of this work to the community is diminished.
    *   **Significance and Novelty Trade-Off:** While the paper consolidates existing techniques and thoroughly examines their interactions, the *novelty* of individual techniques is somewhat limited. The paper doesn't introduce groundbreaking new methods but rather provides a comprehensive analysis of existing ones. While significant, this comprehensiveness does come at a cost, reducing the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in the field: efficiently training large models. Low-precision training, particularly FP4, is essential for maximizing hardware throughput. The paper's systematic analysis contributes to a better understanding of FP4 training dynamics and provides valuable guidance for practitioners. This work contributes to the wider aim of democratising access to large model training by reducing computational costs.
*   **Impact:**  The paper's findings can influence the design and implementation of FP4 training strategies, potentially accelerating the development and deployment of large models. The work can spur further research into hardware optimizations and the development of more robust and efficient low-precision training techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper.

*   It provides a *significant* contribution by offering a systematic evaluation of FP4 training techniques and illuminating the design space. The unified framework is a valuable tool, and the empirical scope is commendable.
*   The identified configurations and insights provide *actionable guidance* for practitioners.
*   *However*, the primarily simulation-based evaluation and lack of larger-scale validation slightly limit the impact. A few important details for hardware implementation seem to be missing which makes the work more suited for theoretical understanding than as a guide. And although the authors perform a wide range of experiments, recommendations are not fully consistent from task to task.
*   The *limited novelty* in terms of introducing entirely new techniques also factors into the score. The missing release of code or a fully explained hardware environment limits the utility to the community.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing Semantic Segmentation with Continual Self-Supervised Pre-training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17816v1)
- **Authors**: Brown Ebouky, Ajad Chhatkuli, Cristiano Malossi, Christoph Studer, Roy Assaf, Andrea Bartezzaghi
- **Abstract**: Self-supervised learning (SSL) has emerged as a central paradigm for training foundation models by leveraging large-scale unlabeled datasets, often producing representations with strong generalization capabilities. These models are typically pre-trained on general-purpose datasets such as ImageNet and subsequently adapted to various downstream tasks through finetuning. While recent advances have explored parameter-efficient strategies for adapting pre-trained models, extending SSL pre-training itself to new domains - particularly under limited data regimes and for dense prediction tasks - remains underexplored. In this work, we address the problem of adapting vision foundation models to new domains in an unsupervised and data-efficient manner, specifically targeting downstream semantic segmentation. We propose GLARE (Global Local and Regional Enforcement), a novel continual self-supervised pre-training task designed to enhance downstream segmentation performance. GLARE introduces patch-level augmentations to encourage local consistency and incorporates a regional consistency constraint that leverages spatial semantics in the data. For efficient continual pre-training, we initialize Vision Transformers (ViTs) with weights from existing SSL models and update only lightweight adapter modules - specifically UniAdapter - while keeping the rest of the backbone frozen. Experiments across multiple semantic segmentation benchmarks on different domains demonstrate that GLARE consistently improves downstream performance with minimal computational and parameter overhead.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GLARE (Global Local and Regional Enforcement), a novel continual self-supervised learning (SSL) pre-training task designed to enhance semantic segmentation performance, especially under limited data conditions. GLARE adapts vision foundation models to new domains without extensive labeled data. It focuses on three levels of consistency: (1) global image-level consistency, (2) regional consistency enforced using cross-attention over attention-guided regions, and (3) local consistency through patch-wise augmentations like blurring. The method leverages adapter modules within Vision Transformers (ViTs) initialized with existing SSL weights, freezing the backbone and only updating the adapters for parameter efficiency. Experiments on several semantic segmentation benchmarks (ADE20k, Pascal Context, Cityscapes, and LoveDA) demonstrate consistent performance improvements over baseline SSL methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies primarily in the combination of multiple consistency constraints tailored for dense prediction tasks within a continual SSL framework. While each individual component (global consistency, region sampling, local consistency) has been explored in prior works, the specific combination and the attention-guided regional consistency, coupled with adapter-based efficient fine-tuning, presents a novel approach. This targeted combination differentiates the method from more general continual learning or SSL strategies.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing the practical problem of adapting pre-trained vision models to new, data-scarce domains for semantic segmentation.  The adapter-based fine-tuning is practically useful in reducing computational costs and parameter overhead. The consistent improvements across diverse datasets highlight the robustness of the proposed method. The detailed ablations offer insights into the importance of each component and their interplay. This is a practical and incremental improvement that improves on current methods in the literature.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined architecture and training procedure.
    *   Comprehensive experiments on multiple datasets.
    *   Thorough ablation studies that justify the design choices.
    *   Parameter efficient due to use of adapters.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The individual components of GLARE, while effectively combined, are not entirely novel. The core novelty is in the integration strategy.
    *   The results, while consistent, show incremental improvements rather than a breakthrough. This suggests a well-engineered solution but perhaps not a fundamentally new paradigm.
    *   The method is heavily reliant on the initial pre-trained model (UDI). The impact of a weaker pre-trained model on the performance of GLARE needs further exploration.
    *   Limited exploration of other adapter architectures besides UniAdapter. This could further improve performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's practical approach and solid experimental results are likely to influence researchers working on domain adaptation, continual learning, and semantic segmentation. The emphasis on data efficiency will resonate with practitioners dealing with real-world data constraints.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

The paper offers a solid contribution by combining existing SSL concepts into a tailored approach for data-scarce semantic segmentation scenarios. The attention-guided regional consistency and adapter-based fine-tuning demonstrate a practical and effective strategy. The thorough experiments and ablations strengthen the claims. However, the incremental nature of the improvements, the reliance on a strong initial pre-trained model, and the limited exploration of different adapter architectures prevent a higher score. It's a well-executed piece of engineering that solves a practically relevant problem, but the core innovation is limited. While it advances the state-of-the-art, it is an incremental improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### ContextFlow: Training-Free Video Object Editing via Adaptive Context Enrichment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17818v1)
- **Authors**: Yiyang Chen, Xuanhua He, Xiujun Ma, Yue Ma
- **Abstract**: Training-free video object editing aims to achieve precise object-level manipulation, including object insertion, swapping, and deletion. However, it faces significant challenges in maintaining fidelity and temporal consistency. Existing methods, often designed for U-Net architectures, suffer from two primary limitations: inaccurate inversion due to first-order solvers, and contextual conflicts caused by crude "hard" feature replacement. These issues are more challenging in Diffusion Transformers (DiTs), where the unsuitability of prior layer-selection heuristics makes effective guidance challenging. To address these limitations, we introduce ContextFlow, a novel training-free framework for DiT-based video object editing. In detail, we first employ a high-order Rectified Flow solver to establish a robust editing foundation. The core of our framework is Adaptive Context Enrichment (for specifying what to edit), a mechanism that addresses contextual conflicts. Instead of replacing features, it enriches the self-attention context by concatenating Key-Value pairs from parallel reconstruction and editing paths, empowering the model to dynamically fuse information. Additionally, to determine where to apply this enrichment (for specifying where to edit), we propose a systematic, data-driven analysis to identify task-specific vital layers. Based on a novel Guidance Responsiveness Metric, our method pinpoints the most influential DiT blocks for different tasks (e.g., insertion, swapping), enabling targeted and highly effective guidance. Extensive experiments show that ContextFlow significantly outperforms existing training-free methods and even surpasses several state-of-the-art training-based approaches, delivering temporally coherent, high-fidelity results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ContextFlow paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ContextFlow: Training-Free Video Object Editing via Adaptive Context Enrichment" presents a novel approach to video object editing that doesn't require training. It tackles the challenges of maintaining fidelity and temporal consistency in edited videos, particularly when using Diffusion Transformers (DiTs). The method consists of three main components: 1) high-fidelity inversion using a Rectified Flow (RF) solver, 2) Adaptive Context Enrichment (ACE) which dynamically fuses information from the original and edited video paths, and 3) a data-driven Vital Layer Analysis to pinpoint the most effective layers for injecting the contextual information. The method demonstrates strong performance on tasks like object insertion, swapping, and deletion, outperforming existing training-free and even some training-based approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel components. The use of Rectified Flow for inversion in the video editing context is a significant departure from the commonly used DDIM inversion, addressing the fidelity issue. The Adaptive Context Enrichment mechanism is also novel, offering a more nuanced approach than simply replacing features.  The data-driven Vital Layer Analysis tackles the challenge of effectively guiding DiTs by identifying the crucial layers for modification. While the individual components draw from existing ideas, their combination and application to DiT-based video editing is genuinely new.

* **Significance:** Video object editing is a crucial task in computer vision. Training-free methods are especially valuable as they provide flexibility and reduce computational burden. ContextFlow addresses a significant gap in the field by offering a high-quality, training-free solution tailored for the DiT architecture.  The improved fidelity and consistency demonstrated by the method are vital for practical applications. The paper shows impressive performance, often surpassing existing training-based methods.  This advancement could make video editing more accessible and efficient.

* **Strengths:**
    * **High Fidelity and Consistency:** A clear strength of ContextFlow is its ability to produce high-fidelity and temporally consistent edits, overcoming limitations of previous training-free methods.
    * **Adaptive and Nuanced Guidance:** The Adaptive Context Enrichment is a significant step forward, allowing the model to dynamically balance content preservation and synthesis.
    * **Data-Driven Layer Analysis:** Replacing ad-hoc layer selection with a data-driven approach for DiTs is a smart move, improving efficiency and effectiveness.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments clearly demonstrate the superiority of ContextFlow over existing methods, with comprehensive quantitative and qualitative evaluations.
    * **Well-written and clear:** The paper is well-structured and explains the technical details thoroughly. The visualizations and ablations are very helpful.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Cost:** Training-free usually means "training" is shifted to a more computationally intensive inference. The VRAM requirements (120GB) and processing time (25 minutes) for a short video are very high and will limit broader adoption. This is a major practicality hurdle.
    * **Reliance on First-Frame Edit:** As noted by the authors, the method's performance is contingent on the quality of the initial frame edit. This dependency limits the method's autonomy and adds an extra step to the workflow.
    * **Motion and Occlusion:** The paper acknowledges limitations with extreme motion and occlusion, which remain significant challenges for many video editing techniques.
    * **Limited Exploration of DiT Architecture:** While the Vital Layer Analysis is valuable, there could be a deeper exploration of the specific characteristics of the DiT architecture that make ContextFlow so effective, potentially leading to further optimizations.

* **Potential Influence:**  ContextFlow has the potential to influence the field by providing a strong foundation for future research in training-free video editing.  The ideas of adaptive context enrichment and data-driven layer analysis could be adopted and extended by other researchers. The results highlight the importance of high-fidelity inversion and carefully designed guidance mechanisms for DiT-based models.

**Justification for Score:**

While ContextFlow demonstrates significant improvements in video object editing, the high computational cost represents a substantial barrier to practical adoption. Its dependency on first-frame editing is a limitation. The work has a positive impact on the field due to its novelty and the demonstrated improved results.  The paper's methods are well thought-out and effectively address real-world video editing limitations, which justifies a good score.

### Fine-Grained Detection of AI-Generated Text Using Sentence-Level Segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17830v1)
- **Authors**: Lekkala Sai Teja, Annepaka Yadagiri, and Partha Pakray, Chukhu Chunka, Mangadoddi Srikar Vardhan
- **Abstract**: Generation of Artificial Intelligence (AI) texts in important works has become a common practice that can be used to misuse and abuse AI at various levels. Traditional AI detectors often rely on document-level classification, which struggles to identify AI content in hybrid or slightly edited texts designed to avoid detection, leading to concerns about the model's efficiency, which makes it hard to distinguish between human-written and AI-generated texts. A sentence-level sequence labeling model proposed to detect transitions between human- and AI-generated text, leveraging nuanced linguistic signals overlooked by document-level classifiers. By this method, detecting and segmenting AI and human-written text within a single document at the token-level granularity is achieved. Our model combines the state-of-the-art pre-trained Transformer models, incorporating Neural Networks (NN) and Conditional Random Fields (CRFs). This approach extends the power of transformers to extract semantic and syntactic patterns, and the neural network component to capture enhanced sequence-level representations, thereby improving the boundary predictions by the CRF layer, which enhances sequence recognition and further identification of the partition between Human- and AI-generated texts. The evaluation is performed on two publicly available benchmark datasets containing collaborative human and AI-generated texts. Our experimental comparisons are with zero-shot detectors and the existing state-of-the-art models, along with rigorous ablation studies to justify that this approach, in particular, can accurately detect the spans of AI texts in a completely collaborative text. All our source code and the processed datasets are available in our GitHub repository.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of detecting AI-generated text, focusing on fine-grained (sentence-level) segmentation to identify transitions between human-written and AI-generated content within a single document.  The authors propose a model combining a pre-trained Transformer encoder, a Neural Network layer, and a Conditional Random Field (CRF) layer. The NN-CRF component is designed to capture sequence-level dependencies and improve boundary predictions. They evaluate their model on two publicly available datasets (TriBERT and M4GT) and compare its performance against zero-shot detectors and existing state-of-the-art models. The paper includes ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of different components and optimization techniques.  The authors claim their approach outperforms existing methods in accurately detecting spans of AI text in collaborative texts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The combination of Transformer + NN + CRF is not entirely novel in the general NLP landscape. However, the paper claims novelty in several aspects tailored *specifically* for sentence-level AI-generated text detection in collaborative text. These targeted optimizations include: Layer-wise Learning Rate Decay, Dynamic Dropout, CRF Loss Calculation with Masking, Multiple Boundary Prediction Flexibility, and Xavier Initialization for Weights Stability. The hierarchical integration of authorial cues and token boundary-aware loss are also presented as novel contributions. The novelty resides in adapting and optimizing this architecture *for this specific task*.

*   **Significance:** Detecting AI-generated text, especially at a fine-grained level, is becoming increasingly important due to the potential for misuse and abuse. The ability to identify and segment AI-generated content within hybrid texts has significant implications for academic integrity, content moderation, and preventing the spread of misinformation. If the model consistently outperforms existing methods, it could have a real impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The focus on sentence-level segmentation is a significant step beyond document-level classification, addressing a crucial limitation of existing methods.
    *   The model architecture seems well-reasoned and incorporates components designed to capture relevant linguistic features and dependencies.
    *   The paper provides detailed ablation studies, which helps to understand the contribution of each component and optimization technique.
    *   The evaluation is performed on two benchmark datasets, allowing for a fair comparison with existing methods.
    *   The paper acknowledges the limitations of its model, particularly its vulnerability to adversarial attacks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the components are described, the *specific* details of how these fine-tuning and loss functions *uniquely* contribute to this particular task need further elaboration in the paper.
    *   Although the results seem promising, the performance gains over existing methods on certain datasets do not appear to be overwhelmingly significant (e.g., on the M4GT dataset).
    *   The high accuracy scores reported using traditional metrics (Accuracy, Precision, Recall, F1-Score, MCC, and Kappa) may be misleading, as acknowledged by the authors, because they focus primarily on token-level predictions, overlooking the structural coherence of boundaries. It's not clear what practical significance these metrics provide given the task aims to segment AI-generated text at the token level accurately.
    *   Lack of comprehensive adversarial testing:  The paper acknowledges vulnerability to adversarial attacks, but does not provide a rigorous analysis of how the model performs under such scenarios.
    *   The code availability is a plus but does not replace a more in-depth analysis of model parameters and computational costs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in AI-generated text detection, particularly in the area of fine-grained segmentation and collaborative text analysis. The model architecture and optimization techniques proposed in the paper could serve as a starting point for other researchers. The paper also highlights the importance of developing robust methods that are resistant to adversarial attacks.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper tackles an important and timely problem with a solid, well-justified approach. It demonstrates the feasibility of sentence-level AI-generated text detection. However, the results' *degree* of impact is somewhat tempered by the somewhat incremental performance gains in certain scenarios, a possible lack of substantial adversarial testing and the lack of novel methodology and contributions from similar papers. While the adaptations and the performance numbers are strong, there is still substantial room for refinement in the methodology.

### Semantic and Visual Crop-Guided Diffusion Models for Heterogeneous Tissue Synthesis in Histopathology
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17847v1)
- **Authors**: Saghir Alfasly, Wataru Uegami, MD Enamul Hoq, Ghazal Alabtah, H. R. Tizhoosh
- **Abstract**: Synthetic data generation in histopathology faces unique challenges: preserving tissue heterogeneity, capturing subtle morphological features, and scaling to unannotated datasets. We present a latent diffusion model that generates realistic heterogeneous histopathology images through a novel dual-conditioning approach combining semantic segmentation maps with tissue-specific visual crops. Unlike existing methods that rely on text prompts or abstract visual embeddings, our approach preserves critical morphological details by directly incorporating raw tissue crops from corresponding semantic regions. For annotated datasets (i.e., Camelyon16, Panda), we extract patches ensuring 20-80% tissue heterogeneity. For unannotated data (i.e., TCGA), we introduce a self-supervised extension that clusters whole-slide images into 100 tissue types using foundation model embeddings, automatically generating pseudo-semantic maps for training. Our method synthesizes high-fidelity images with precise region-wise annotations, achieving superior performance on downstream segmentation tasks. When evaluated on annotated datasets, models trained on our synthetic data show competitive performance to those trained on real data, demonstrating the utility of controlled heterogeneous tissue generation. In quantitative evaluation, prompt-guided synthesis reduces Frechet Distance by up to 6X on Camelyon16 (from 430.1 to 72.0) and yields 2-3x lower FD across Panda and TCGA. Downstream DeepLabv3+ models trained solely on synthetic data attain test IoU of 0.71 and 0.95 on Camelyon16 and Panda, within 1-2% of real-data baselines (0.72 and 0.96). By scaling to 11,765 TCGA whole-slide images without manual annotations, our framework offers a practical solution for an urgent need for generating diverse, annotated histopathology data, addressing a critical bottleneck in computational pathology.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel latent diffusion model called HeteroTissue-Diffuse (HTD) for generating realistic and heterogeneous histopathology images.  HTD uses a dual-conditioning approach that combines semantic segmentation maps with tissue-specific visual crops to guide the image generation process.  This allows for fine-grained control over region-specific tissue synthesis, preserving crucial morphological details often lost in text-based or embedding-based methods. A self-supervised extension enables the use of unannotated whole-slide images (WSIs) from the TCGA dataset by clustering them into tissue types and generating pseudo-semantic maps. Experimental results on Camelyon16, PANDA, and TCGA datasets demonstrate superior performance on downstream segmentation tasks and reduced Fréchet Distance compared to other methods.  A blinded evaluation by certified pathologists further confirms the clinical realism of the generated images.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the dual-conditioning architecture that combines semantic maps with visual crops. While semantic map conditioning and region-guided generation exist, the direct incorporation of raw tissue crops to preserve morphological details is a significant advancement. The self-supervised extension for unannotated data is also valuable, although deep clustering is a well-established technique, the application in histopathology WSI is novel.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a key bottleneck in computational pathology: the lack of diverse, annotated histopathology data. By generating realistic and heterogeneous synthetic data, HTD offers a practical solution for training and evaluating diagnostic algorithms, especially for rare cancer types or underrepresented populations. The demonstrated ability to train segmentation models on entirely synthetic data with performance comparable to real data is a major contribution. Overcoming the reliance on real patient data has implications for privacy and data accessibility. The scalability to large, unannotated datasets like TCGA is also critical.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The dual-conditioning approach is well-motivated and demonstrably effective.
    *   The self-supervised extension broadens the applicability to unannotated datasets.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation with both quantitative metrics and expert pathologist assessment.
    *   Clear presentation and thorough ablation studies in supplementary materials.
    *   The results demonstrate a substantial improvement over existing methods, particularly in preserving crucial morphological details.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on high-performance computing for TCGA feature extraction could limit accessibility.
    *   The predefined clustering in the self-supervised step might miss very rare pathological patterns.
    *   The current implementation focuses on H&E stained images, restricting its immediate applicability to other staining protocols.
    *   While the results are impressive, the classifier, while effective, achieves 47% testing accuracy. There's room to improve here, as this could result in misclassification and erroneous training on a lower-quality dataset.
    *   The improvement demonstrated is not always consistent. In some cases, SM + Crops and SM Only achieve similar, or very close results. While there is justification and evidence for the improvement, this is another aspect that can be improved.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by:
    *   Democratizing access to annotated histopathology data.
    *   Accelerating the development of AI diagnostics for various cancer types.
    *   Enabling privacy-preserving research collaborations.
    *   Motivating further research into visual conditioning techniques in generative models for medical imaging.

*   **Justification of the Score:** While the paper has some limitations in terms of computational resources and cluster algorithm performance, its strengths in addressing a critical data bottleneck, demonstrating a novel and effective approach, and validating results with expert assessment outweigh its weaknesses. The demonstrated improvement over existing methods and the potential influence on the field warrant a high score.

### SocialTraj: Two-Stage Socially-Aware Trajectory Prediction for Autonomous Driving via Conditional Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17850v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Zhou, Zengqi Peng, Jun Ma
- **Abstract**: Accurate trajectory prediction of surrounding vehicles (SVs) is crucial for autonomous driving systems to avoid misguided decisions and potential accidents. However, achieving reliable predictions in highly dynamic and complex traffic scenarios remains a significant challenge. One of the key impediments lies in the limited effectiveness of current approaches to capture the multi-modal behaviors of drivers, which leads to predicted trajectories that deviate from actual future motions. To address this issue, we propose SocialTraj, a novel trajectory prediction framework integrating social psychology principles through social value orientation (SVO). By utilizing Bayesian inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) to estimate the SVO of SVs, we obtain the critical social context to infer the future interaction trend. To ensure modal consistency in predicted behaviors, the estimated SVOs of SVs are embedded into a conditional denoising diffusion model that aligns generated trajectories with historical driving styles. Additionally, the planned future trajectory of the ego vehicle (EV) is explicitly incorporated to enhance interaction modeling. Extensive experiments on NGSIM and HighD datasets demonstrate that SocialTraj is capable of adapting to highly dynamic and interactive scenarios while generating socially compliant and behaviorally consistent trajectory predictions, outperforming existing baselines. Ablation studies demonstrate that dynamic SVO estimation and explicit ego-planning components notably improve prediction accuracy and substantially reduce inference time.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SocialTraj, a novel two-stage framework for trajectory prediction of surrounding vehicles in autonomous driving scenarios.  It tackles the challenge of multi-modal driver behavior by integrating social psychology principles, specifically Social Value Orientation (SVO), through Bayesian inverse reinforcement learning (IRL). First, the framework estimates the SVO of surrounding vehicles. Then, it incorporates these estimated SVOs, along with the planned trajectory of the ego vehicle (EV), into a conditional denoising diffusion model (DDPM). This aims to generate socially compliant and behaviorally consistent trajectory predictions. The authors demonstrate the framework's effectiveness on the NGSIM and HighD datasets, showing improved accuracy and inference time compared to existing baselines.  Ablation studies further validate the importance of dynamic SVO estimation and explicit ego-planning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper demonstrates some degree of novelty in its approach, combining several existing techniques in a novel way. The integration of Social Value Orientation (SVO) into trajectory prediction is not entirely new, but the use of *attention-based Bayesian IRL for real-time SVO estimation* is a significant step forward compared to static or heuristically selected SVO values. Leveraging conditional diffusion models for trajectory generation is also being actively explored, but *conditioning it on dynamically estimated SVOs and ego-vehicle planning* offers a specific contribution to the field.  The two-stage approach is well-motivated, separating social inference from trajectory generation.

**Significance:**

Accurate trajectory prediction is critical for the safety and efficiency of autonomous vehicles, and the paper addresses a key challenge: capturing the multi-modal and interactive nature of human driving behavior. By modeling social context through SVO, the framework aims to generate more realistic and reliable predictions.  The performance improvements demonstrated on benchmark datasets suggest that the approach has practical value. Furthermore, the interpretable nature of SVO allows for a deeper understanding of the factors influencing driver behavior, potentially facilitating the development of more socially aware autonomous driving systems. The ablation studies are well designed and convincingly demonstrate the importance of each component.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The problem of multi-modal trajectory prediction is clearly articulated, and the proposed solution is logically sound.
*   **Novel Combination of Techniques:** Integrates Bayesian IRL, SVO, and conditional diffusion models in a non-trivial way.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** Demonstrates performance improvements over several strong baselines on standard datasets.
*   **Thorough Ablation Studies:**  Validates the contribution of each component of the framework.
*   **Clear and well-written:** The paper is easy to follow, with clear explanations of the methodology and results.
*   **Focus on Interpretability:** Emphasizes the importance of understanding driver behavior through the interpretable SVO metric.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on SVO Assumption:** The assumption that SVO remains constant during short interactions might not always hold true. Drivers can exhibit rapid behavioral shifts, especially in critical situations, not fully captured by a static SVO. The authors acknowledge that the Bayesian IRL approach tries to dynamically respond to changing intent, but its ability to track *very* fast changes is limited.
*   **Computational Complexity:** While the paper reports reduced inference time compared to some baselines, the computational cost of Bayesian IRL with HMC sampling and diffusion models might still be a concern for real-time applications, especially with a large number of surrounding vehicles. The authors claim reduced inference time, but without a direct comparison of runtimes, it is difficult to assess if the proposed technique is fast enough for online applications.
*   **Generalization to complex urban scenarios:** The evaluation is primarily focused on highway scenarios. The effectiveness of SocialTraj in more complex urban environments with pedestrians, cyclists, and more intricate interactions needs further investigation.
*   **Dependence on the Planned EV trajectory**: While incorporating the EV trajectory is beneficial, it also introduces an external element that can be noisy and impact trajectory prediction.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of trajectory prediction.  The novel combination of techniques, the solid experimental results, and the focus on interpretability make it a significant advancement. However, the limitations related to SVO assumption and computational complexity prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. The paper also needs further testing in more challenging environments to establish its full potential.

### Make Every Letter Count: Building Dialect Variation Dictionaries from Monolingual Corpora
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17855v1)
- **Authors**: Robert Litschko, Verena Blaschke, Diana Burkhardt, Barbara Plank, Diego Frassinelli
- **Abstract**: Dialects exhibit a substantial degree of variation due to the lack of a standard orthography. At the same time, the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to process dialects remains largely understudied. To address this gap, we use Bavarian as a case study and investigate the lexical dialect understanding capability of LLMs by examining how well they recognize and translate dialectal terms across different parts-of-speech. To this end, we introduce DiaLemma, a novel annotation framework for creating dialect variation dictionaries from monolingual data only, and use it to compile a ground truth dataset consisting of 100K human-annotated German-Bavarian word pairs. We evaluate how well nine state-of-the-art LLMs can judge Bavarian terms as dialect translations, inflected variants, or unrelated forms of a given German lemma. Our results show that LLMs perform best on nouns and lexically similar word pairs, and struggle most in distinguishing between direct translations and inflected variants. Interestingly, providing additional context in the form of example usages improves the translation performance, but reduces their ability to recognize dialect variants. This study highlights the limitations of LLMs in dealing with orthographic dialect variation and emphasizes the need for future work on adapting LLMs to dialects.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of dialectal variation in NLP, specifically focusing on Bavarian and its relationship to Standard German.  The authors introduce DIALEMMA, a novel annotation framework that uses monolingual corpora to create dialect variation dictionaries. This framework aims to overcome the limitation of requiring parallel corpora, which are often scarce for dialects.  They use DIALEMMA to build a dataset of 100k German-Bavarian word pairs, manually annotated to indicate direct translations, inflected forms, or unrelated words.  The authors then evaluate the ability of nine state-of-the-art Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform two tasks: judging whether a given word pair is a valid translation and translating Bavarian words into their German counterparts. The results reveal that while larger LLMs perform better, they still struggle, particularly in distinguishing between translations and inflected variants. Furthermore, the paper analyzes the impact of context, prompt language, and Levenshtein distance on LLM performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The DIALEMMA framework is a significant contribution. While prior work exists on dialect lexicon induction, the reliance on monolingual corpora makes this approach more scalable and applicable to low-resource settings where parallel data is limited.  The large, manually-annotated dataset is also a valuable resource for the community. The systematic evaluation of LLMs on these specific tasks, and the analysis of factors like context and Levenshtein distance, is novel and provides valuable insights.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a critical gap in current LLM capabilities: the ability to handle orthographic dialect variation. This is important because many languages and language varieties lack standardized orthographies, and current NLP methods are often trained on standard-language data.  The findings have implications for building more robust and inclusive NLP systems that can effectively process diverse language varieties.  The work also contributes to the broader understanding of LLM strengths and weaknesses.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The DIALEMMA framework is well-defined and replicable.
    *   The dataset is large and carefully annotated, providing a solid ground truth.
    *   The experimental design is rigorous, with multiple models and variations in prompts.
    *   The analysis is thorough, examining the impact of various factors on LLM performance.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly articulates the research questions, methods, and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The focus on Bavarian, while providing a detailed case study, limits the generalizability of the findings to other dialects and languages. However, the paper introduces a framework and the analysis of LLM behavior around dialects that *could* be applied to others.
    *   The evaluation is primarily word-level, lacking a broader evaluation on downstream tasks (e.g., machine translation, information retrieval) or sentence-level inference.
    *   The paper mostly highlights problems with LLMs without delving much into what solutions might look like.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in dialect NLP and LLM adaptation.  It emphasizes the need for developing methods that are more robust to spelling variation and for creating more inclusive training datasets.

*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper rigorously evaluates the hypothesis that current LLMs are not equipped to handle dialectal differences. They are thorough in the number of LLMs tested and the different prompts provided. They correctly provide baselines to compare their findings to.

The paper provides a crucial and timely contribution.
